Raw evidence: `w2-4/armC-run.txt`, `w2-4/armC-poll.txt` (0.2 s sampler across the whole chain), `w2-4/armC-ids.txt`. **No DB mutation anywhere in this arm** — every transition is the product's own, including the mutex release.
- **Observed:** The chain, all times on the DB clock, from `w2-4/armC-run.txt` and `w2-4/armC-poll.txt`:
  - `edge-mutex-hog` `ed048344-...` created `22:54:57.284107`, claimed by `agent1` at `22:54:57.402385`, holding `edge-mutex` from `22:54:57.324655`.
  - `edge-sideeffect` `bd557f17-...` (same mutex) created `22:55:06.437476` and stayed **`Pending`** — `tryQueueRun` rolls back on the `mutex_holders` unique violation (`internal/store/postgres.go:546-555`) and leaves the status untouched, exactly as the W2 plan's corrected W2-9 note says.
  - Both agents `kill-hard`ed at `22:57:14.319` (host clock), so the `agents` rows survived with frozen `last_seen_at` (`agent1 22:57:05.430145`, `agent2 22:57:05.65128`). At that moment the victim had been `Pending` for **125.9 s**.
  - The **stuck-run** reaper failed the hog at `22:58:59.806587` — `last_seen_at + 90 s = 22:58:35.430`, so **24.376 s past eligibility**, again inside the nominal 30 s interval — and `FinishRun`'s transaction released `edge-mutex` with it (`mutex_holders` 1 → 0 in the same sample).
  - **196.9 ms later** the scheduler transitioned the victim `Pending → Queued`: `updated_at = 22:59:00.003445`. Its `created_at` was already **233.566 s** old, i.e. **7.8× the 30 s grace**, so it was reapable the instant it became `Queued`.
  - The **queued-run** reaper failed it at `22:59:29.818383` (reason line at `22:59:29.815423`), releasing the mutex the victim had itself just acquired.
  - **`queued_window` = `22:59:00.003445` → `22:59:29.818383` = 29.815 s.** Total age at failure **263.381 s**, of which **233.566 s Pending** and **29.815 s Queued**.
- **Expected:** `docs/high-availability.md:352-356` says queued runs "are failed once they have **waited** longer than the queued-run reaper grace", and `queuedRunGraceDefault`'s own comment (`cmd/controller/main.go:98-101`) reads "how long a run may sit **Queued** with no eligible live agent before being failed". Both describe a Queued-time budget. `ListUnclaimableQueuedRuns` implements `r.created_at < NOW() - make_interval(secs => $1)` (`internal/store/postgres.go:1280`; `:1279` is the query's `WHERE r.status = 'Queued'` line, which is what W2-9 cites it for at `:1430`) and there is no `queued_at` column to implement the other reading with (`internal/store/migrations/001_init.up.sql:213-226` — only `created_at` and `updated_at`). The doc statement is a published contract; the `main.go` comment is an unexported function's own comment and is cited only as evidence of intent.
- **Notes:**
  - **Severity reasoning, stated explicitly because the plan's test does not discriminate here.** The plan's rule is "major if the run is failed before one full claim poll could have occurred, else observation with the measured window". The claim long-poll on this rig is 30 s (`ClaimDetached(..., "30s", ...)`, `internal/agent/agent.go:411-415`) and the measured Queued window was **29.815 s** — just inside it, so the rule points at *major* by 0.185 s. But the rule cannot actually discriminate in this arm: **no agent was alive to poll at all**, so "one full claim poll could have occurred" is false for a second, unrelated reason. Filed as an observation on that ground, not by rounding the window.
  - **What is transferable is the clock, and the number that shows it is 0, not 29.8.** The grace contributed **zero** seconds of protection after the Queued transition — the age gate was already satisfied by 203.6 s. The 29.815 s the run survived came entirely from where the transition landed on the sweep grid, which is uniform on [0, 30) s (see the Part B observation above). A victim whose mutex is released 1 s *before* a sweep gets ~1 s of Queued life, not 29.8 s. **The 29.815 s is one draw from that distribution, not a bound.**
  - **The reap instant is consistent with the same sweep grid, but this is derived, not captured.** Statement logging was already reverted when Part C ran (`w2-4/partB-stmtlog-off.txt`, `22:46:50`), so there is no direct sweep capture for it. Extrapolating Part B's grid with its own measured period — `22:26:59.016` → `22:31:59.148` is 10 ticks in 300.132 s, i.e. **30.013 s/tick**, so the grid drifts ~13 ms per tick rather than sitting still — lands the `22:59:29` cluster at ≈`22:59:29.8`, matching the observed `22:59:29.815` reason-line write and the hog's `22:58:59.806` stuck-run reap. **Consistent, not measured.**
  - **Where this stops being cosmetic is in combination with the major above.** A run that has been `Pending` a long time (mutex, semaphore, or a `git://` `uses:` awaiting resolution — `postgres.go:513-518` skips those the same way) becomes `Queued` the moment its blocker clears. If the blocker clears because a full agent outage is *ending*, that run is simultaneously (a) instantly past `minAge` with no grace left and (b) inside the stale-list window of the sweep that is running as the agents re-register. Both halves were observed in this scenario, in different arms.
  - **Not established:** the git-resolution variant of the same setup. `tryQueueRun` skips unresolved `git://` `uses:` steps before touching status, so such a run also sits `Pending` accumulating age, and this campaign has no git fixture (deferred with W1-7). Same mechanism, untested.

## W2-5 — a `call:` child whose one link report is lost becomes permanently unreachable: the parent's cancel cascade walked an empty child list and the orphan ran to `Succeeded` 38.840 s later, writing 37 further side effects after the parent was terminal
- **Invariant:** **I6** (zombie containment — *measure, don't judge*, per the design spec's own caveat at `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-07-29-edge-case-testing-design.md:53`, so the 38.840 s lifetime and 37-marker count below are the deliverable of this limb and not its verdict) and **I7** (state display consistency: `GET /api/v1/runs/a833b977-...` carries no `calledBy`, the parent's run detail carries no call step at all, and both contradict the reality that this child was created by that parent). **I1 is cited as the closest fit only** — the orphan did reach exactly one terminal state (`Succeeded`), so I1's literal text is *not* what is contradicted; what I1 is being stretched to cover is that the run reached that state with no accounting edge to the parent that spawned it. Same "closest fit only" shape as the precedent at `FINDINGS.md:489`. **The violation itself rests on a published documented contract — `docs/jobs.md:671-673` — not on an invariant.** **NOT I3:** campaign I3 is *no lock leaks* (design spec `:50`; this file uses it correctly at `FINDINGS.md:350`), and no mutex, semaphore or named-lock slot is involved in either workload here — an earlier draft of this entry relabelled I3 as "no orphaned side effects", which is not a campaign invariant at all.
- **Severity:** major
- **Repro:** `test/edgecase/scenarios/w2-5-unlinked-child.md`, Part B (Part A is the control; see the per-control injection-state bullet below — **control 1 is the uninjected one**). Raw evidence: `w2-5/baseline-gate.txt`, `w2-5/partB-inject.txt`, `w2-5/partB-rearm.txt`, `w2-5/partB-inject2.txt`, `w2-5/partB-b4-unlinked.txt`, `w2-5/partB-b4b-b7-cancel.txt`, `w2-5/partB-b8-childpoll.txt`, `w2-5/partB-b9-final.txt`, `w2-5/partB-b5-b9-logs.txt`, `w2-5/agent2-full.log`, `w2-5/nginx-full.log`, `w2-5/armA-link.txt`, `w2-5/armA-cascade.txt`, `w2-5/armA-childlog.txt`, `w2-5/armA2-control2.txt`, `w2-5/armA2-childlog-raw.txt`.
- **Observed:** `edge-call-parent` run `b556c457-1d78-487a-94ce-8d896260aad6` (claimed by `agent2`) created child run `a833b977-84df-49e6-b70e-6aeacbb7b19e` (`edge-call-child`, claimed by `agent1`) while `agent2`'s step-report endpoint was refused at nginx and its child-create endpoint was not. Timeline, DB clock except where noted:

  ```
  23:43:55.878014  parent created            (runs.created_at)
  23:43:56.471154  parent claimed by agent2
  23:43:56.479124  step_reports row: step 0 'prelude' Running
  23:44:08.510227  steplock armed for agent2 (probe confirmed 403 at 23:44:09.321, host clock)
  23:44:16.584001  agent2: ERROR "permanent error, giving up retry" status=403   <-- prelude terminal report
  23:44:16.589078  CHILD RUN CREATED  a833b977...  (POST .../children, not blocked)
  23:44:16.590933  agent2: INFO "call: child run created" childRunId=a833b977...  (agent clock)
  23:44:16          nginx: 3 x POST /api/v1/agents/agent2/steps -> 403           <-- the link report is one of these
  23:44:17.508838  child claimed by agent1; first /data/child.log marker 23:44:17
  23:44:49.777573  steplock cleared (nothing after the child's creation is injected)
  23:45:09.110168  PARENT CANCELLED via POST /api/v1/runs/{id}/cancel -> 204     (api_runs.go:382 cascade)
  23:45:11.483120  agent2: "received cancellation signal from master"  (+2.373 s)
  23:45:11          nginx: POST .../agents/agent2/steps -> 200 87                <-- the "self-heal" report
  23:45:11          nginx: POST .../runs/b556c457.../finish -> 200 94            <-- alreadyFinalized
  23:45:47.950560  CHILD SUCCEEDED   (orphan lifetime 38.840 s past the parent)
  ```

  **The unlinked state, measured with `ListChildRunIDs`' own query.** At `23:44:47` and again after the run finished, `SELECT child_run_id FROM step_reports WHERE run_id='b556c457-...' AND child_run_id IS NOT NULL` returned **0 rows** (`w2-5/partB-b4-unlinked.txt`, `w2-5/partB-b9-final.txt`). The parent's *entire* step history is one row — `step 0 'prelude' status='Running'` with a NULL `ended_at`. **There is no row for step 1 at all**, so not even the call step's name survives. The reverse query is equally empty: `SELECT count(*) FROM step_reports sr JOIN runs r ON r.id=sr.run_id WHERE sr.child_run_id='a833b977-...'` (`GetRunParent`'s predicate) returned **0**, and `GET /api/v1/runs/a833b977-...` carried **no `calledBy` object** while the control's did.

  **The consequence, on the side effect.** `/data/child.log` holds markers 1-90 at `23:44:17` … `23:45:46` (`w2-5/partB-b9-final.txt`): the child ran **all 90 iterations to completion**, of which **37 markers (`child,54` … `child,90`, `23:45:10` … `23:45:46`) are strictly after the parent's terminal timestamp** `23:45:09.110168`, the last one **≈36.9 s** after it (`23:45:46` − `23:45:09.110168` = 36.890 s; **the markers are second-granular, so this figure carries no more than ±1 s and is deliberately not quoted to three decimals** — only the `Succeeded` headline of 38.840 s is DB-clock-to-DB-clock and therefore exact). Cross-clock comparison is safe at this granularity: the child's DB `created_at` `23:44:16.589078` and `agent2`'s own log line for the same event `23:44:16.590933` are **1.855 ms** apart in the causally required order, bounding agent-to-DB skew well under the second granularity of the markers.

  **The control proves the cascade itself works, twice, on both agent pairings.** With the link row written, the cascade is immediate: parent `09b5013b` (agent1) -> child `13dd04e6` (agent2), cancelled at `23:41:27.415940`/`23:41:27.419735` = **3.795 ms** apart; parent `6eebe7ba` (agent2) -> child `f907911a` (agent1), `23:43:18.303064`/`23:43:18.306692` = **3.628 ms** apart (`w2-5/armA-cascade.txt`, `w2-5/armA2-control2.txt`). Those children stopped at markers **15/90** and **45/90** respectively — note that even a working cascade leaves ~2 s of side effects, because the agent learns of the cancellation from its own poll.
  - **Injection state of each control, stated precisely — only control 1 is uninjected, and it alone suffices.** **Control 1 (`09b5013b`) is fully clean:** created `23:40:52.085023`, cancelled `23:41:27.415940`, and the steplock was cleared at `23:40:26` (nginx exec PID 51, `w2-5/baseline-gate.txt`) with the next arm not issued until `23:42:27` (PID 59, `w2-5/partB-inject.txt`) — no part of its span is inside an armed window. **Control 2 (`6eebe7ba`) is NOT uninjected and must not be described as such**: the steplock was re-armed for `agent2` at `23:42:44.459Z` (`w2-5/partB-rearm.txt`) and not cleared until `23:44:48.865Z` (`w2-5/partB-b4-unlinked.txt`), so its entire call-step-to-cancel span sits inside a *nominally* armed window — `w2-5/armA2-control2.txt` says so in its own header ("steplock still armed for agent2"). What is true, and is what the control actually needs, is that **the injection did not cover control 2's link-report window**: its link was written at `23:42:35` while the lock was clear (three `204`s on `POST /api/v1/agents/agent2/steps` at `23:42:35`, `w2-5/nginx-full.log:335,336,338`), and the arm demonstrably was not in force afterwards either — `nginx-full.log:451` records `POST /api/v1/agents/agent2/steps -> 200 87` **succeeding at `23:43:19` while nominally armed**, as does a `204` at `23:43:56` (`:498`). The captured nginx exec PIDs run `43, 51, 59, 67, 75, [83 absent], 91, 105, 113, 121` — stride 8 everywhere else — so one nginx exec in that interval went uncaptured, and the mechanism note in the runbook's execution notes records that `nginx -s reload` does not deterministically re-configure an *already-established* upstream keepalive connection. **The differential conclusion is unaffected:** control 1 alone establishes that the cascade works when the link exists, and control 2 remains valid as a second measurement of the *cascade* (its link was written; its cancel fired at `23:43:18.264`) rather than as a second uninjected trial. **Part B is separately unaffected** — its arm at `23:44:08.510227` was probe-confirmed `403` at `23:44:09.321` and is bracketed by three logged agent-originated denials at `23:44:16` (`nginx-full.log:539-545`), so its window is verified from inside, not assumed.
- **Expected:** One published statement in `docs/jobs.md` is contradicted outright; a second is contradicted only on a reading whose scope is narrower than it first appears; a third published sentence says the *opposite* and has to be adjudicated here rather than filed elsewhere.
  1. **PRIMARY — `docs/jobs.md:671-673`, which this arm falsifies without qualification.** "Once the child run exists, the call step's status badge in the run detail view follows the child run's current status. **This remains accurate if the parent agent stops before it can submit a final step report.**" That is a published *durability* promise about precisely the composition observed: the child run existed (`23:44:16.589078`) and the final step report never landed. And the observed state is worse than an inaccurate badge — **there is no badge**: the parent's `step_reports` holds exactly one row (step 0 `prelude`) and no row for step 1 at all, so the sentence has nothing to be true of. The reason is structural: the badge's substrate is created by a *different, unretried* request than the one that created the child, so when that request is the one that fails, the promised accuracy has no substrate to attach to. **The major rests on this citation.**
  2. **SECONDARY, and its scope is disclosed rather than glossed — `docs/jobs.md:675-680`.** The clause that promises cancellation is inside the `timeoutMinutes` sentence: "To bound the wait, set the step's `timeoutMinutes`: if the child run has not reached a terminal state within that many minutes, the call step fails with a timeout and — once the parent run finalizes — **the child run is cancelled**." (`:675-678`). **This workload sets no `timeoutMinutes`** (`test/edgecase/workloads/call-parent.payload.json` — the call step is bare `call: {job: edge-call-child}`), so that clause does not govern it. The sentence that *does* govern the unset case is `:679-680`: "When `timeoutMinutes` is unset there is no timeout (the call waits until the child finishes **or the parent run is cancelled**)." Read strictly, that promises only that the *parent's wait* ends on cancel — it does not itself say the child is cancelled. So `:678` is cited as **evidence of the documented intent** (the docs plainly consider a surviving child to be the thing the cancellation path exists to prevent), not as the contract this arm breaches. `docs/high-availability.md:415-416` ("`call:` descendants cascade-cancelled") is likewise **corroboration of intent only** — it is scoped to the startup/heartbeat reconcile path (`POST /api/v1/agents/{id}/runs/reconcile`), which is *not* the endpoint driven here; the endpoint driven here was the human cancel `POST /api/v1/runs/{id}/cancel`.
  3. **ADJUDICATED HERE — `docs/jobs.md:707-708` says the child *should* complete, and it is the stale sentence.** Inside the slot-deadlock warning: "Cancelling the parent releases its slot, after which the child completes (and the parent's `finally` block still runs)." That describes **exactly the outcome this arm observed**, so leaving it in a separate observation as merely "appears stale" would leave a published sentence standing that undercuts this entry. Adjudication: (a) the code cascades — `handleCancelRun` calls `cancelDescendantRuns` unconditionally at `api_runs.go:382`, under a comment (`:378-381`) whose stated purpose is "so a child job doesn't keep running after its parent is cancelled"; (b) `cancelDescendantRuns` → `MarkRunFinished` cancels a `Queued` descendant just as readily as a `Running` one, so the `:707` scenario's own `Queued` child would be cancelled too, not completed; (c) both controls here measured the cascade firing in **3.795 ms / 3.628 ms**. `:707-708` therefore contradicts `:671-673`, `:678`, `docs/high-availability.md:415-416`, the code, and the measurements — five to one. **It is a documentation defect in its own right** (a reader following it would design a `max-concurrent: 1` recovery procedure that does not work), and it is the sentence that must change, not the basis of this entry. **Not exercised:** no arm here produced a slot-deadlocked `Queued` child, so (b) is a code reading, not a measurement.

  **Why this remains major on the rebuilt basis.** The published `:671-673` promise is broken; the design intent is unambiguous across four `cancelDescendantRuns` cascade sites, the `api_runs.go:378-381` comment, a WebUI "Called by …" feature (`web/src/routes/RunDetail.svelte:1148`) that exists solely to surface this edge, and two controls measuring the cascade working end-to-end; and the failure is permanent and silent, with no operator-visible trace of the lost link on any surface.
- **Notes:**
  - **Root cause: the only persisted parent-to-child edge is written by a separate, unretried, error-discarded HTTP request.** `ExecuteCallStep` creates the child at `internal/agent/callstep.go:50` and publishes the link at `:62-66` with `_ = client.ReportStep(...)`; `Client.ReportStep` (`internal/agent/client.go:207-210`) is a bare `c.do` with **no retry wrapper at all** — one attempt, any error, link gone, and nothing on the agent, the controller, or the operator's side records that it happened. `handleAgentCreateChildRun` (`internal/controller/api_agent.go:780-806`) receives `parentRunID` but uses it only for the ownership guard at `:783` and calls `createRunFromJob(..., "agent:"+agentID)` at `:800`, so the child row itself keeps no parent reference.
  - **Two production triggers need no proxy, no WAF and no injection at all — they are properties of the two lines themselves.** (a) **A stale access token loses the link outright, because the client's 401 replay is GET-only.** `Client.do` retries a `401` after invalidating the token source **only when `method == http.MethodGet`** (`internal/agent/client.go:93-96`, whose own comment says the restriction exists to avoid replaying a non-idempotent write). `ReportStep` is a `POST`, so a token that expires in the window between `callstep.go:50` and `:62` yields one `401`, no replay, and — via the bare `_ =` — no trace. (b) **`ctx` cancellation or the step deadline in the same window does the same.** `:62` is passed the **same `ctx`** as the surrounding step (`callstep.go:89-96` documents that this ctx carries `timeoutMinutes` when set and the run's cancellation otherwise), so a cancel or a deadline landing in that gap aborts the link request while the child — already created at `:50` by a *completed* request — exists and runs. In other words the state this scenario produced by refusing a URI is also reachable from an ordinary expiring token and from an ordinary cancel, on an unmodified deployment.
  - **The design's only redundancy is nullified end to end, so nothing after the fact can repair the link.** The terminal step report genuinely does re-send `ChildRunID` (`internal/agent/orchestrator.go:663`) and genuinely does go through `retryUntilSuccess` (`:666`) — that is the redundancy `callstep.go:56-61` is relying on. But `handleAgentStepReport` returns `200 {"alreadyFinalized":true}` **before** touching `step_reports` whenever the run is terminal (`internal/controller/api_agent.go:506-521`), and `Client.do` treats **any** 2xx as success (`client.go:106-112`, only `>= 400` becomes an error), so `retryUntilSuccess` sees success and stops after one attempt. Measured live: `POST /api/v1/agents/agent2/steps -> 200 87` at `23:45:11` with the link still absent afterwards. Full detail in the companion observation below.
  - **Blast radius: one query gates all seven entry points.** `cancelDescendantRuns` (`api_runs.go:390-412`) reaches children only through `ListChildRunIDs` (`internal/store/postgres.go:311-328`), the exact query measured empty above. Its four direct call sites are `api_runs.go:382` (public cancel), `api_agent.go:626` (agent parent-finish report), `queuedrun_reaper.go:77`, and `stuckrun_reaper.go:88` inside `failOrphanedRun`; expanding `failOrphanedRun`'s four callers (`stuckrun_reaper.go:58`, heartbeat reconcile `api_agent.go:114`, claim-build failure `api_agent.go:188`, startup reconcile `api_agent.go:824`) gives **seven**. **Only `api_runs.go:382` was exercised live here**; the other six are the same helper over the same query, which is a code-read inference, not six measurements.
  - **A second entry point fired and did nothing, for an unrelated reason.** After the cancel, `agent2` reported the parent finished: nginx logged `POST /api/v1/agents/agent2/runs/b556c457.../finish -> 200 94` at `23:45:11`, the `alreadyFinalized` no-op body. `handleAgentFinishRun` returns at that point — the CAS-miss branch opens at `api_agent.go:613` and returns at `:621` — **before** reaching `cancelDescendantRuns` at `:626`. So the cancel endpoint's single walk was the only chance this child ever got: with a link present that is enough (3.6-3.8 ms, measured), with the link missing there is no second attempt. **This early return is not specific to this scenario or its injection** — it applies to every human cancel — so it is filed separately below as its own observation.
  - **The system logs a promise it cannot keep.** `agent2` emitted `WARN "call: context done before child finished; child run will be cancelled once the parent run finalizes" childRunId=a833b977-...` at `23:45:11.483212` (`w2-5/agent2-full.log`, source `callstep.go:102`). The parent had already finalized 2.373 s earlier and the child was never cancelled. An operator reading that line has been told the opposite of what happened.
  - **How the state was produced, and the honest limits of that.** The 1-5 ms gap between `callstep.go:50` and `:62` is not hittable with container-level injection (the same wall W2-3's 1 ms Arm D1 and W2-4's 4 ms Part B hit), so the scenario refuses the *request* rather than racing the *instant*: an nginx exact-match `location = /api/v1/agents/agent2/steps` returning 403, armed by `tools/inject.sh steplock` (`test/edgecase/compose/nginx-steplink.conf`). The baseline gate proves the scoping — armed, `POST .../agents/agent2/steps` = **403** while `POST .../agents/agent1/steps` = 401 and `POST .../agents/agent2/runs/{id}/children` = 401, i.e. proxied (`w2-5/baseline-gate.txt`). **The 403 is a stand-in**, not a reproduction of a specific production incident: a WAF or LB rule, a controller restart mid-request, a reset connection, a request timeout, or the agent dying in the window all reach the same state, because the failure handling for that request is `_ =` and there is no retry. **What is not measured is how often that request fails in production.** What *is* established is that the system has no recovery from it at all, and that the 4xx flavour is permanent by construction (W1-5's `retryUntilSuccess` 4xx-abandonment major, `internal/agent/retry.go:33-37`).
  - **Cost of the substitution, stated because it widens the injected window beyond the one request.** While armed, *every* step report from `agent2` was refused: nginx logged **three** 403s on that path at `23:44:16` — the `prelude` step's terminal report (the one that produced `permanent error, giving up retry status=403`, since it alone goes through `retryUntilSuccess`), the call step's own `Running` report (`orchestrator.go:427`, also `_ =`), and the link report. That is why step 1 has no row rather than a row with a NULL `child_run_id`; both shapes return nothing from `ListChildRunIDs`, so the defect is the same either way. The steplock was armed at `23:44:08.5` and cleared at `23:44:49.8`, so everything from the child's creation onward — its claim, its execution, the cancel, the self-heal attempt — ran uninjected. **That the clear really took effect is evidenced from inside, not assumed:** the post-cancel terminal report reached the controller (`POST /api/v1/agents/agent2/steps -> 200 87` at `23:45:11`, `w2-5/nginx-full.log:672`) on the very path that had been denied, which matters given the arm-activation caveat recorded in the per-control bullet above.
  - **Cross-referenced, not re-filed:** the dangling `step 0 'prelude' Running` row under a `Cancelled` run is the shape already recorded at `FINDINGS.md:587` (W2-2) and `:330` (W1-5); the 4xx abandonment that makes the loss permanent is W1-5's major.
  - **The fix has to make the child and its link one write.** Any repair that keeps them as two requests keeps this window: options are to have `POST .../children` persist the edge itself (it already receives `parentRunID`; a step index would need adding), or to make `callstep.go:62` blocking and fatal — a child whose link cannot be recorded should fail the step, since an unrecordable child is exactly an unmanageable one. Note that retrying harder is **not** sufficient on its own: see the companion observation below on the terminal report being dropped with a `200`.
  - **Not established:** whether the child, had it *failed*, would have been noticed at all (the parent was already terminal, so its status could not propagate); and whether an unlinked child that is still `Queued` when the parent is cancelled is likewise left behind — the same query gates it, but this arm's child was already `Running`.

## W2-5 — observation: the terminal report that `callstep.go:56-61` says "self-heals" a lost child link is answered `200 OK` and silently dropped once the parent run is terminal — the one case where the link is needed
- **Invariant:** none directly; it is the reason the major above is permanent rather than transient. Cited under I1 as the missing recovery path.
- **Severity:** minor (observation — as-designed guard, wrong outcome in this composition)
- **Repro:** `test/edgecase/scenarios/w2-5-unlinked-child.md`, Part B steps B7-B9. Raw evidence: `w2-5/nginx-full.log` (line 672), `w2-5/partB-b9-final.txt`, `w2-5/agent2-full.log`.
- **Observed:** After the parent was cancelled at `23:45:09.110168`, `agent2` terminated the call step and sent the terminal step report — which **does** carry `ChildRunID` (`internal/agent/orchestrator.go:653-668`) and **does** go through `retryUntilSuccess`. nginx recorded it succeeding: `172.20.0.8 - - [29/Jul/2026:23:45:11] "POST /api/v1/agents/agent2/steps HTTP/1.1" 200 87`. The 87-byte body is the no-op JSON, not a 204. Immediately afterwards `step_reports` for the parent still held exactly one row (`step 0 'prelude' Running`) and `SELECT count(*) ... child_run_id IS NOT NULL` was still **0**. The mechanism is explicit in the source: `handleAgentStepReport` re-reads the run and, for `Succeeded/Failed/Cancelled`, returns `200` with `{"alreadyFinalized":true}` **before** touching `step_reports` (`internal/controller/api_agent.go:506-521`). The same 200/87 pair appears in the two control trials (`23:41:28`, `23:43:19`) where it was harmless because the link had already been written — **note the `23:43:19` one landed inside a nominally armed steplock window and still returned `200`, which is the counter-example recorded in the major's per-control injection-state bullet.**
- **Expected:** `internal/agent/callstep.go:56-61` states the design intent: "The terminal report re-sends the link, so a report lost here self-heals; failure to send is non-fatal." Three independent conditions defeat it, and the third is the interesting one: (1) it presupposes the agent survives to send a terminal report; (2) `retryUntilSuccess` abandons on any status `< 500` (`internal/agent/retry.go:33-37`); (3) **even a delivered report is discarded whenever the parent run is already terminal — which is precisely the state a lost link matters in**, because it is the cascade that needed the link. The comment is an unexported function's own doc and is therefore *not* a documented contract under this campaign's rule (`FINDINGS.md:476-514`), which is why this is filed as an observation and the violation above rests on `docs/jobs.md` instead.
- **Notes:** The agent cannot distinguish success from silent discard: `Client.ReportStep` returns nil for any 2xx, so both the `204` (written) and the `200` `alreadyFinalized` (dropped) read as success, and `retryUntilSuccess` stops. The terminal-run guard is defensible on its own terms — it exists so a late report cannot leave a step status inconsistent with a terminal run (comment at `api_agent.go:506-512`) — but `child_run_id` is not step *status*: it is an immutable identity fact protected by `COALESCE(EXCLUDED.child_run_id, step_reports.child_run_id)` (`internal/store/postgres.go:803`) that can never contradict anything. Letting the link column through the terminal guard while continuing to reject status/timing fields would make the advertised self-heal real for case (3), though not for (1) or (2).

## W2-5 — observation: `api.Run.ParentRunID` does not exist and `runs` has no parent column at all — the child-to-parent edge is the same single `step_reports` row read backwards, so one lost report breaks navigation in both directions at once
- **Invariant:** none violated on its own; it is the structural reason the major above has no fallback. Recorded to settle a plan-level open question.
- **Severity:** minor (observation — settles `docs/superpowers/plans/2026-07-30-edge-case-campaign-w2.md:78`)
- **Repro:** `test/edgecase/scenarios/w2-5-unlinked-child.md`, Part B step B6, plus the Part A control. Raw evidence: `w2-5/partB-b4b-b7-cancel.txt` (live `information_schema` read and both API bodies), `w2-5/armA-cascade.txt`.
- **Observed:** The plan's "Facts NOT established" asked whether `api.Run.ParentRunID` (`internal/api/types.go:65`) is ever populated. **The question is misattributed and the answer is now definitive on both readings.**
  1. **There is no `api.Run.ParentRunID` field.** `api.Run` (`internal/api/types.go:51-61`) has no such member; `types.go:65` is `api.CalledBy.ParentRunID`, reached only through the nested, `omitempty` `CalledBy *CalledBy` field (`:60`).
  2. **There is no `runs` parent column.** Live: `SELECT string_agg(column_name, ', ') ... WHERE table_name='runs'` returned `id, job_name, status, params, spec, claimed_by, claimed_at, created_at, updated_at, agent_selector, triggered_by, required_caps, detached`, and `SELECT count(*) ... AND column_name LIKE '%parent%'` returned **0**. Consistent with the schema: `internal/store/migrations/001_init.up.sql:213-226` defines no parent column and none of `002`-`017` adds one.
  3. **`calledBy` *is* populated — derived at read time, from the same row the cascade reads.** `handleGetRun` calls `GetRunParent` (`api_runs.go:183` -> `internal/store/postgres.go:859-878`), which is `SELECT sr.run_id, r.job_name, sr.step_name FROM step_reports sr JOIN runs r ON r.id = sr.run_id WHERE sr.child_run_id = $1`. Control child `13dd04e6`: `"calledBy":{"parentRunId":"09b5013b-...","parentJobName":"edge-call-parent","stepName":"invoke_child"}`. Injected child `a833b977`: **no `calledBy` key at all**, and that query returns 0 rows.

  So the field is populated exactly when `step_reports.child_run_id` was written, and never otherwise — one lost fire-and-forget request removes the parent-to-children traversal (`ListChildRunIDs`), the child-to-parent traversal (`GetRunParent`), and the WebUI's "Called by …" link (`web/src/routes/RunDetail.svelte:1148`) simultaneously. **There is no independent second edge anywhere.**
- **Expected:** No documented contract is contradicted — the docs never promise a `parent_run_id` column, and `docs/jobs.md:671-673`'s durability claim is already cited under the major above. What is worth stating is the design consequence: a parent-child relationship is a property of the *runs*, but it is stored as a property of a *step report*, written by a different request than the one that created the child, and read only through that row.
- **Notes:**
  - **The only surviving forensic trace of the orphan's origin is `runs.triggered_by = 'agent:agent2'`** (`api_agent.go:800`, `createRunFromJob(..., "agent:"+agentID)`), which names the **agent** that created it, not the run. With two agents and concurrent parents that does not identify a caller. `GET /api/v1/runs?jobName=edge-call-child` still lists the orphan, so it is discoverable — just unattributable.
  - **A cheap, durable fix exists in the schema direction:** `POST .../agents/{id}/runs/{runId}/children` already knows the parent run id (it authorizes on it), so a `runs.parent_run_id` column populated inside `createRunFromJob`'s own transaction would make the edge exist as soon as the child does, with `step_reports.child_run_id` demoted to a UI convenience.
  - **The `docs/jobs.md:707-708` conflict is adjudicated inside the major above, not here.** An earlier draft of this scenario filed it as a passing observation ("appears simply stale"); that was the wrong home for it, because a published sentence that describes the major's own observed outcome as the *intended* one has to be resolved where the major is argued. See the major's Expected item 3.

## W2-5 — observation: a human cancel gets exactly **one** un-retried cascade attempt, because `handleAgentFinishRun`'s CAS-miss return makes the agent-side cascade entry point structurally unreachable and `cancelDescendantRuns` logs-and-continues on a failed child lookup
- **Invariant:** none violated — the cascade did fire, once, on both controls. This is a structural redundancy observation about the cancel path generally.
- **Severity:** minor (observation)
- **Repro:** No injection required to reach the conclusion; it is a code-read confirmed by the live nginx/DB captures of `test/edgecase/scenarios/w2-5-unlinked-child.md`, Part A (both controls) and Part B step B7. Raw evidence: `w2-5/nginx-full.log` (the `.../runs/{id}/finish -> 200 94` line at `23:45:11`), `w2-5/partB-b4b-b7-cancel.txt`, `w2-5/armA-cascade.txt`, `w2-5/armA2-control2.txt`.
- **Observed:** Of the two `cancelDescendantRuns` entry points that a human `POST /api/v1/runs/{id}/cancel` can reach, only one is ever taken, and it is never retried.
  1. **The cancel endpoint's own walk (`api_runs.go:382`) runs exactly once, best-effort, and its result is discarded.** `handleCancelRun` calls `MarkRunFinished` then `cancelDescendantRuns(...)` and returns `204` unconditionally (`api_runs.go:372-384`); `cancelDescendantRuns` returns nothing at all (`:390-412`). The `204` therefore carries **no information** about whether any descendant was reached — the client cannot distinguish "no children" from "the child lookup failed".
  2. **The agent's own parent-finish report can never reach the second walk after a human cancel.** The human cancel already made the run terminal, so when the agent subsequently reports the parent finished, `FinishRun`'s CAS matches no row and `handleAgentFinishRun` takes the `if !updated {` branch that opens at `api_agent.go:613` and returns at `:621` — **before** the `cancelDescendantRuns` call at `:626`. Observed live: `POST /api/v1/agents/agent2/runs/b556c457.../finish -> 200 94` at `23:45:11`, the `alreadyFinalized` body, with no cascade behind it. The ordering is not incidental: a human cancel is *by definition* the case where the run is already terminal when the agent reports, so this branch is taken on **every** human cancel of a running parent, not occasionally.
  3. **And the one attempt swallows its own failure.** `cancelDescendantRuns`' only child lookup is `ListChildRunIDs`, and on error it `slog.Warn`s and `continue`s the BFS (`api_runs.go:396-399`) rather than propagating or retrying. A transient DB error on that single `SELECT` therefore produces the same end state as this scenario's missing link — an uncancelled descendant — with one `WARN` line and a `204` to the operator.
- **Expected:** No published contract states how many attempts the cascade gets, so nothing is contradicted; this is filed because the *combination* removes the redundancy a reader would reasonably assume from there being multiple entry points. `docs/high-availability.md:415-416` documents the cascade on the reconcile path and `docs/jobs.md:678` on the timeout path, which together give the impression of several independent chances; for a human cancel there is exactly one.
- **Notes:** Cheap improvements, in increasing order of cost: let `cancelDescendantRuns` return an error and have `handleCancelRun` surface it (a `500` on a failed cascade is more honest than a `204`); or make the cascade idempotent-and-retried, since `MarkRunFinished` on an already-`Cancelled` descendant is already a no-op. Note that neither helps the major above — with no link row there is nothing to retry *over* — which is why the major's fix has to be at the write side, not the read side. **Also relevant to any later wave that reasons about the other six entry points:** the same `if !updated` shape guards `handleAgentFinishRun` alone. The reaper/reconcile paths call `cancelDescendantRuns` straight after their own `MarkRunFinished` with no such gate (`stuckrun_reaper.go:84-88` inside `failOrphanedRun`, `queuedrun_reaper.go:73-77`), so they are not affected by this particular return — verified by code read, not exercised here.

## W2-6 — a schedule fire is two unsynchronised writes and nothing reconciles them: two runs were created for cron occurrence `00:22:00`, 2.030 s apart, on two different replicas, and both executed the step

- **Invariant:** **I1 (run accounting)**, whose second clause names this defect verbatim — "every API-accepted run reaches exactly one terminal state; **no phantom runs from duplicate fires/webhooks**" (`docs/superpowers/specs/2026-07-29-edge-case-testing-design.md:48`). Unlike W2-5's entry, I1 is **not** being stretched here: a second run created for one cron occurrence is literally a phantom run from a duplicate fire. **I2 (at-most-once side effects)** — "step side effects execute at most once" (`:49`) — is the consequence limb: both runs ran the `tick` step to completion. **Instrument gap, disclosed:** the fixture is `edge-tick` (`test/edgecase/workloads/tick.payload.json`), whose only observable effect is its own log lines, so I2 is evidenced at the *run/step-execution* level (two independent `step_reports` rows, two independent 30-line log streams) and **not** with the shared-volume append-only log that I2's parenthetical prescribes. **NOT I3** — campaign I3 is *no lock leaks* (`:50`) and no mutex, semaphore or named-lock slot is involved in any arm of this scenario.
- **Severity:** major — **reasoning stated, because an earlier version of this entry gave none at all and its I2 limb sits closest to the critical band of any major in this wave.** For **major**: one cron occurrence produced two runs that each executed the step to completion, so a real job's side effects fire twice; the duplicate is **invisible from every API surface** (`runs.triggered_by` carries no occurrence stamp and no unique index involves it, so the only artifact naming the occurrence is the `UPDATE schedules SET last_fired_at = $1` bind in a Postgres statement log an operator does not have); and nothing in the product reconciles or detects it after the fact. Against **critical** by this file's rubric (`:6` — data loss / silent corruption / security): nothing is lost or corrupted — both runs are complete, correct, individually accounted rows that each reached exactly one terminal state, and the schedule itself recovers to steady state on the next occurrence (Part C's `01:14:50.949` line); no privilege boundary is involved; and the *record* does not lie about anything it asserts — it is silent about the occurrence, not false about it. **The clause that would flip it is the I2 limb, and it is bounded only by the fixture:** `edge-tick` emits nothing but log lines, so the doubled side effect here is 30 extra log rows. On a job carrying a deploy, a migration or a payment the same two runs execute the same non-idempotent work twice with the operator unable to see that they did, and this file's own corroboration-of-intent citations (`docs/operations.md:77`, `docs/troubleshooting.md:801` — "never re-queued, since re-running partially-executed steps can duplicate side effects") show the project already treats that as a harm worth refusing a retry over. **The measurement that would settle it is cheap and was not taken:** re-run Part C with `sideeffect.payload.json` (the shared-volume append-only log I2's own text prescribes) instead of `edge-tick`, and count the appends. Two full sets of appends from one occurrence is silent corruption of an external artifact and would be **critical**; this entry is held at major on a fixture that cannot show it either way.
- **Repro:** `test/edgecase/scenarios/w2-6-schedule-duplicate-fire.md`, Part C (the deterministic arm). Part A is the uninjected control and Part B (Arm A) is the unaided race, which missed 20/20 — see the companion observation below. Raw evidence: `w2-6/partC.txt`, `w2-6/partC-lock.txt`, `w2-6/partC-fires.txt`, `w2-6/pg-partC.log.gz`, `w2-6/all-fires.txt`, `w2-6/detector.txt`, `w2-6/final-census.txt`, `w2-6/partA-control.txt`, `w2-6/baseline-gate.txt`.
- **Observed:** DB clock throughout (host and DB agreed to within the ~0.3 s of command latency separating a bracketed `date`/`SELECT NOW()` pair — `00:41:34.839Z` host, `00:41:35.169` DB, `00:41:35.409Z` host, `w2-6/baseline-gate.txt`; no skew correction is applied anywhere below, and every figure quoted to milliseconds is DB-clock-to-DB-clock or Postgres-log-to-Postgres-log).

  ```
  01:13:38.898  psql: BEGIN; SELECT ... FROM schedules WHERE name='edge-every-minute' FOR UPDATE   (last_fired_at = 00:21:00)
  01:13:48.724  INSERT INTO runs ...  triggered_by='schedule:edge-every-minute'   h=172.20.0.5 pid=14303   -> run bbee7673
  01:13:48.727  UPDATE schedules SET last_fired_at = $1 ...  $1 = '2026-07-30 00:22:00+00'   h=172.20.0.5 pid=14303  -> BLOCKS
  01:13:48.971  pg_stat_activity: pid=14303 ip=172.20.0.5 Lock/transactionid state=active
  01:13:49.507  schedules.last_fired_at STILL 00:21:00, n_fires=40         <-- the :189->:194 window, held open
  01:13:50.359  SIGKILL controller3 (172.20.0.5)                          (host clock)
  01:13:50.754  INSERT INTO runs ...  h=172.20.0.3 pid=15188              -> run b5d29e08   <-- DUPLICATE
  01:13:50.756  UPDATE schedules SET last_fired_at = $1 ...  $1 = '2026-07-30 00:22:00+00'   h=172.20.0.3 pid=15188  -> BLOCKS
  01:13:51.624  pg_stat_activity: pid=14303 (dead leader) Lock/transactionid  AND  pid=15188 (172.20.0.3) Lock/tuple
  01:14:48.908  psql: ROLLBACK — row lock released
  01:14:50.949  next natural fire, occ 00:23:00 (steady state resumed)
  ```

  **The proof that both fires were for the same occurrence is the bind parameter, and it is the only surface in the system that carries it.** `runs.triggered_by` is `'schedule:edge-every-minute'` for both rows with no occurrence stamp, so the duplicate is invisible in the API. What distinguishes them is `UPDATE schedules SET last_fired_at = $1` — the value written is `next`, the cron occurrence, not the fire instant — and both blocked statements bound **`$1 = '2026-07-30 00:22:00+00'`** (`w2-6/partC-fires.txt`, fires 40 and 41; raw at `w2-6/pg-partC.log.gz` lines **1413015** and **1414990** — the `DETAIL:  parameters: $1 = '2026-07-30 00:22:00+00'` lines themselves; an earlier draft cited `1413014`/`1414989`, which are the `LOG:  execute ... UPDATE schedules SET last_fired_at = $1` half of each pair, one line above the bind being quoted). Across the whole 47-minute session the extractor found **four** occurrences with two `INSERT`s behind them, and this is **the only one that is not an artifact of the deliberate Part D schedule reset** — i.e. the only duplicate *within a single schedule incarnation* (`w2-6/all-fires.txt`). The other three are `00:19:00` (fires 37 and 46), `00:20:00` (38 and 47) and `00:21:00` (39 and 48): Part D's `DELETE`+`POST` returned the schedule to `last_fired_at IS NULL`, so the new incarnation re-walked occurrences the old one had already fired, and each of those pairs straddles the reset (`01:10-01:12` vs `01:18-01:20`) rather than being two fires of one live schedule. **An earlier draft of this sentence said "exactly one", which is wrong on the face of the cited file;** the substance — that only Part C produced a duplicate *without* an operator resetting the schedule — is unaffected.

  **Both runs executed.** Final census (`w2-6/final-census.txt`): `bbee7673` `Succeeded` (created `01:13:48.724`, step 0 `tick` `01:13:50.934`->`01:14:21.004`, **30** log rows) and `b5d29e08` `Succeeded` (created `01:13:50.754`, step 0 `tick` `01:14:49.046`->`01:15:19.112`, **30** log rows). One cron occurrence produced 60 log lines where 30 were due, and would have produced two of whatever real side effect the job carried.

  **The detector saw it independently of the Postgres log:** `n_fires` went 39 -> 40 -> 41 at `01:13:36.405` / `01:13:48.885` / `01:13:50.979` — two fires **2.094 s** apart as sampled, **2.030 s** apart by their `created_at` — while `last_fired_at` stayed frozen at `00:21:00` for both (`w2-6/detector.txt`).
- **Expected:** Two runs for one occurrence of one schedule is wrong on the invariant alone, and the code offers no mechanism that could have prevented it. `checkAndFireSchedules` (`internal/controller/scheduler.go:86-204`) contains no `Begin`/`Commit` anywhere: `CreateRun` at `:189` and `UpdateScheduleLastFiredAt` at `:194` are two independent autocommit statements, and **nothing anywhere checks whether a run already exists for an occurrence** — `triggered_by` is `"schedule:"+sc.Name` with no timestamp and there is no unique index that involves it (`internal/store/migrations/001_init.up.sql:213-240`). A crash, a lost connection, or any delay between the two therefore leaves the first write committed and the bookkeeping behind, and the successor recomputes the *same* `next` from the stale `last_fired_at` and fires it again. **No published statement is contradicted, and two that look relevant are not:** `docs/high-availability.md:159` ("Only **Pending->Queued transitions and schedule fires are paused** while there is no leader") and `:163` ("After promotion, the new leader processes any accumulated Pending Runs on the next tick — no runs are lost") are both *loss*-direction promises and say nothing about a run being created twice; `docs/high-availability.md:81` ("Only the leader transitions Pending->Queued and fires schedules") is **true and not contradicted** — the two fires came from two *sequential* leaders ~0.4 s apart, not from two concurrent ones, so this is not a split-brain observation (that is W0-1's, `FINDINGS.md:156`). `docs/operations.md:77` and `docs/troubleshooting.md:801` ("never re-queued, since re-running partially-executed steps can duplicate side effects") are corroboration of *intent* only: the design elsewhere treats duplicated side effects as a harm worth refusing a retry over.
- **Notes:**
  - **How the state was produced, and the honest limits of that.** The unaided window is **1-3 ms** wide (38 fires measured, median 2.0 ms — see the companion observation), which container-level injection cannot hit; so Part C keeps the *fault* (the leader dies with the run committed and `last_fired_at` not advanced) and removes the timing precision by **widening the window**: a `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE` held on the schedule's own row makes the `:194` statement block for as long as the lock is held, and the `SIGKILL` then lands anywhere inside a ~60 s window instead of a 2 ms one. This is the same substitution W2-5 made with its URI-scoped 403 (`FINDINGS.md:885` notes) and it is a **stand-in, not a reproduction of a specific production incident**: the identical state is reachable from `kill -9`, an OOM kill, a pod eviction, a pool connection reset, a Postgres failover, or a `statement_timeout` landing in the 2 ms gap. **What is not measured is how often that happens in production.** What *is* established is that the system has no recovery from it at all.
  - **The blocked write is not merely a device — it is the second half of the fault, and the injection is faithful about that.** `pg_stat_activity` recorded the leader waiting on `Lock/transactionid` for ~1.4 s before it was killed and the successor waiting on `Lock/tuple` behind it, and at `01:13:51.624` **both** were queued on the same row — including the dead leader's backend, which Postgres had not yet reaped because it was itself asleep in the lock wait.
  - **Correction to the campaign plan's facts block (`docs/superpowers/plans/2026-07-30-edge-case-campaign-w2.md:74`), which says the two writes go to "separate pool connections".** In **all 48** fires captured here the `INSERT` and the `UPDATE` were logged under the **same** Postgres backend pid (e.g. `pid=14303` for both halves of fire 40, `pid=15188` for both halves of fire 41; the pid *changes between* fires as pgxpool hands out different connections). The extractor pairs each `INSERT` with the next `UPDATE` **on the same pid** and prints `ORPHANED FIRE` when there is none, so every one of the 48 rows of the paired table in `w2-6/all-fires.txt` is itself the same-pid evidence. **An earlier draft said "45", which is that file's *distinct-occurrence* count (`distinct occurrences written: 45`), not its fire count** — conservative, and it changes nothing. The defect is therefore **not** "two connections" but "two autocommit statements with no enclosing transaction" — the same defect, but the plan's phrasing would send a fixer looking at pool configuration instead of at the missing `Begin`.
  - **Correction to the Task 7 brief's detection rule.** The brief says to "identify duplicates by `created_at` falling in the same minute". That test is **unsound on this system** for two independent reasons, both measured here: (i) a leadership change resets the schedule-check phase, so an ordinary failover legitimately produces two runs a few hundred milliseconds apart *for different occurrences* — **8** such pairs appear in Part B, spanning **123-372 ms** (e.g. `00:53:46.588` occ `23:54:00` and `00:53:46.960` occ `23:55:00`, 372 ms apart, `w2-6/armA-fires.txt`; an earlier draft said 5 pairs at 198-372 ms, so the control is stronger than first claimed); and (ii) the schedule runs ~59.9 minutes behind wall clock (see the companion major), so `created_at` minutes bear no relation to occurrence minutes at all. The sound test is the repeated `$1` bind **with two `INSERT`s behind it**.
  - **The fix has to make the two writes one transaction, or make the occurrence unique.** Either wrap `:189`-`:196` in a single transaction (both statements already run on one pooled connection, so this is a `Begin`/`Commit` away), or record the occurrence on the run and give it a unique constraint — e.g. `triggered_by='schedule:<name>@<occurrence>'` or a dedicated column — which would additionally make duplicates *detectable* after the fact, which today they are not from any API surface. Retrying `:194` harder is **not** sufficient on its own: the run is already committed when the process dies, so there is nothing left running to do the retry.
  - **Cross-referenced, not re-filed:** the un-retried `:195` Warn path and the silent-skip branch at `:197-201` are the subject of the companion major below; the pgbouncer split-brain that lets two leaders fire *concurrently* is W0-1's (`FINDINGS.md:156`).
  - **Not established:** whether the same duplication can be produced without any injection by an ordinary controller restart — a rolling deploy sends SIGTERM, which lets `RunScheduler` return through its `defer release()` rather than dying mid-check, and that path was not exercised here.

## W2-6 — an every-minute schedule is born ~59.9 minutes behind, cannot catch up under stable leadership (a promotion buys back only one occurrence), and lives 8.4-13.1 s from a cliff: one refused `last_fired_at` write pushed it over and it then silently never fired again — 8 consecutive checks, 0 runs, 0 log lines

- **Invariant:** the violation rests on the **documented-contract limb**, not on an invariant — **and the citation's antecedent is handled explicitly here rather than left implicit, because citing a sentence that does not cover the observed case is this wave's declared weak spot (`:1509`) and neither arm of this entry involved controller downtime.** `docs/resources.md:324` reads "**If the controller is down** during a scheduled fire time, the fire is caught up within 1 hour after restart", and it is the **only** catch-up sentence anywhere in `docs/`. Its literal antecedent is not met by the drift arm (uninjected, no restart) or by Part D (one refused `UPDATE`, no downtime), so it is **not** cited as a directly falsified conditional and any reading of this entry that treats it as one is wrong. What *is* cited is the mechanism the sentence describes and the only place the product documents it: the "within 1 hour" window **is** `windowStart = now-1h` (`internal/controller/scheduler.go:92-97`), and that expression is evaluated on **every** check — not only after a restart (Observed (1) measures a fresh schedule being born ~59.9 minutes behind with no controller ever having been down). The sentence therefore states the system's general catch-up rule through its most operator-visible instance, and read that way it is false for a `* * * * *` schedule in the strongest available form: no fire is *ever* caught up — every fire is ~59.9 minutes late, and the lag only ever grows while one leader stays leader (a promotion retires one extra occurrence, so a restart catches up **one** occurrence, not the backlog — Observed (2b)) — and after a single lost `last_fired_at` write nothing is ever fired again at all. **I5 (bounded recovery)** is the closest-fitting invariant — "after fault injection the system returns to steady state within documented bounds" (`docs/superpowers/specs/2026-07-29-edge-case-testing-design.md:52`) — but its own parenthetical names `docs/high-availability.md` as the source of the bounds and the bound broken here is in `docs/resources.md`, so I5 is cited as closest fit and the contract citation carries the entry. **I1 (run accounting)** is cited on W0-2's precedent (`FINDINGS.md:88`, which argued I1 for a permanently dropped occurrence): a scheduled run that was due and never executes is silent loss.
- **Severity:** major — **re-argued on the *demonstrated* path, because an earlier version of this line gated the severity on the wrong question.** That version held the entry at major "**only because** the no-fault path to the cliff is derived rather than run to completion", i.e. it made the severity wait on a 3-hour drift measurement. It should not: **the path this scenario actually ran already carries the entry on its own.** One refused `UPDATE` at `:194` — reachable with no exotic fault at all, from a connection reset, a Postgres failover, a `statement_timeout`, a deadlock, a disk-full `ERROR` or the process dying between `:189` and `:194`, all of which `:195` treats identically as `slog.Warn` and continue (see the injection note at `:1060`) — consumed the schedule's whole remaining headroom in one step and the schedule then **never fired again**: 8 consecutive checks, 8 `UPDATE`s, **0 runs, 0 log lines**, measured, with everything after the 15.6 s arm window uninjected. That is silent, permanent, unrecoverable loss of **every** occurrence of a live schedule — no run, no `WARN`, no metric, a `last_fired_at` that keeps advancing so the row looks healthy, and no path back through the API short of `DELETE`+`POST` (`UpsertSchedule` omits `last_fired_at` from its `ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE` list). **Held at major, not critical, on this file's rubric (`:6`) for reasons that do not depend on the follow-up:** nothing already recorded is corrupted or falsified (the 48 runs that did fire are correct and complete), no data is destroyed — the loss is of work that was *due*, not of work that existed — no privilege boundary is involved, and one `DELETE`+`POST` restores full service, so the condition is permanent but not irreparable. **The 3-hour follow-up below remains the highest-value measurement in the batch and its value is unchanged — but what it settles is *reachability with zero faults*, not this entry's severity.** If it lands, the entry becomes critical on the strength of needing no trigger whatsoever; if it does not, the demonstrated one-refused-write path stands exactly as argued here. For the record, the derived no-fault arithmetic: at the measured `+0.084 s/min` drift the 13.067 s of birth headroom in the first incarnation is consumed in **~156 minutes with nothing injected at all** (see Observed (2) and the reachability note below). **If that holds, this is critical** — silent, permanent loss of *every* occurrence of a schedule, with no run, no log line, no metric and a `last_fired_at` that keeps advancing so even the row looks healthy. An earlier draft of this entry's rationale said "not critical because it needs one fault"; that clause is **withdrawn**, because it is contradicted by the drift arithmetic in this same entry.

  > **HIGHEST-VALUE FOLLOW-UP MEASUREMENT IN THIS BATCH — recommend triaging this before any other W2-6 item.** One unattended ~3-hour observation settles the severity of this entry: apply a fresh `* * * * *` schedule to an otherwise idle stack, **inject nothing and kill nothing**, and sample `schedules.last_fired_at`, `NOW()` and `count(*) FROM runs WHERE triggered_by='schedule:...'` every 30 s. If the fire count freezes while `last_fired_at` keeps advancing, the cliff is reached with **zero** faults and this entry is **critical**. Two validity conditions, both learned here: the window must be **promotion-free** (a leader promotion buys back ~60 s of headroom — see the "promotions heal the lag" note below, which is the one thing that could stretch the wait far past 156 minutes), and the sampler must be **continuous** (the intermediate fingerprint survives only ~60 s).
- **Repro:** `test/edgecase/scenarios/w2-6-schedule-duplicate-fire.md`, Part A (birth lag and drift, uninjected) and Part D (one refused write, then eight dead checks). Raw evidence: `w2-6/detector.txt`, `w2-6/partA-control.txt`, `w2-6/all-fires.txt`, `w2-6/partD.txt`, `w2-6/final-census.txt`, `w2-6/pg-full.log.gz`.
- **Observed:** four independent measurements, all on the plain `test/ha` stack, all DB clock.

  **(1) The birth lag, uninjected, and its formula.** `last_fired_at` starts `NULL`, so `base = windowStart = now-1h` (`internal/controller/scheduler.go:92-97`) and the first fire is for the first cron occurrence *after* `now-1h`. Three fires, two separate schedule incarnations, both created through the API with no DB mutation:

  | incarnation | first check | occurrence written | lag (s) | headroom to 3600 s |
  |---|---|---|---|---|
  | applied `00:41:50.007` | `00:42:46.932` | `2026-07-29 23:43:00` | **3586.933** | 13.067 |
  | re-applied `01:18:34.754` | `01:18:51.378` | `2026-07-30 00:19:00` | **3591.378** | 8.622 |
  | (same, 2nd fire) | `01:19:51.581` | `2026-07-30 00:20:00` | **3591.582** | 8.418 |

  The lag equals `3600 - (60 - f)` where `f` is the sub-minute phase of the schedule check (`46.932`, `51.378`, `51.581` s respectively) — the formula is a code reading and all three rows satisfy it exactly. **So the headroom to the 1-hour catch-up boundary is `60 - f` seconds, i.e. somewhere in (0, 60] depending on nothing but when the scheduler leader happened to acquire its lock.**

  **(2) It does not catch up — measured over 6 uninjected fires, and it slowly loses ground.** Part A, single leader (`controller2`, promoted `00:34:46.105`, the only promotion in the window), 6 fires `00:42:46.932` ... `00:47:47.352`, 6 runs, all `Succeeded`, alternating `agent1`/`agent2`. The lag over those fires was `3586.933, 3587.138, 3587.142, 3587.149, 3587.347, 3587.352` — it never decreased. Total change over 5 intervals: **+0.419 s**, i.e. **+0.084 s per minute** (measured check intervals `60.004`-`60.205` s). The mechanism is that a check retires exactly one occurrence (W0-2 measured this rate directly, `FINDINGS.md:138-149`) while one minute of wall clock adds exactly one, so for a cron period <= 1 minute the drain rate equals the accumulation rate.

  **The closed form, and what makes the drift one-directional.** Writing `A_k` for the lag at check `k` (`created_at` of the fire minus the occurrence it was for) and `c` for the interval between consecutive checks: since each check retires exactly one 60 s occurrence, `A_{k+1} = A_k + (c - 60)`. The gate at `scheduler.go:71` is `t.Sub(lastScheduleCheck) >= time.Minute` on a 200 ms ticker, so **for a leader that stays leader** `c ∈ [60, 60.2]` and `A` is monotonically **non-decreasing** — it can never shrink, only stand still or creep. **All 17 of the same-leader intervals across Parts A and B satisfy this**: `dA ∈ [-0.006, +0.205]`, with only three negatives and none beyond `-0.006 s`, which is measurement jitter rather than real recovery — the quantity measured is `INSERT`-to-`INSERT` at 1 ms Postgres-log resolution, not tick-to-tick, so a few ms of query-latency variation is expected on top of a `c` the gate guarantees is `>= 60`. The 20 *post-promotion* intervals are the only ones that go meaningfully negative (14 of 20), and they are the subject of (2b). The observed `+0.084 s/min` is exactly the mean ticker overshoot the formula predicts. **Derived, and labelled as such:** at `+0.084 s/min` the 13.067 s of headroom in the first incarnation is consumed in **~156 minutes**; that figure rests on 5 intervals of drift measurement and should be read as "a couple of hours", not as a precise prediction.

  **(2b) Promotions heal the lag, and this is the one force pushing the other way — it was measured, and it is why the schedule survived 20 kills.** `c ∈ [60, 60.2]` only holds while leadership is stable. `lastScheduleCheck` is process-local (`scheduler.go:31`) so a freshly promoted leader checks on its first tick, making `c ≈ 0` for that one interval — and by the closed form the lag then drops by ~60 s. Measured across Arm A's 20 kills: **8 of the promotions landed after that minute's check had already run and therefore retired an extra occurrence, each cutting the lag by 59.6-59.9 s, total −478.156 s**, taking the lag from `3586.933` at fire 1 to `3108.515` at fire 38 (per-fire lag recomputed from `w2-6/armA-fires.txt`: `created_at` minus the `$1` occurrence bind). The other 12 promotions merely pulled that minute's check a few hundred ms earlier and changed the lag by <1 s. **Consequences, and they cut both ways:** (i) the headline "can never catch up" is precisely true only under *stable leadership* — a cluster with churn drains the backlog at roughly one occurrence per promotion-that-lands-after-a-check, and ~59 such promotions would drain it entirely; (ii) one promotion buys back ~60 s of headroom, which at `+0.084 s/min` is ~12 hours of drift, so the no-fault drift path above needs a **promotion-free** window of ~156 minutes, not merely 156 minutes of uptime; and (iii) it explains the otherwise puzzling fact that 20 `SIGKILL`s aimed at this schedule left it healthier than they found it. None of this softens the Part D result, where the fault *adds* 60 s in one step and no promotion intervened.

  **(3) One refused `last_fired_at` write ended the schedule permanently.** Part D re-created the schedule through the API (`DELETE /api/v1/schedules/edge-every-minute` -> `204` at `01:18:33`, `POST /api/v1/schedules` -> `200` at `01:18:34.754`, `last_fired_at` `NULL`), let it fire twice, then armed a `BEFORE UPDATE` trigger on that one row for the span of exactly one schedule check:

  ```
  01:20:45.368  trigger armed  (BEFORE UPDATE ON schedules ... RAISE EXCEPTION)
  01:20:51.582  LIST schedules            h=172.20.0.3
  01:20:51.583  INSERT INTO runs ...      -> run 61d939c2  (occurrence 00:21:00, later Succeeded)
  01:20:51.584  UPDATE schedules ...      -> REFUSED
  01:20:51.586  controller2 WARN "checkAndFireSchedules: failed to update last_fired_at"
                  schedule=edge-every-minute
                  error="ERROR: w2-6 injection: schedules UPDATE refused (SQLSTATE P0001)"
  01:20:52.000  detector: last_fired_at 00:20:00, newest run 01:20:51.583, d = 3651.583   <-- +60.001 s
  01:21:01.017  trigger dropped  (one refused write only; everything after is uninjected)
  01:21:51.588  LIST + UPDATE $1='00:21:00'  with NO INSERT     <-- silent-skip branch, occurrence dropped
  01:22:51.792  UPDATE $1='00:22:00'  no INSERT
  01:23:51.992  UPDATE $1='00:23:00'  no INSERT
  01:24:52.195  UPDATE $1='00:24:00'  no INSERT
  01:25:52.207  UPDATE $1='00:25:00'  no INSERT
  01:26:52 ... 01:28:52  (detector) last_fired_at 00:26:00, 00:27:00, 00:28:00 — still no runs
  ```

  `n_fires` froze at **48** at `01:20:51.583` and had not moved by the final detector sample at `01:29:52.407`, i.e. **8 consecutive schedule checks produced 8 `UPDATE`s and 0 runs** over **8 m 01 s**. `SELECT status, count(*) ... triggered_by='schedule:edge-every-minute'` at teardown: `Succeeded 48`, nothing else. And the whole session's controller logs contain exactly **one** line from `checkAndFireSchedules` — the injected `WARN` above; `docker compose logs --since 300s controller1 controller2 controller3 | grep -icE "schedule|last_fired"` across the dead window returned **0** (`w2-6/partD.txt`).

  **Why one lost write is fatal rather than merely duplicating.** With the update lost, the occurrence stays due, and what happens next depends on how long until the next check versus how much headroom is left. Part C's successor re-checked **2.030 s** later, inside the window, and fired the occurrence again -> the duplicate major above. Part D's next check came **60.005 s** later against **8.418 s** of headroom, so by then `next` (`00:21:00`) was `3651.6 s` old, `windowStart` had passed it, and `scheduler.go:197-201` took the `default:` branch: `last_fired_at` advanced with **no run and no log line**. From that point `last_fired_at` advances 60 s per check while wall clock advances ~60.08 s, so the lag never closes and every subsequent occurrence takes the same branch. **The two failure modes are the same defect on opposite sides of one threshold**, and which one you get is decided by arithmetic the operator cannot see.

  **The prescribed fingerprint exists but is transient — this is why continuous sampling was required.** The Task 7 brief predicts `schedules.last_fired_at` disagreeing with the newest run's `created_at` "by a full cron period". It appeared exactly: `d` went `3591.582` -> **`3651.583`** (`+60.001 s`) at `01:20:52.000` and held for **60.36 s** — and was then **erased** at `01:21:52.362` when the silent-advance branch reset it to `3591.583`, at the same moment the loss became permanent. A sampler that ran only at kill instants, or once a minute, would have seen nothing at all; and after the erasure the only surviving signal is `n_fires` refusing to increment while `last_fired_at` marches on (visible in `w2-6/detector.txt` as `d` *decreasing* by 60 per check: `3591.583, 3531.583, 3471.583, 3411.583, 3351.583, 3291.583, 3231.583, 3171.583`).
- **Expected:** `docs/resources.md:324` tells an operator that a missed fire is caught up within an hour. For a 1-minute schedule that is false in three ways, in increasing severity: (i) in steady state the schedule is permanently ~59.9 minutes behind and **no** occurrence is ever fired on time; (ii) the lag can only grow or stand still while leadership is stable (`A_{k+1} = A_k + (c - 60)` with `c >= 60`), and a single lost `:194` write adds a full 60 s to it in one step — the *only* force that removes lag is a leader promotion (Observed (2b)), which is not something an operator can rely on, and certainly not something the docs offer as the catch-up mechanism they describe; and (iii) once the accumulated lag exceeds 1 hour the schedule stops firing **forever**, with no run, no `WARN`, no `ERROR`, no metric and no state anywhere that says "this schedule is dead" — `last_fired_at` keeps advancing every minute, so even the row looks healthy.

  **"Forever" is literal, because the product has no heal path — the obvious operator reflex does nothing.** `UpsertSchedule` (`internal/store/postgres.go:2109-2122`) is `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT (name) DO UPDATE SET cron = EXCLUDED.cron, job_name = EXCLUDED.job_name, params = EXCLUDED.params, updated_at = NOW()` — **`last_fired_at` is not in that list**. So re-applying the schedule through `POST /api/v1/schedules`, which is what an operator reaches for when a schedule looks stuck, preserves the poisoned `last_fired_at` exactly and changes nothing observable except `updated_at`. `PUT`/`PATCH` do not exist for schedules; there is no "reset", "fire now" or "resync" endpoint anywhere. **The only two ways back are `DELETE /api/v1/schedules/{name}` followed by `POST` (which is what Part D itself used to obtain a birth-state schedule) or direct DB surgery on the row.** That is also why this cannot be dismissed as self-limiting: nothing in the normal operation of the system, and nothing an operator can do through the API short of deleting the schedule, restores it. The blast radius is not exotic: `* * * * *` is the finest granularity the documented cron format supports (`docs/resources.md:305-320`) and it is the campaign's own fixture; the headroom a fresh one is born with is `60 - f` seconds, decided by the scheduler leader's acquisition phase, and **a single failed `UPDATE` on one row consumes 60 s of it**.
- **Notes:**
  - **Cross-reference, not a re-file: the silent-skip branch itself is W0-2's major** (`FINDINGS.md:88`, "Catch-up window boundary ... silently and permanently dropped"), which established the mechanism, argued I1 and recommended a `WARN`. Three things are new here and none of them duplicate it. (a) **Reachability:** W0-2 reached the branch with a Go probe that stamped an occurrence 65 minutes old; this scenario reached it on the live 3-replica stack from *steady state* with a single refused write — no outage, no clock skew, no operator error. (b) **Permanence at the schedule level:** W0-2's finding is about one dropped occurrence; here **every** subsequent occurrence takes the branch, because the walk-back rate and the wall clock advance at the same speed. (c) **The 1-minute-cron corollary to W0-2's own drain-rate observation** (`FINDINGS.md:138-149`): that observation measured 1 occurrence retired per call and concluded "expected, bounded, and self-terminating"; that conclusion holds for the `*/5` schedule it tested and **fails for any period <= 1 minute**, where the drain never terminates. W0-2's numbers are what make this a corollary rather than a new mechanism, and its "no defect" verdict should be read as scoped to periods > 1 minute.
  - **The injection, and why it is a fair stand-in.** A `BEFORE UPDATE` trigger raising `P0001` is a synthetic way to fail one `UPDATE`, chosen because it is scoped to one row, is deterministic, and needs no timing precision (`REVOKE UPDATE` is not available: `unified` is the `POSTGRES_USER` superuser and bypasses privilege checks). It stands in for anything that fails that statement once — a connection reset, a Postgres failover, a `statement_timeout`, a deadlock, a disk-full `ERROR`, or the process dying between `:189` and `:194`. `:195` treats all of them identically: `slog.Warn` and continue. **It was armed for 15.6 s spanning exactly one check** (`01:20:45.368` -> `01:21:01.017`), and the arm's effect is evidenced from inside by the `WARN` line and by `last_fired_at` remaining `00:20:00` across the fire; **everything after `01:21:01.017` was uninjected**, which is what makes the eight dead checks a property of the system and not of the injection.
  - **The brief's Arm C was not needed and is superseded.** The brief asks for the silent-skip path to be demonstrated by backdating `last_fired_at` via `psql`, explicitly labelled as a code-path demonstration rather than a natural observation. That was not done, because Part D reached the same branch by **pure fault injection** on a schedule whose state was established entirely through the API — a strictly stronger result. **No arm of this scenario mutated `schedules` state by hand;** the only direct DB writes were the Part C row lock (a `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE`, which writes nothing) and the Part D trigger.
  - **What an operator can do today, and why it is unsatisfying.** Nothing in the product surfaces this. Detection requires comparing `schedules.last_fired_at` to `NOW()` and alerting when the gap exceeds the cron period by more than a little — a query no documented dashboard, CLI command or metric performs. The minimal fix is the `WARN` W0-2 already recommended at `:197-201`; the real fix is to stop treating a 1-hour absolute window as the catch-up rule for schedules whose period is minutes (e.g. bound the catch-up in *occurrences* rather than in time, or retire the whole backlog in one check instead of one occurrence per check), and to make `:189`-`:196` atomic so the lag cannot grow by 60 s from a single failed statement.
  - **Reachability of the cliff with no fault of any kind — two candidate paths, and one proposed path that this scenario's own data refutes.** This matters because it is the whole difference between major and critical (see Severity).
    - **Path 1 — pure drift, no external event at all.** `A_{k+1} = A_k + (c - 60)` with `c ∈ [60, 60.2]`: the lag creeps up by 0-200 ms per check for as long as one leader stays leader, and the birth headroom is only `60 - f` seconds (measured 8.4-13.1 s). At the measured `+0.084 s/min` a 13.067 s headroom is gone in ~156 promotion-free minutes. **Derived from 5 drift intervals; not run to completion — this is the follow-up measurement named under Severity.**
    - **Path 2 — a check-loop stall longer than 60 s, from an actor that is not the product.** The companion C2 observation below establishes that a plain row lock on the `schedules` row blocks `:194` inline on the tick loop with **no timeout at all** (`statement_timeout` and `lock_timeout` are both `0` and neither string exists in the codebase), so the check's duration is bounded by nothing. Because `lastScheduleCheck` is stamped with the tick time at which the check *started* (`scheduler.go:71-73`), a stall of `s` seconds adds `max(0, s - 60)` to the lag — so any lock hold that keeps the scheduler's write waiting beyond ~68-73 s pushes a fresh 1-minute schedule straight over the cliff. C2's own enumerated actors are all no-fault: a long `ALTER TABLE schedules`, a backup tool taking row locks, an operator's forgotten `BEGIN`, or a `POST /api/v1/schedules` inside a longer transaction. **Code reading plus the C2 measurement; the >60 s case was not itself run.**
    - **Refuted, and recorded so it is not repeated: C2's measured stall did *not* consume headroom.** It is tempting to read C2's 60.004 s lock hold as "a no-fault event already consumed 7× the 8.4 s headroom", and that reading is **wrong**. The scheduler's `UPDATE` was blocked for **51.741 s** (`01:16:51.165` -> `01:17:42.906`), which is *under* 60 s, and because the gate compares against the tick time at the *start* of the stalled check the next check still arrived on cadence at `01:17:51.369` — `c = 60.205 s`, so the lag moved by `+0.205 s`, i.e. an ordinary tick's worth. Measured directly: the fire-44 lag was `3111.164` and the fire-45 lag `3111.370` (`w2-6/detector.txt`, `w2-6/all-fires.txt`). A sub-60 s stall of the check loop is therefore **free** in lag terms, which is exactly why Path 2 needs the `s > 60` qualifier.
  - **Not established:** the exact drift mechanism behind `+0.084 s/min` (the `>= time.Minute` gate on a 200 ms ticker at `scheduler.go:71` will always overshoot by 0-200 ms per check, which is consistent in sign and magnitude, and the closed form in Observed (2) predicts it, but no experiment isolated it); whether a period between 1 and 5 minutes behaves like W0-2's `*/5` or like this `* * * * *` (the crossover is where `period > 1 check interval`, i.e. immediately above 1 minute — a code reading, not measured); whether a fresh schedule left entirely alone really dies at ~156 minutes, which is derived from 5 drift intervals and was not run to completion; and how long a real cluster typically goes without a scheduler promotion, which is what decides whether Path 1 is reachable in practice at all (Observed (2b) shows each promotion buys back ~12 hours of drift).

## W2-6 — observation: one locked `schedules` row stops all scheduling cluster-wide, because the schedule check is inline on the scheduler tick loop and the write has no timeout — 51.3 s of `Pending` measured on an unrelated, API-triggered run

- **Invariant:** none violated. `I5` is the relevant frame (recovery *was* bounded — 0.16-0.43 s after the lock released) and this is filed as the risk, not as a failure.
- **Severity:** minor (observation — as-designed structure, unbounded blast radius)
- **Repro:** `test/edgecase/scenarios/w2-6-schedule-duplicate-fire.md`, Part C2 (the row lock held for 60 s with **no** kill, so nothing is confounded by failover). Raw evidence: `w2-6/partC2-stall.txt`, `w2-6/partC2-lock.txt`, `w2-6/final-census.txt`.
- **Observed:** DB clock.

  ```
  01:16:42.902  psql: BEGIN; SELECT ... FROM schedules WHERE name='edge-every-minute' FOR UPDATE
  01:16:51.164  scheduler fires; run cfc81283 created (Pending)
  01:16:51.695  pg_stat_activity: leader pid=15707 ip=172.20.0.3 blocked Lock/transactionid
  01:16:52.001  UNRELATED run 0fea5f6f created via POST /api/v1/runs (triggered_by='env:UNIFIED_TOKEN')
  01:16:52.407 ... 01:17:41.367   0fea5f6f sampled Pending on a ~2.6 s poll (20 consecutive samples)
  01:17:42.906  psql: ROLLBACK — lock released (held 60.004 s)
  01:17:43.061  cfc81283 -> Running   (+0.155 s after release)
  01:17:43.336  0fea5f6f -> Running   (+0.430 s after release)
  ```
