Read that distribution with its caveat: 10 of the 11 come from Part C, which deliberately placed `timeout_at` ~20 s ahead of a sweep, so the tight clustering is an artifact of the experiment design; the only unaimed sample is `a7177225` at **21.744 s**, and the structural bound from the measured 60.012 s grid is `[0, 60.02) s`.
  3. **`run_approvals` is designated an audit record by the product's own docs, and this is the limb that matters.** `docs/jobs.md:1740` calls it "The approval **audit** row"; `:1725` says "The identity of the decider is recorded (`decidedBy`) in the **audit record**"; `docs/audit.md:4-5` calls it "the existing per-run approval **audit trail** (`run_approvals`)". So I7's "**audit rows** never contradict … reality" bites on the falsified row directly rather than by analogy — and the separate `audit_logs` row makes the same false claim a second time, in the table `docs/audit.md:32` documents.
- **Notes:**
  - **Severity reasoning, both halves, stated rather than asserted.** For **major**: the record is durable (nothing reconciles it — Part D2), it is attributed to a **named** principal, it is displayed as an ordinary decision on all three operator surfaces, the trigger is a documented CLI command that prints success, the audit log — the one artifact that must not lie — asserts a human approved a run that had already failed or been cancelled, and the Part D window is ~10 minutes as configured (default 60) rather than ~60 s. Against **critical**: nothing executes **in the trials this entry covers — a claim now scoped, because Part F falsified it outside them.** The `after` step stayed `Skipped`/`Pending` in all six trials here, no run was created, no side effect fired, no terminal status was reopened. But every one of those decisions landed with the agent already gone (the five timeout-path trials) or already past its cancel-detection fence (Part D, +9.19 s). **When the decision commits *inside* that fence the post-gate step body runs — see the sibling entry immediately below, which was probed rather than assumed and which carries the execution half of this defect.** For this entry's shapes the harm is still a false record rather than unauthorized execution, and that is what holds it at major. **The reason it matters operationally is compliance, not execution:** in a change-control audit these rows are indistinguishable from genuine approvals, and the timestamps that would reveal the problem require joining `run_approvals` to `runs` by hand.
  - **Fix shape, and the trap in the obvious fix.** `handleDecideApproval` (`internal/controller/api_approvals.go:13-54`) should refuse when the run is terminal, and the guard belongs in `DecideApproval`'s SQL (`postgres.go:2439-2450`) so it is atomic — e.g. `AND EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM runs WHERE id = $1 AND status NOT IN ('Succeeded','Failed','Cancelled'))`, plus, for the timeout case, `AND (timeout_at IS NULL OR timeout_at > now())`. **Do not fix this by tightening only the handler:** a handler-side `GetRun` check leaves a TOCTOU window of the same order the observation below measures. Be precise about *which* concurrency, because it is not the same writer pair: V2 measures the decision against the **reaper**, whereas a handler-side `GetRun` guard would race the run's own **terminalization** (the agent's `FinishRun`, or a cancel) landing between the read and the `UPDATE`. What V2 legitimately supplies is the **magnitude** of such a window on this rig — host→`UPDATE` latency **49.9–64.3 ms with ~14 ms of jitter** — not the identity of the racing writer. And note the `timeout_at` clause alone would not close Part D at all — that row had 9m48s left — so the run-status clause is the necessary one. The refusal also needs a response distinguishable from `409 "already decided"`, which currently means something else (see the observation below).
  - **This entry closes W1's carry-forward item 1 (`FINDINGS.md:493`; an earlier draft cited `:497`, which is a blank line), which predicted exactly this and left it unfired.** W1-3 Part B measured a ~14 s window in which such a POST "*would have* satisfied the guard, returned `204`, and written Approved/decidedBy/decidedAt onto an already-Failed run" but never sent one. It has now been sent and confirmed, and the window is **wider than that lead assumed in two ways**: it needs no outage to produce (the agent-local deadline suffices, so the natural window is the full 0–60 s reaper period — ~19–20 s in the sampled cases), and via Part D it needs no timeout at all.
  - **Not a duplicate of W2-7's forged-terminal-write entry (`FINDINGS.md:1163`).** That one requires a stolen agent credential and leaves **no** audit row; this one requires only ordinary `dev` rights on the documented human API and leaves a `204` audit row that reads as success. Same invariant, opposite audit behaviour, disjoint threat models.

## W2-8 Part F — an approve committing inside the agent's 5 s cancel-detection fence makes the gate return **true**, so the post-gate step body **executes** on a run the controller has already marked `Cancelled` — 4/4 aimed attempts, `echo` output written 0.56–2.96 s after the cancel, while `step_reports` records that the step never started
- **Invariant:** **I7 (state display consistency)** — "run status, approval status, and audit rows never contradict each other or reality" (`docs/superpowers/specs/2026-07-29-edge-case-testing-design.md:54`). This is a **second, distinct** I7 breach from the entry above, on the "or reality" limb: on the same run, `logs` holds a `step_index = 2` stdout line while `step_reports` holds **no row at all** for step 2 and the live API answers `{"index":2,…,"status":"Pending"}`; and step 1 reads `WaitingApproval` in `step_reports` while `run_approvals` reads `Approved`. The displayed state actively denies that a step ran, and no artifact anywhere records that one did. **I6 (zombie containment)** — "after the controller fails a run, observed agent-side behavior is *measured and documented* (**not pass/fail**: the architecture has no hard fencing; the operator judges acceptability)" (`…:53`) — is the honest home for the *execution* itself, and per its own text it is a measurement handed to the operator, **not** a scored violation. Two deviations from I6's letter are stated rather than glossed: the controller **cancelled** rather than failed the run, and unlike W1-5's zombie (where the agent could not reach the controller) this agent was fully connected and simply had not polled yet. **I2 (at-most-once side effects) is NOT violated and must not be claimed** — checked, not assumed. I2's text is "step side effects execute **at most once**"; the `after` step executed **exactly once** in each of the four trials, there is no duplication, and this fixture carries none of I2's named detector (the append-only side-effect log on a shared volume). The defect is **zero-vs-once**, not once-vs-twice: the step should not have executed at all. Naming I2 here would be the stretch W2-5 and W2-7 were both corrected for. **I1 is NOT violated either:** the run reached exactly one terminal state (`Cancelled`) and stayed there, no phantom run was created, and the agent's own `FinishRun(Succeeded)` was refused. **NOT I3, NOT I4, NOT I5:** no lock is involved, no log integrity claim is made (the one log line that exists is correct — it is the step table that is silent), and no fault is injected anywhere. **There is also a published-contract limb**, which is unusual for an I6-adjacent finding and is set out in full under **Expected**: `docs/jobs.md:1775-1777` promises that on mid-run cancellation "an in-flight step is interrupted", and it was not.
- **Severity:** major — **at the top of the major band, and it escalates the scope of the entry above from "a false record" to "a false record plus execution the operator's cancel did not stop".** Stated plainly, not softened: this is a more serious finding than the entry above, it was **probed rather than assumed**, and the "nothing executes" clause that entry used against critical is false outside its own trials. It is nevertheless held at **major** against this file's own rubric (`FINDINGS.md:6`, critical = data loss / silent corruption / security), with the reasons and the single measurement that would flip it given in the Notes.
- **Repro:** `test/edgecase/scenarios/w2-8-approval-race.md`, **Part F**. Stack = plain `test/ha`, no overlay, both agents up; workload `approval.payload.json` (`edge-approval`, `timeoutMinutes: 10`) so no timeout can interfere. Evidence: `w2-8/partF.py` (the aimer/firer), `w2-8/partF-control.py`, `w2-8/partF-capture.sh` — all three in repo under `test/edgecase/tools/w2/` as `w2-8-partF.py`, `w2-8-partF-control.py`, `w2-8-partF-capture.sh` — `w2-8/partF-gate.txt`, `w2-8/partF-F1.txt` + `w2-8/partF-F1-evidence.txt`, `w2-8/partF-F2b.txt` + `w2-8/partF-F2b-evidence.txt`, `w2-8/partF-F3.txt` + `w2-8/partF-F3-evidence.txt`, `w2-8/partF-F5wide.txt` + `w2-8/partF-F5wide-evidence.txt`, `w2-8/partF-Fctl.txt` + `w2-8/partF-Fctl-evidence.txt`, `w2-8/partF-teardown.txt`, `w2-8/partF-F1-ctrl-raw.txt`.
- **Observed:**
  - **The mechanism is a race between two agent-side pollers, and `WaitForApproval` checks the wrong one first.** Code-read, then confirmed live. The cancel poller runs on `CancelPollInterval = 5 * time.Second` (`internal/agent/orchestrator.go:37`), and only its `client.GetRun` tick sets `cancelledByMaster` and calls `cancelRun()` (`orchestrator.go:123-149`). `WaitForApproval` is called on `stepCtx` at `orchestrator.go:356` and its loop (`internal/agent/approval.go:51-73`) calls `GetApproval` **first** on the 3 s `ApprovalPollInterval` (`internal/agent/agent.go:27`) and returns **`true`** on `a.Status == "Approved"` at `:55-56` — *before* it ever reaches `case <-ctx.Done()` at `:69`. So if **any** 3 s approval tick falls between the decision's commit and the next 5 s cancel tick, the gate returns `true`. Nothing downstream stops it: `orchestrator.go:357-362` reports the gate `Succeeded` and returns `nil`; `RunPipeline` (`internal/agent/pipeline.go:126-157`) has **no** `ctx.Err()` check between stages; and the `if:` gate cannot skip the next step because `EvalCondition` computes `successVal = !status.Failed && !status.Cancelled` (`internal/dsl/condition.go:44`) from `cancelledByMaster` (`orchestrator.go:93-99`), which is still **`false`** — the cancel has not been observed. The step body then runs.
  - **Aiming method, because a race report without one is worthless.** For each attempt the firer reads **both live poll grids** out of the controller HTTP log for that run — the agent's approval grid is `GET /api/v1/agents/{id}/runs/{rid}/approvals/1` and its cancel grid is `GET /api/v1/runs/{rid}`, two different paths, so they separate cleanly — predicts them one period forward, and fires `cancel` then `approve` from a single Python process such that the approve commits ~0.45 s before the next predicted approval tick while the next cancel tick is still ≥3.4 s away (`w2-8/partF.py`). Accuracy: predicted-vs-actual approval tick was **+1.77 ms** (F1: predicted `05:18:48.779`, logged `05:18:48.777282`) and **−0.61 ms** (F3: predicted `05:27:56.596`, logged `05:27:56.596537`). The measured cancel→approve send gap was **119.6, 119.9, 120.0 ms** on F1/F2b/F3 and **2500.6 ms** on F5wide. (The script's host↔controller clock-offset probe returned **0 samples** — the `POST /api/v1/runs` log line does not carry the run id it creates, so the filter never matched — and the offset therefore defaulted to `0`. That is a harness wart, not a correction that was applied silently: the sub-2 ms predicted-vs-actual agreement above is the empirical check that host↔controller skew is negligible on this rig, consistent with the ~51 µs and ~10 ms bounds in the clock entry below.)
  - **The result table. 4/4 in-fence attempts produced post-gate execution; the one out-of-fence control produced none.** All timestamps on the **Postgres** clock except the two marked, so `decided` / `cancelled` / `output` are directly comparable:

    | attempt | run | agent | cancel→decide | post-gate output − cancel | step-2 output line | `step_reports` row for step 2 |
    |---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
    | F1 | `03f3bffd` | agent2 | **+119.591 ms** | **+564.082 ms** | `after-gate` @ `05:18:48.783082` | **none** |
    | F2b | `c709f204` | agent1 | **+120.090 ms** | **+591.711 ms** | `after-gate` @ `05:26:10.555851` | **none** |
    | F3 | `6e7bc851` | agent2 | **+119.944 ms** | **+582.107 ms** | `after-gate` @ `05:27:56.602151` | **none** |
    | F5wide | `1ea5c7a8` | agent2 | **+2.498859 s** | **+2.960459 s** | `after-gate` @ `05:31:51.974823` | **none** |
    | **Fctl** (control) | `1e843de8` | agent2 | **+8.001325 s** | — **no step-2 output at all** | — | **none** |

    Both agents reproduce it (F2b landed on agent1), so it is not a property of one container.
  - **F1 walked end to end on one controller-log timeline, because the ordering is the whole finding** (`w2-8/partF-F1-evidence.txt` §7/§8): `POST …/cancel` → **204** at `05:18:48.224159`; `POST …/approvals/1` → **204** at `05:18:48.341038`; the agent's next approval poll `GET /api/v1/agents/agent2/runs/03f3bffd…/approvals/1` → **200** at `05:18:48.777282` — this is the read that returned `Approved`; then three `POST /api/v1/agents/agent2/steps` at `48.778933`, `48.780977`, `48.789508` (gate `Succeeded`, `after` start, `after` `Succeeded`), **every one answered `200`, not `204`**; `POST …/steps/2/logs/bulk` → **204** at `48.787944`; `POST …/finish` → **200** at `48.792211`. Elapsed from the cancel to the run's last agent write: **568 ms**.
  - **`echo after-gate` really ran — this is not inferred from a status field.** The `logs` table holds `step_index=2, stream=stdout, line='after-gate'` at `05:18:48.783082`, i.e. **564.082 ms after the run's own `Cancelled` write** at `05:18:48.219` (single-statement comparison, `partF-F1-evidence.txt` §4 and §6). The step's stdout is the artifact; nothing else could have produced that row.
  - **The agent believed the run had `Succeeded`, and said so.** `cancelledByMaster` was never set and no failure was recorded, so `overallStatus = api.RunSucceeded` (`orchestrator.go:733-741`) and the agent posted `FinishRun(Succeeded)` — **code-derived, not captured off the wire**: the controller HTTP log records only `POST …/finish → 200`, never the request body, and the `alreadyFinalized` branch (`internal/controller/api_agent.go:613-621`) answers `200` for any of the three terminal statuses, so the specific status the agent sent is read from `orchestrator.go:733-741`'s logic, not observed directly. The controller answered **`200 {"alreadyFinalized":true}`** (`internal/controller/api_agent.go:614-621`) because `Cancelled` was already committed. **The agent's log contains no cancellation line whatsoever for these four runs** — only `{"msg":"running",…}` — because the poller's `cancelRun()` fired from the deferred cleanup at `orchestrator.go:117-120` after `RunClaim` had already finished the pipeline. The cancel was never observed by the agent at all.
  - **What kept the *record* clean is a controller guard on the report path, and it fences the write, not the work.** `handleAgentStepReport` looks the run up and, on `Succeeded`/`Failed`/`Cancelled`, returns `200` with `{"alreadyFinalized":true}` **without upserting** (`internal/controller/api_agent.go:513-521`) — which is exactly why all three post-cancel step reports logged `200` while the pre-cancel ones logged `204`. That guard is doing its documented job (its own comment: "Guard against writing stale step state under an already-terminal run", `:506-512`). It simply has no bearing on whether the step body executed, and the `logs/bulk` path has **no** equivalent guard — the log line was accepted with `204` and persisted, which is the one reason the execution is visible at all.
  - **Nothing else reconciles it.** `step_reports` for the four runs holds exactly two rows — step 0 `Succeeded`, step 1 `WaitingApproval` — permanently; `GET /runs/{id}/steps` synthesizes step 2 as `"status":"Pending"`; and the run's log pane for step 2 contains `after-gate`. Both readings were taken live (`partF-F1-evidence.txt` §3, and the final `/steps` body in `partF-F1.txt`).
  - **The control measures the fence rather than assuming it** (`w2-8/partF-Fctl-evidence.txt`). Same procedure, approve deliberately fired **8.008 s** after the cancel — outside `CancelPollInterval`. The agent's cancel poller detected the cancel at `05:29:50.781053` (agent-container clock), **3.861 s** after the cancel send at `05:29:46.919991` (host clock) — squarely inside the **0.939–4.938 s** fence W2-7 measured — and logged `"received cancellation signal from master; interrupting run"`. One step report followed at `05:29:50.782201` → `200` (the gate reported `Failed` after `WaitForApproval` returned `false` through `approval.go:69`). The approve at `+8.008 s` still returned **`204`** and still wrote `Approved`/`env:UNIFIED_TOKEN` — the entry above's defect, unchanged — but `logs` holds **only** the step-0 line: **no post-gate execution**. So the two sides of the boundary are both measured, and the discriminator is the fence, not the decision.
  - **Attempt count, stated either way as required.** **7 runs triggered.** **4 aimed inside the fence → 4 hits, 0 misses.** **1 control aimed outside it → 0 execution.** **2 aborted at the aiming stage before any cancel/approve pair was fired**, both for harness bugs and neither for a missed window: the first used `stepIndex` where the `/steps` API returns `index`, so it never detected `WaitingApproval`; the second read only one cancel-poll sample in its 9 s observation window and refused to aim (the window was widened to 13.5 s). **Both causes are prose-only** — no abort log or script-error capture exists for either attempt, so the `stepIndex`-vs-`index` mismatch and the 9 s observation window are stated, not evidenced by an artifact; what the census does corroborate, and what carries the load-bearing weight, is only that no approve was fired at either. Both were cancelled without an approve and appear in the census as `Cancelled|TimedOut|system` and `Cancelled|Pending` (`w2-8/partF-teardown.txt`). The final grouped census, **derived from the final 7-row census rather than read off a captured grouped query** (the grouped table actually captured in `partF-teardown.txt` reads `Cancelled|Approved|env:UNIFIED_TOKEN` **4**, because it was taken at `05:30:52`, before F5wide's run had completed), is `Cancelled|Approved|env:UNIFIED_TOKEN` **5** (the four hits plus the control), `Cancelled|Pending` **1**, `Cancelled|TimedOut|system` **1** — with `post_gate_output_lines` = **1** for each of the four hits and **0** for every other row, and `post_gate_step_rows` = **0** for all seven.
- **Expected:** **There IS a published promise here and it is contradicted — found by grepping for it rather than assumed absent, and quoted in full so the limb can be checked.** `grep -rn -i "cancel" docs/jobs.md docs/high-availability.md` returns one statement about what cancellation does to a run's steps, `docs/jobs.md:1775-1777` — inside the "Finally Block" (`finally`) section, heading `## Finally Block` at `docs/jobs.md:1746`, not under an approval or cancellation section:

  > "Both the standard and Kubernetes agents detect mid-run cancellation: an in-flight step is interrupted, `finally` still runs (with `failure()` false), and the run finishes as `Cancelled`."

  Three clauses, and they must be taken separately — W2-7 was corrected for citing a passage that *sanctioned* the behaviour it was cited against, so the direction of each clause is checked here rather than assumed.
  1. **"an in-flight step is interrupted" — contradicted.** The in-flight step when the cancel committed was the gate (step 1, `WaitingApproval`). It was not interrupted: it returned **success** and reported `Succeeded`. And a step that had not yet started — `after` — was then allowed to start *and run to completion* after the cancel, an outcome the sentence does not contemplate at all.
  2. **"the run finishes as `Cancelled`" — satisfied, but not by the agent.** The run is `Cancelled` only because the controller had already written it and refused the agent's `FinishRun(Succeeded)`. The agent's own verdict was `Succeeded`. So this clause holds on the stored value while being false about the mechanism the sentence describes.
  3. **"detect mid-run cancellation" — not bounded, so the control does not contradict it.** The passage sets **no** latency bound, which is why the control's 3.861 s detection is fully compliant and only the in-fence trials break anything. This is the qualifier that keeps the limb honest: the finding is not "detection is slow", it is "a step ran, and a further step started, inside the detection gap".
  4. **Not exercised: the `finally` clause.** `edge-approval` has no `finally` block, so nothing here bears on it. Stated so the passage is not treated as wholly tested.
  5. **A second, independent published-contract sentence is also contradicted, which strengthens rather than restates the finding.** `docs/jobs.md:1788` documents `success()` as true "when no step has failed and the run was not cancelled," and the implicit-`success()` rule at `docs/jobs.md:1791-1793` says an `if:` that does not mention a status function is "implicitly treated as requiring `success()`". `after` (`test/edgecase/workloads/approval.payload.json`) carries no `if:` at all, and the run **was** cancelled — so by this documented semantics `after` should have been **skipped**, not merely "not interrupted". `internal/dsl/condition.go:44` (cited above) explains the mechanism — `successVal` is computed from a `cancelledByMaster` that is still `false` when the gate evaluates — but that is the *cause*; this clause is the second *promise* the same observation breaks, alongside clause 1.

  Two other passages were checked and are **not** contradicted: `docs/jobs.md:1721` ("An **approval** allows the run to continue with the next step") describes exactly what happened and says nothing about a cancelled run; `docs/high-availability.md` bounds reap latency, not cancel propagation. And the campaign spec's own I6 says "the architecture has no hard fencing", so the *architecture* is behaving as specified — it is `docs/jobs.md:1775-1777` that over-promises what the polling mechanism can deliver.
- **Notes:**
  - **Severity reasoning, both halves, and the one measurement that would make it critical.** For **major, top of band**: the operator's stop button is not binding for up to `CancelPollInterval` (5 s) after it is pressed; the work that runs is arbitrary user code (here `echo`, in a real pipeline a deploy or a migration); it is reachable with two ordinary documented calls — or two clicks, since `RunDetail.svelte:1295-1315` still draws live Approve/Reject buttons on a `Cancelled` run — needing only `dev` rights, no fault injection, no credential theft; it reproduced **4/4** with cheap host-side aiming; and the execution leaves **no** `step_reports` row, so the run's own step view denies it happened. It also breaks a **published promise** rather than only an inferred one (`docs/jobs.md:1775-1777`, "an in-flight step is interrupted"), which under this file's classification rule (`FINDINGS.md:479`) is an independent basis for filing it as a violation and not merely an I6 measurement. There is also a plausible two-operator shape that needs no aiming skill at all: A cancels a gate that has been sitting in `WaitingApproval`, B approves it a second later without knowing, and B's decision defeats A's cancel — a waiting gate is precisely the object two people are looking at. Against **critical** by this file's rubric (`FINDINGS.md:6` — data loss / silent corruption / security): no data is lost; the run's recorded terminal state is not corrupted (it stays `Cancelled`, and `FinishRun(Succeeded)` is refused); no privilege boundary is crossed, since the principal who approves already held decide rights on that gate and could have approved without cancelling; and execution is **bounded** — `stepCtx` descends from `runCtx`, so `cancelRun()` interrupts anything still running within ≤5 s of the cancel. This matches the standard the W1-5 and W2-6 entries set (`FINDINGS.md:327`, `:1008`) for holding a wrong-behaviour finding at major when the run still settles on exactly one terminal state. **The measurement that would flip it to critical, and which was NOT taken:** put a *long* step after the gate (say 120 s) and check whether `cancelRun()` actually kills the running process or whether the step survives the way W1-5's zombie did (`FINDINGS.md:362` — 40.2 s of post-reap execution). If a long post-gate step is **not** interrupted, the exposure is unbounded rather than ≤5 s and this is critical. That is one cheap run and it is the highest-value follow-up in this entry. Note the asymmetry either way: an *interrupted* post-gate step is not a benign outcome — a half-applied deploy is arguably worse than a clean skip — but the interruption at least bounds it. **Three refinements for whoever runs that follow-up, so the answer is not narrower than it looks:** (a) this fixture is `native: true` (`test/edgecase/workloads/approval.payload.json`), and `docs/jobs.md:314-322` documents native `ucd-sh` background-job behavior as an in-process goroutine "bounded only by the step's own context (a step timeout or run cancellation eventually stops it)" — a container-exec step's kill path is a different mechanism entirely, so the follow-up must be run on **both** backends, or the answer only covers native and says nothing about container-exec. (b) The "execution is **bounded**" premise in the severity reasoning above (`stepCtx` descends from `runCtx`, so `cancelRun()` interrupts anything still running within ≤5 s) is **code-read, not measured**, and is labelled as such here, at its point of use, rather than left to be assumed as a measurement. (c) **The "plausible two-operator shape" claim below is derivable without a new run, not merely plausible.** Modelling the two agent pollers' phases as independent uniforms — next approval-poll tick `A ~ Uniform(0, ApprovalPollInterval=3s)`, next cancel-poll tick `C ~ Uniform(0, CancelPollInterval=5s)` — the probability that an unaimed approve landing shortly after an unaimed cancel still lands inside the fence is `P(A<C) = ∫₀³ (5−a)/5 · 1/3 da = 0.7`, i.e. **exactly 70%** under this uniform-phase model. Stated as **derived**, not merely asserted, because it removes "needs aiming skill" as an anti-severity argument: A cancels a waiting gate, B approves it a second later without knowing, and B's approve defeats A's cancel on roughly seven trials in ten with no timing skill involved on either side.
  - **One fix closes both halves of W2-8, and it is the one the entry above already recommends.** Adding the run-status conjunct to `DecideApproval`'s `UPDATE` (`internal/store/postgres.go:2439-2450`) — `AND EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM runs WHERE id = $1 AND status NOT IN ('Succeeded','Failed','Cancelled'))` — means the decision never commits on a `Cancelled` run, so `GetApproval` never returns `Approved`, so the gate never returns `true` and the step never starts. The false record and the unfenced execution have the same root cause and the same one-line remedy; nothing agent-side is strictly required. Two defences in depth are still worth having, because they close the general shape rather than this one path: (a) `WaitForApproval` should check `ctx.Err()` **before** honouring an `Approved` read (`internal/agent/approval.go:55` — today `ctx.Done()` is only consulted after the poll, at `:69`), and (b) `RunPipeline` should bail between stages on `ctx.Err()` (`internal/agent/pipeline.go:126`), which today it never checks — that second one bounds *every* cancel-versus-next-step race, not just the approval one, and is the more valuable of the two. **Tally treatment, stated explicitly per the campaign's precedent for entries that could be double-counted (`FINDINGS.md:1184`): this entry is additive to the entry above, not a restatement of it.** `approval.go:55` (honouring an `Approved` read before `ctx.Err()`) and `pipeline.go:126` (no `ctx.Err()` check between stages) are agent-side ordering defects that exist **independently** of `DecideApproval`'s missing run-status guard — they would still race a cancel against the next step even on a controller where that guard were already fixed — and they bound every cancel-versus-next-step race, not only this approval path. The two entries therefore count as **2 violations**, matching the plan (`plan:306`) and the runbook (`:726`), not 1. *(An earlier version cited `plan:116`, which is Task 1's `bulk-submit.sh` step; corrected at the W2 checkpoint.)*
  - **This is the entry the review of Task 9 asked for, and it changes two claims in the entry above rather than sitting beside them.** Both have been edited in place, not left to be noticed: the invariant line's blanket "NOT I6 … nothing keeps executing after the terminal write" and the severity note's "Against critical: nothing executes" are now scoped to that entry's own trials, with a pointer here. The reason the original claims were made in good faith is worth recording, because it is a general trap: **every** trial in the first entry fired with the agent already gone (the timeout path — the agent had exited before the POST) or already past its fence (Part D, +9.19 s). Neither shape can execute, so "nothing executes" was true of everything measured and false as a general property. **The methodological rule for later waves: when an entry's anti-severity argument rests on a negative ("nothing X happens"), check whether every trial shares a feature that guarantees the negative. If it does, the negative is a property of the aim, not of the system.**
  - **Sampler hygiene, captured this time instead of asserted** (`w2-8/partF-teardown.txt`). The prior W2-8 run claimed in prose that no sampler was left running without capturing it. Part F captured it twice — before the instrument revert and again immediately before `down -v`: `jobs` printed nothing (empty job table) and `ps -W | grep -iE "psql|curl|python"` returned no match on both passes. Every sampler in this scenario, Part F included, is a bounded foreground loop inside a single script, which is *why* the claim holds; the capture is what makes it evidence.
  - **Instrument.** `log_statement='all'` / `log_line_prefix='%m [%p] h=%h '` were armed with **one `ALTER SYSTEM` per `psql -c`** and verified `all` in a **fresh** session at `05:12:47`, then `ALTER SYSTEM RESET` and verified **`none`** in a fresh session at `05:30:52` and again at `05:33:07` (`partF-gate.txt`, `partF-teardown.txt`); stack torn down with `down -v`. One attempt (F5wide) deliberately ran **after** the revert and is recorded as such — it needed only the controller HTTP log and DB reads, never the statement log, so no number in this entry rests on an instrument that was not armed at the time it was taken.

## W2-8 — observation: the decision and the reaper are two unsynchronised writers CAS-ing one row, and whichever `UPDATE` reaches Postgres first wins — 9 phase-locked attempts, 3 human wins / 6 reaper wins, with the human's loss reported as `409 "already decided"` when nobody decided anything
- **Invariant:** none violated. **I7** (`docs/superpowers/specs/2026-07-29-edge-case-testing-design.md:54`) is the invariant in the neighbourhood and it is **not** breached by the reaper's win — a row that ends `TimedOut`/`system` on a `Failed` run is the correct record. The risk this entry records is the *message the loser receives*, not the stored state. Filed as an observation because both statements are individually correct and the interleaving is as-designed; a lost race is not inflated into a violation here.
- **Severity:** minor (observation)
- **Repro:** `test/edgecase/scenarios/w2-8-approval-race.md`, Part C. Evidence: `w2-8/partC-grid-pre.txt`, `w2-8/partC-attempts.txt`, `w2-8/partC-stmts.txt`, `w2-8/partC-stmts-late.txt`, `w2-8/pglog-approval-updates-raw.txt`, `w2-8/grid2.awk`, `w2-8/fire.py`, `w2-8/partC2.sh` (the three drivers in repo under `test/edgecase/tools/w2/` as `w2-8-grid2.awk`, `w2-8-fire.py`, `w2-8-partC2.sh`).
- **Observed:**
  - **The sweep grid, measured not assumed.** With `log_statement='all'` armed, the reaper's arg-less `UPDATE run_approvals SET status='TimedOut' …` is logged on **every** winning tick whether or not it matches a row, so the grid is visible on an idle stack. Over `03:53:05 → 04:04:05` (`w2-8/partC-grid-pre.txt`): **12 clusters, 30 executions**, cluster size 2 or 3 (**2.50 per nominal interval**, inside the 2.15–2.58 range W2-1/W2-3/W2-4 measured for the other per-tick jobs), within-cluster spread **8–19 ms**, between-cluster period **60.003–60.019 s** (mean 60.012, drift ≈ **+12 ms/min**). Same shape as the 30 s jobs: a burst of 2–3 within ~10 ms then a full interval of silence — so **query load scales at `interval / N` while worst-case latency stays at the nominal 60 s**, per the corrected rule at `docs/superpowers/plans/2026-07-30-edge-case-campaign-w2.md:47`.
  - **Aiming method and its accuracy, because a race report is worthless without them.** Attempts C1–C3 used a shell busy-wait and landed **+61 to +129 ms** past the sweep: those are **missed aims, not lost races**, and are reported as such. The rewrite (`w2-8/partC2.sh` + `w2-8/fire.py`) predicts the sweep instant from the cluster one period earlier and fires from a single Python process; measured self-test overshoot was **0.33 / 0.76 / 0.94 ms**, and prediction error against the actual first reaper statement was **−1 to +10 ms** across the nine attempts. The dominant term is neither: the POST's own client-to-`UPDATE` latency measured **49.9–64.3 ms** (mean ≈ 59.8 ms, derived per attempt as `DECIDE statement ts − t_send`), which is why an aim of −10 ms cannot win.
  - **The outcome table, on a single Postgres clock** — `Δ = DECIDE statement − first REAPER statement of that cluster`, both read from the Postgres statement log so no cross-clock correction is needed (`w2-8/partC-stmts.txt`, `w2-8/partC-stmts-late.txt`):

    | attempt | aim | DECIDE stmt | first REAPER stmt | Δ | HTTP | row after |
    |---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
    | C5 | −300 ms | 04:16:05.109 | 04:16:05.359 | **−250 ms** | 204 | `Approved` / `env:UNIFIED_TOKEN` |
    | C8 | −100 ms | 04:22:05.401 | 04:22:05.442 | **−41 ms** | 204 | `Approved` / `env:UNIFIED_TOKEN` |
    | C6 | −40 ms | 04:18:05.393 | 04:18:05.393 | **≈0 ms** | 204 | `Approved` / `env:UNIFIED_TOKEN` |
    | C13 | −55 ms | 04:33:05.586 | 04:33:05.579 | **+7 ms** | 409 | `TimedOut` / `system` |
    | C12 | −40 ms | 04:31:05.581 | 04:31:05.562 | **+19 ms** | 409 | `TimedOut` / `system` |
    | C11 | −25 ms | 04:29:05.574 | 04:29:05.533 | **+41 ms** | 409 | `TimedOut` / `system` |
    | C7 | −10 ms | 04:20:05.462 | 04:20:05.415 | **+47 ms** | 409 | `TimedOut` / `system` |
    | C9 | 0 ms | 04:24:05.524 | 04:24:05.460 | **+64 ms** | 409 | `TimedOut` / `system` |
    | C10 | +40 ms | 04:26:05.586 | 04:26:05.490 | **+96 ms** | 409 | `TimedOut` / `system` |

    **The rule holds in 9/9 with no exception: the earlier statement wins, and the transition sits inside a 7 ms band** — tightest human win `Δ ≈ 0 ms` (same logged millisecond), tightest reaper win `Δ = +7 ms`. Exactly one writer ever changed the row; no attempt produced a double write or a lost update.
  - **The distribution, with its sample size stated.** **9 phase-locked attempts: 3 human wins, 6 reaper wins, 0 ambiguous.** Do not read 3/9 as a probability — the split is a function of the aim offsets, which were deliberately swept rather than sampled. In *aim* space the outcome is **not monotonic**: −40 ms won once (C6) and lost once (C12), and −55 ms lost (C13) while −40 ms won, because the ~50–64 ms client latency jitters by ~14 ms — more than the whole transition band. Separately, **3 mis-aimed attempts (C1–C3, +61…+129 ms late in aim space) all went to the reaper**, and **1 attempt (C4) issued no POST at all** (the firer aborted on a path bug) and is excluded from the tally; its row was reaped normally at `04:14:05.325888`.
  - **The reaper's win is independently attributable, three ways.** The controller log line `"approval reaper: marked timed-out approvals" count=1` (`internal/controller/approval_reaper.go:56`, guarded by `if n > 0` so it fires only on a tick that actually reaped) was captured for C1/C2/C3/C7/C9/C11; `decided_by='system'` on the row; and the statement itself in the Postgres log. The `count=1` values also confirm the reaper is otherwise idle per tick.
- **Expected:** as-designed. `DecideApproval` (`postgres.go:2439-2450`) and `MarkExpiredApprovalsTimedOut` (`postgres.go:2473-2484`) are each a single autocommit `UPDATE` guarded on `status='Pending'`, so Postgres row locking makes exactly one of them match and neither can corrupt the row. (Quote the statement, not the plan's summary: the reaper's guard has a third conjunct, `timeout_at IS NOT NULL`, which `plan:81` elides.) **The risk is the loser's message.** `handleDecideApproval:44-52` maps `changed == false` to **`409 "already decided"`** whenever `GetApproval` still finds a row — the `404` branch requires the row to be *absent*. So an operator whose approve lost to the reaper is told a decision was already made when **nobody decided anything**: the gate expired and a background job stamped it. Measured body text, six attempts: `already decided`. That message is also the one a *correct* fix for the major above would most naturally reuse, which is why it is recorded here.
- **Notes:**
  - **Consequence for later waves that need to race a per-tick job:** the approval reaper's grid is the cheapest phase-lock target in the system, because its `UPDATE` executes unconditionally on every winning tick — unlike `ListStuckRunIDs` (W2-3) or the queued-run predicate (W2-4), no work has to exist for the grid to be visible. Measured: period 60.012 s, drift +12 ms/min, cluster spread 8–19 ms, `(epoch mod 60)` phase ≈ 5.1–5.6 s over the session.
  - **And the limit of host-side aiming is the request pipeline, not the clock.** Sub-millisecond firing accuracy plus ±10 ms grid prediction still could not reliably win at aims inside −55 ms, because host→`UPDATE` latency is 50–64 ms with ~14 ms of jitter. A later scenario needing a decisive win against a per-tick job should aim at **≥ −100 ms** (3/3 wins at −100 and −300 ms here) or move the client inside the compose network.
  - **Instrument note.** `DecideApproval` is a *parameterised* query and Postgres therefore logs it as `LOG: execute stmtcache_<hash>: …`, not `LOG: statement: …`, while the reaper's arg-less query logs as `statement:`. An extractor that only matches `statement:` sees the reaper and silently misses every decision — the first version of this scenario's parser did exactly that. `w2-8/grid2.awk` handles both forms and reads the `DETAIL: parameters: $1 = '<run id>'` line for attribution.

## W2-8 — observation: the two approval clocks do not measurably differ; the agent's expiry detection is nonetheless **always later** than the controller's `timeout_at`, by 6.6–19.5 ms over 16 runs, and that lateness is code ordering plus ticker drift, not skew — settles the plan's open question at `plan:92`
- **Invariant:** none. This is the measurement the plan asked for (`docs/superpowers/plans/2026-07-30-edge-case-campaign-w2.md:92`, "Direction and magnitude of clock skew between the agent's `WaitForApproval` deadline and the controller's `timeout_at`"), recorded so no later wave re-derives it. No invariant is engaged and nothing here is a defect.
- **Severity:** minor (observation)
- **Repro:** `test/edgecase/scenarios/w2-8-approval-race.md`, Part B, over all 16 `edge-approval-short` runs of the scenario. Evidence: `w2-8/gate-clocks.txt`, `w2-8/gate-clocks-bracketed.txt`, `w2-8/partB-clocks.txt`, `w2-8/partB2-and-operator-surface.txt`, `w2-8/partB-controller-http.txt`, `w2-8/gate-g5-throwaway.txt`.
- **Observed:**
  - **The observable difference — measured, 16/16 positive.** `t(agent "approval timed out" log line, agent-container clock) − timeout_at (controller clock)`: **+6.642, +6.965, +8.669, +8.789, +9.297, +12.302, +13.190, +14.009, +14.185, +14.956, +15.251, +15.306, +15.772, +16.747, +17.612, +19.487 ms** — mean **+13.07 ms** (derived), median ≈ +14.1 ms, range **+6.642 … +19.487 ms**. Two of the sixteen ran on `agent2` (+9.297, +13.190 ms) and sit inside the `agent1` spread, so the result is not a property of one container. Raw inputs: per-run `timeout_at` from the DB and per-run log lines from `docker compose logs agent1 agent2`, both in `w2-8/partB-clocks.txt`.
  - **The direction is structural, not empirical luck.** `approval.go:33-38` calls `CreateApproval` (where the controller evaluates `timeout_at` at `api_approvals.go:86-89`) and then `ReportStep`, and only then computes `deadline` at `:48`. The agent's deadline is therefore later than `timeout_at` by two HTTP round trips *by construction*, whatever the clocks do.
  - **Skew is below the resolution obtainable, bounded by two same-row brackets rather than asserted.** Each bracket pairs two timestamps whose real-time order is known, so the observable equals `elapsed + skew` with `elapsed > 0`:
    - **controller ↔ Postgres:** `(timeout_at − run_approvals.created_at) − 30 s` = `ctrl_now(:87) − pg_now(INSERT)` = **−51 µs to −525 µs** across 16 rows (`w2-8/partB-clocks.txt`; the tightest row is `a9b519eb` at `−00:00:00.000051`, the widest `2ccb87ac` at `−00:00:00.000525`). The INSERT necessarily follows the `:87` evaluation, so the binding bound comes from the *tightest* row: `skew(pg − ctrl) < +51 µs` — the two agree to within ~51 **microseconds**, expected for two containers on one kernel clock and now measured. (An earlier version of this entry quoted `±54 µs`, taking the second-tightest row and stating a two-sided bound the bracket does not support: the bracket is one-sided.)
    - **agent ↔ Postgres, both directions:** `step_reports[0].started_at` (agent clock) − `runs.claimed_at` (pg clock) = **+4.689 … +10.108 ms**, and the claim precedes step 0, so `skew(agent − pg) < +4.689 ms`. Conversely `run_approvals.created_at` (pg) − `step_reports[0].ended_at` (agent) = **+3.535 … +7.822 ms**, and step 0 ends before the approval INSERT, so `skew(agent − pg) > −3.535 ms`. Hence **`skew(agent − pg) ∈ (−3.54, +4.69) ms`**, sign indeterminate, consistent with zero.
    - Combining the two: **`skew(agent − controller)` is bounded to roughly `(−3.6, +4.8) ms`** (derived) — **and this needs one stated assumption, because the two brackets are not symmetric.** `skew(agent − ctrl) = skew(agent − pg) + skew(pg − ctrl)`; the agent↔pg bracket is two-sided, but the controller↔Postgres bracket is one-sided (`skew(pg − ctrl) < +51 µs`), which alone yields only the **upper** limit `< +4.689 + 0.051 ≈ +4.74 ms`. The lower limit `> −3.6 ms` therefore holds **only under the assumption `|skew(pg − ctrl)| ≲ 51 µs`** — i.e. that the two containers' offset is symmetric in magnitude around the measured one-sided bound, which the 16 rows make plausible (all 16 sit in a 474 µs band with no drift across the 38-minute session) but do not prove. Nothing in this entry's conclusions depends on the lower side: the point is that the magnitude is about three orders of magnitude below the 3 s `ApprovalPollInterval`, and smaller than the observable difference's other terms. The coarse instrument agrees but cannot do better: a bracketed `docker exec date` census put agent1, agent2 and Postgres within **~10 ms** of one another with a **±280 ms** per-sample error bar dominated by `exec` startup (`w2-8/gate-clocks-bracketed.txt`), and the controller image's `date` does not support `%N` at all (`w2-8/gate-clocks.txt`) — which is why the row-internal brackets are the instrument that settles this.
  - **The poll-granularity term is ~0 for this fixture, and that is a general rule worth carrying.** `w2-8/partB-controller-http.txt` shows the agent's `GetApproval` polls for run `2ccb87ac` at `…45.681, 48.682, 51.682, 54.682, 57.681, 00.690, 03.689, 06.689, 09.689, 12.690, 15.690` — eleven polls, the eleventh landing **on** the deadline. `approval.go:49-50` starts the 3 s ticker immediately after computing `deadline = now + 30 s`, so with 30 s an exact multiple of the 3 s interval the last tick coincides with the deadline and contributes ~0 instead of `U(0, 3 s)`. **The rule:** the granularity term vanishes iff the truncated whole-second timeout (`time.Duration(timeoutMin*60)`, `approval.go:48`) is a multiple of 3 — true for every whole-minute value and for `0.5`, false for e.g. `0.52` (→ 31 s), which would add up to ~2 s. This is why all 16 observables are tens of milliseconds rather than seconds.
  - **Residual decomposition (derived, not a second measurement).** Of the mean +13.07 ms: the code gap `agent_now(:48) − ctrl_now(:87)` is bounded below by `step_reports[1].started_at − run_approvals.created_at` = **+1.16 … +1.81 ms** (agent `:44` against the pg INSERT) plus the `ReportStep` round trip, and the remainder (~5–12 ms) is Go ticker lateness accumulated over ten ticks — the poll grid above drifts from `…45.681` to `…15.690`, i.e. **+9 ms over 30 s**. No term requires a non-zero skew to explain the data.
- **Expected:** consistent with the code and with `test/edgecase/README.md` § "Fractional `timeoutMinutes`", which states that `timeout_at` and the agent deadline "agree" for `0.5`. That is right to sub-second precision; this entry adds the sign and the millisecond magnitude of the residual disagreement, and the reason it can only be positive.
- **Notes:**
  - **Consequence, small but real.** Because `timeout_at` precedes the agent's detection by 6.6–19.5 ms, a reaper sweep landing inside that window would mark the row `TimedOut` *before* the agent fails the step. Derived likelihood on this rig: ≈ 13 ms / 60.012 s ≈ **2.2 × 10⁻⁴ per gate**. It was never observed here (0 of 16), and the outcome would be benign — the agent's next `GetApproval` poll sees `TimedOut` and returns false through the same branch (`approval.go:57-59`) — but a later wave should not treat "the agent always notices first" as an invariant.
  - **What was NOT established:** the controller container's clock was never read directly (no `%N` in that image, no clock-exposing endpoint), so every controller-clock statement here is inferred from `timeout_at` and the `run_approvals` row rather than from an independent read of that container. A tighter bound would need the controller to emit a timestamp pair, which is a product change and out of scope (spec §8).

## W2-9 — the scheduler only ever examines the **50 oldest** `Pending` runs, so a fully runnable run that declares no concurrency at all is never *considered*, not merely deferred — and the same 50-row ceiling makes `docs/high-availability.md:163`'s post-promotion promise false above 50: 252.639 s and 787.615 s of starvation against a 0.185 s baseline, 127 consecutive ticks whose candidate set was the identical 50 rows, an agent long-polling 273 times for work, a first post-promotion tick that processed 50 of 58, and **not one log line anywhere**
- **ONE defect, two doors — and it must be counted ONCE.** The root cause is a single hardcoded `limit = 50` at `internal/controller/scheduler.go:58`, reached here two ways: by ordinary `POST /api/v1/runs` traffic (Parts A/B, no fault injected) and by a SIGKILL of the scheduler leader (Part D). This was drafted as **two** major entries and is deliberately filed as **one**, because neither door carries the finding standing alone. Parts A/B breach no invariant and no documented contract (`docs/jobs.md` is silent, `docs/operations.md:53` is scoped away — see **Expected**), so alone they would fall to an observation, which is the wrong outcome for a real denial of service. Part D alone is one extra tick per 50 runs (~200 ms, bounded) on a *queueable* backlog that this session never exercised — a docs gap, i.e. **minor** by `FINDINGS.md:6-8`. Merged, the entry rests on a contradicted published contract (`docs/high-availability.md:163`) **plus** I5, and it honestly earns major: a runnable run denied service for 787.6 s with an idle agent long-polling 273 times, no reaper, and no log line. **For the W2 checkpoint tally: W2-9 contributes 1 major + 1 minor (observation), NOT 2 majors + 1 minor. `scheduler.go:58` is one defect and must not be double-counted.**
- **Invariant:** **I5 (bounded recovery)** — "after fault injection the system returns to steady state within documented bounds (leader re-election ≤ seconds; stuck-run reap ≤ staleAfter 90s + interval 30s; **the bounds in `docs/high-availability.md` are the contract**)" (`docs/superpowers/specs/2026-07-29-edge-case-testing-design.md:52`) — **claimed on the Part D limb only, where both of I5's gates are actually satisfied**: there **is** a real fault injection (SIGKILL of the scheduler leader) and the broken sentence **is** in `docs/high-availability.md`, the document I5's own text names as the contract. **The published-contract limb is the primary one; I5 is the invariant that makes that document binding on this campaign.**
  - **On the no-fault limb (Parts A/B), I5 is an explicit NULL RESULT and is not claimed.** That setup injects no fault — the state arises from ordinary `POST /api/v1/runs` traffic — and `docs/high-availability.md` carries no Pending→Queued latency bound and no queue-depth bound (grep it: §"Orphaned-Run Recovery" bounds reaping, §"Horizontal scaling" bounds agent loss). Recovery there is also *demonstrably* bounded once the backlog drops below the threshold (Part B: **0.196 s** from the 50th slot opening to the enqueue). Neither gate holds, so the honest report on that limb is a null result. Keeping the two-gate distinction visible is the point of this bullet: Part D has both gates, Parts A/B have neither.
  - **I1 is NOT claimed, and an earlier draft of this entry that claimed it was WRONG.** I1 is "every API-accepted run reaches exactly one terminal state; no phantom runs from duplicate fires/webhooks" (`…design.md:48`). The draft read "exactly one" as a **lower** bound and asserted the starved probe was in **zero** terminal states. That fails on this entry's own evidence: **both probes did reach exactly one terminal state** — probe 1 `Succeeded` at `06:27:52.866707` and probe 2 `Succeeded` at `06:43:35.688398` (`w2-9/partB4-outcome.txt`). It also proves far too much: I1 contains **no liveness bound**, so "in zero terminal states right now" is true of *every* non-terminal run at *every* instant, and the reading would make I1 violated by every `Pending` row in the system. The only version of the claim that would bite is "never reaches one" — which is exactly what was **not** measured, because both windows were deliberately ended (one by cancelling, one by the backlog draining). This is the same over-stretch the campaign has already rejected twice: W2-5's I3 relabel and W2-8's "at most once does not forbid zero". The `I1, I5` attribution came from the scenario brief (`docs/superpowers/plans/2026-07-30-edge-case-campaign-w2.md:314`, since corrected), so this is a **labelling correction only — no number in this entry changes.**
  - **NOT I3** (`:50`, "mutex/semaphore/concurrency slots are released when the holder reaches a terminal state"). Eight successive holders acquired and released `edge-mutex` cleanly across the session and `mutex_holders` was empty at teardown; the mutex is only the *cause* of the backlog, not a leak. W2-5 was corrected for exactly this relabelling.
  - **NOT I7.** Every surface told the truth: the run really was `Pending`. That is the point — see the sibling observation below.
- **Severity:** major — **one** major for the whole scenario (see the first bullet)
- **Repro:** `test/edgecase/scenarios/w2-9-head-of-line.md`, Parts A, B and D (Part D was added during execution; the runbook as committed did not contain it). Evidence: `w2-9/gate.txt`, `w2-9/gate-g6-probe-baseline.txt`, `w2-9/partA-hog.txt`, `w2-9/partA-bulk-timing.txt`, `w2-9/partA-census-pre.txt`, `w2-9/partA-probe-trigger.txt`, `w2-9/partA-probe-poll.txt`, `w2-9/partA-running.txt`, `w2-9/partA-agent1-idle.txt`, `w2-9/partA-claim-log.txt`, `w2-9/partA-pglog-raw.txt` (7.9 MB), `w2-9/partA-pglog-analysis.txt`, `w2-9/partA-pglog-ids.txt`, `w2-9/partA-pglog-probe-context.txt`, `w2-9/partB-cancel-series.txt`, `w2-9/partB-pglog-raw.txt` (9.6 MB), `w2-9/partB-tick-candidates.txt`, `w2-9/partB-probe-outcome.txt`, `w2-9/partB-probe2.txt`, `w2-9/partB4-natural-drain.txt`, `w2-9/partB4-outcome.txt`, `w2-9/partD-promotion.txt`, `w2-9/partD-pglog-raw.txt` (12.8 MB), `w2-9/partD-tick-candidates.txt`, `w2-9/partD-analysis.txt`, `w2-9/partD-disarm.txt`, `w2-9/ipmap.txt`, `w2-9/g5-round1-partial.txt`, `w2-9/g5-final.txt`, `w2-9/derived-numbers.txt`, `w2-9/partA.sh`, `w2-9/partB.sh`, `w2-9/partD.sh` (the three drivers in repo under `test/edgecase/tools/w2/` as `w2-9-partA.sh`, `w2-9-partB.sh`, `w2-9-partD.sh`).
- **Observed:**
  - **The mechanism is one phase earlier than the campaign spec's original W2-9 row said, and that reframing is what makes it measurable.** Claiming is two phases. Phase 1 `TransitionPendingToQueued` (`internal/store/postgres.go:437-475`) runs `SELECT id, spec, params FROM runs WHERE status = 'Pending' ORDER BY created_at LIMIT $1` (`:440`) with **`limit = 50` hardcoded at its single call site**, `internal/controller/scheduler.go:58`. Phase 2 `claimNextRun` (`:679-724`) runs over `r.status = 'Queued'` (`:695`). A mutex-blocked run **stays `Pending`**: `tryQueueRun` (`:482`) `BEGIN`s, `SELECT status … FOR UPDATE`s, attempts `INSERT INTO mutex_holders` (`:546-549`), returns `false, nil` on the unique violation (`:551-553`) and the `defer tx.Rollback(ctx)` (`:487`) discards everything — **status never written, nothing logged, nothing emitted**. So blocked runs never enter phase 2's candidate set and head-of-line blocking **cannot** occur in the claim query. It occurs in the phase-1 snapshot, and the consequence is stronger than "queued behind": a run at `created_at` position 51+ is **not examined at all**.
  - **The snapshot's saturation, measured directly rather than argued.** With `log_statement='all'` armed for a 25.200 s window (`06:25:01.083 → 06:25:26.283`, `w2-9/partA-pglog-raw.txt`, 74,951 lines) while **56** runs were `Pending`:
    - **127** phase-1 snapshot statements, all from **one** host (`h=172.20.0.3` = controller2, the single scheduler leader per `w2-9/ipmap.txt`) — mean interval **200.0 ms** (derived: 25.200 s / 126), matching the 200 ms `RunScheduler` ticker.
    - **6,350** `SELECT status FROM runs WHERE id = $1 FOR UPDATE` calls = **127 × 50 exactly**. Per-tick candidate counts: **127 ticks, all 50** (`w2-9/derived-numbers.txt`) — not one tick ever looked at 51.
    - The **50 distinct** candidate ids over all 127 ticks are **set-identical** to the 50 oldest `Pending` rows from the pre-probe census (`diff` empty, `w2-9/partA-pglog-analysis.txt`). Positions 51-55 were seen **0** times each; the probe was seen **0** times.
    - The probe id appears 18 times in the whole 7.9 MB capture and **every one is the harness's own poll traffic** — the controller's `GetRun` and `GetRunParent` reads serving `GET /api/v1/runs/{id}` (`w2-9/partA-pglog-probe-hits.txt`, context at `w2-9/partA-pglog-probe-context.txt`). Never a `FOR UPDATE`.
  - **The probe, and the baseline that makes "never claimed" mean something.** All three `edge-unrelated-probe` runs, one read (`w2-9/partB4-outcome.txt`):

    | run | context | `claimed_at − created_at` | `updated_at − created_at` |
    |---|---|---|---|
    | `84d11432` | §G6, idle stack | **0.185024 s** | 0.217939 s |
    | `bda9358f` (probe 1) | 56 `Pending`, ended by cancels | **252.638999 s** | 252.663186 s |
    | `55429883` (probe 2) | 56 `Pending`, ended by natural drain | **787.614598 s** | 787.629717 s |

    i.e. **1,365×** and **4,256×** the unobstructed claim latency (derived). Both probes then ran normally, wrote `probe-ran`, and reached exactly one terminal state (`Succeeded` at `06:27:52.866707` and `06:43:35.688398`) — the fixture is not broken, it was never asked. Probe 1's poll series is **36/36 samples `Pending`** over `06:23:45 → 06:26:26`, API and DB agreeing on every sample, with `Pending=56`, `Queued=0`, `Running=1` throughout (`w2-9/partA-probe-poll.txt`).
  - **An agent was idle and asking the whole time.** The only `Running` row during probe 1's window was the hog on `agent2` (`w2-9/partA-running.txt`). `agent1` issued **273** `POST /api/v1/agents/agent1/claim` long-polls in the 8-minute window, each `200` after ~30,030 ms — the empty-claim response returned when the long poll times out (`writeJSON(w, http.StatusOK, api.ClaimResponse{})` at `internal/controller/api_agent.go:199`, reached from the `if time.Now().After(deadline) || r.Context().Err() != nil` check at `:198`). **Pinpoint caveat for anyone re-checking this:** the byte-identical statement at `api_agent.go:159` is a *different* branch — the shutdown drain, `case <-s.claimDrainCh:` — and an earlier draft of this entry cited `:159` by mistake; `:199` is the one these 273 responses came from. `agent1`'s container log contains **exactly one line for the whole window**, the `"running"` for the probe at `06:27:52.848971` (`w2-9/partA-agent1-idle.txt`, `w2-9/partA-claim-log.txt`). Work was available, an executor was asking for it 273 times, and the two were never introduced. **Capture caveat:** `w2-9/partA-claim-log.txt`'s own header line reads "during 06:23:40-06:26:30", but the 273 figure is the file's own "count of agent1 claim polls in the whole 8m window" and its sample rows start at `06:21:50.407`; the **8-minute window stated here is what matches the data** and the capture's header is the thing that is wrong. Do not re-derive the count from the header's range.
  - **The silence, quantified.** `"scheduler enqueued"` (`scheduler.go:66-68`, logged only when `n > 0`) fired at `06:23:30.066456` for the hog and then **not again until `06:27:52.581607`** — a **262.515 s** gap (derived) with 56 runs `Pending`. No other line, on any controller or agent, mentions the condition. Blocked runs carried **0** `logs` rows and **0** `step_reports` rows (`w2-9/g5-final.txt`) — unlike the queued-run reaper, which at least appends its reason (W2-4).
  - **THE THRESHOLD — measured twice, by two different methods, and it is 50.**
    - **Round 1, directly measured from the snapshot itself.** Cancelling the oldest blocked runs one at a time (`w2-9/partB-cancel-series.txt`, `Pending` 55→54→53→52→51 with the probe `Pending` at every step), then reading the armed statement log across the transition (`w2-9/partB-tick-candidates.txt`): the ticks at `06:27:51.714 / .914 / 06:27:52.114 / .314` each returned **50 candidates without the probe**; the tick at **`06:27:52.514` returned 50 candidates and the probe was one of them** — its first and only appearance in the entire capture; every subsequent tick returned **49** (96 ticks). So the snapshot admitted the probe on the first tick at which the total `Pending` count was **50** — measured, not inferred, but state the instrument exactly: the count is the per-candidate `SELECT status FROM runs WHERE id = $1 FOR UPDATE` tally used as a **1:1 proxy** for the snapshot's row count, and the proxy is verified from `internal/store/postgres.go:482-489` — that `FOR UPDATE` is `tryQueueRun`'s first statement after `BEGIN`, with no earlier return path, so exactly one is issued per snapshot row. (This is the same instrument named explicitly in the Part D bullet below.) Enqueue at `06:27:52.581607`, claimed by the idle `agent1` at `06:27:52.842520`, `Succeeded` at `06:27:52.866707`.
    - **Round 2, cancel-free, independent, same answer.** A second probe was starved from `Pending=56` and left to drain naturally. Two **adjacent** `"scheduler enqueued"` lines settle it: `06:43:35.290938839` (the `edge-sideeffect` run that took the freed mutex, dropping `Pending` 51→50) and `06:43:35.486771268` (**probe 2**) — **195.83 ms apart, exactly one tick** (`w2-9/partB4-outcome.txt`), bracketed by census samples `Pending=51` at `06:43:27.748` and `Pending=49` at `06:43:36.407` (`w2-9/partB4-natural-drain.txt`). The count at admission is therefore **50** here too; this round's figure is **derived** from the adjacent-enqueue pair plus the bracket (the 8.6 s sampler grid straddled both drops), whereas round 1's is direct.
    - **The 50 is the code's `limit`, not a coincidence, and the model that predicted it survived contact.** `scheduler.go:58` + `postgres.go:440`, confirmed against the running image by 188 measured ticks across three windows in which the candidate count was **only ever 50 or 49** and never 51.
  - **And the starvation is a property of queue depth, not of any one lock holder.** Probe 2 sat `Pending` across **seven** successive `edge-mutex` holders — the 600 s hog (`06:23:30.492 → 06:33:30.569`) then six `edge-sideeffect` runs of ~120 s each, each acquiring within **~1 s** of its predecessor's release (largest gap `06:35:31.700684 → 06:35:32.707` = **1.006 s**, so the "within 1.0 s" an earlier draft wrote is false by 6 ms; `w2-9/partB4-outcome.txt`). The mutex was continuously held by a *healthy, progressing* pipeline; nothing was stuck. Only the depth of the `Pending` queue mattered.
  - **Nothing reaps `Pending` — confirmed live, not just by grep.** 49 blocked runs sat `Pending` for **6 m 20.9 s – 6 m 26.4 s**, past `UNIFIED_QUEUED_RUN_GRACE`'s 5 m default (`w2-9/g5-round1-partial.txt`); at the end of the session **58** runs were `Pending`, oldest **25 m 50.2 s**, and the session produced **0** `Failed` runs and **0** reaper log lines of any kind (`w2-9/g5-final.txt`). The queued-run reaper's predicate is `r.status = 'Queued'` (`postgres.go:1279`) and the stuck-run reaper's is `Running`, so a starved run has no timeout, no grace and no healer.
  - **THE SECOND DOOR (Part D): the same 50-row ceiling contradicts `docs/high-availability.md:163` — and this is the limb that injects a real fault.** `docs/high-availability.md:159-163`, §"What happens during leader absence" (heading at `:157`): "Only **Pending→Queued transitions and schedule fires are paused** while there is no leader. … After promotion, the new leader processes any accumulated Pending Runs on the next tick — no runs are lost."
    - **The injection and the promotion, both measured.** With **58** runs `Pending` (`w2-9/partD-promotion.txt`) and `log_statement='all'` armed and verified `all` in a fresh session, `docker compose kill -s SIGKILL controller2` — the scheduler leader since `06:19:39.239646` and the sole source of every snapshot statement — at **`06:48:07.971`** (host clock). `controller1` logged `"scheduler became leader"` at **`06:48:08.336420`**. Last tick from the old leader (`h=172.20.0.3`): **`06:48:08.118`**; first tick from the new leader (`h=172.20.0.5`): **`06:48:08.337`** — a **219 ms** Pending→Queued pause, one tick period, comfortably inside I5's "leader re-election ≤ seconds" and inside the document's own "well under a second". **That half of the promise holds.**
    - **The first post-promotion tick processed 50 of 58.** `w2-9/partD-tick-candidates.txt`, per-tick candidate counts derived from the statement log by the same per-candidate `SELECT status FROM runs WHERE id = $1 FOR UPDATE` instrument: the tick at `06:48:08.337` issued **exactly 50** such calls, as did all 61 ticks in the 12.018 s capture (20 from the dying leader, 41 from the new one, **all 50**). The 8 newest `Pending` runs were not examined by the post-promotion tick, nor by the 40 ticks after it. `Pending` was **58** before the kill and **58** at `06:48:15.555` after it.
    - **So the sentence is false in one clause and true in the other, and the difference is worth stating exactly.** "processes any accumulated Pending Runs on the next tick" — **false** at 58 accumulated; the tick's ceiling is the `limit = 50` at `scheduler.go:58`, and promotion uses the same call site (code-read: `RunScheduler`'s loop contains one `TransitionPendingToQueued` call, `scheduler.go:58`, reached identically on the tick that acquires the lock and on every tick after). "no runs are lost" — **true**: the 8 stayed `Pending` and none was dropped, corrupted or duplicated. An operator reading `:163` would expect a promotion to clear the accumulation; it clears at most 50 of it per tick, and if the head 50 are unqueueable it clears none of it, ever.
- **Expected:** the docs are silent in two places, scoped away in a third, and **contradicted in exactly one** — and the whole severity of this entry turns on that last one, so it is set out separately.
  - **CONTRADICTED — `docs/high-availability.md:163`.** "After promotion, the new leader processes any accumulated Pending Runs on the next tick — no runs are lost." Two escapes were checked and neither applies. (i) **"no runs are lost" is not a saving clause.** It is a **second conjunct joined by an em dash**, adding a further promise, not narrowing "any accumulated … on the next tick"; a reading in which it qualifies the first clause would make the first clause contentless. Both conjuncts were evaluated: the first is false at 58 accumulated, the second is true. (ii) **The W2-7 scoping check passes.** `:163` sits under §"What happens during leader absence" (`:157`) and is about the **post-promotion tick specifically** — which is exactly the case that was tested — and nothing in `:159-162` (pausing, Queued runs continuing, API requests unaffected) authorises any batch size. Contrast the escape that **does** apply and its limit: `docs/high-availability.md:81` says the leader "transitions Pending→Queued" without promising a batch size, so a reader who consults only the job table is not misled. The false statement is `:163`'s.
  - **SILENT — `docs/jobs.md:1417-1429`** (§"Concurrency Control" → "Mutex"): "A named mutual exclusion lock — only one run holding the mutex runs at a time. … Runs that cannot acquire the mutex wait in the queue until it is released", under a section opening "Prevent multiple runs from executing simultaneously when they share a resource" (`:1417`). Nothing here says a mutex cannot delay a run that does not use it — on that limb the docs are **silent, not contradicted**. What the passage *does* do is **scope** the effect to contenders ("when they *share* a resource"), which is why an operator cannot predict the behaviour; that is severity context, not the violation. (Per the W2-7 lesson the surrounding section was read before citing: it is a feature description, not a guarantee about non-contenders.)
  - **SCOPED AWAY — `docs/operations.md:53`** describes this exact hazard — "instead of letting it occupy the head of every oldest-first batch — a handful of broken runs can no longer starve archival, deletion, or resolution for everything newer" — but **names three sweeps** (log archiver, run-retention sweeper, git resolver) and the scheduler is **not** one of them. It is therefore **not a broken promise**; it is evidence that the identical failure mode was recognised and mitigated in three places and left unmitigated on the critical path. `ListPendingRuns` even takes the `excluded` set that implements the mitigation (`postgres.go:2064`); `TransitionPendingToQueued` takes no such parameter.
  - **NOTHING ELSE.** `grep -rn -i "head-of-line\|starv\|fair\|FIFO\|queue depth\|backlog" docs/` and `grep -rn -i "Pending" docs/high-availability.md docs/operations.md docs/troubleshooting.md` returned nothing else relevant (`w2-9/docs-greps.txt`); no document states the 50-row limit or any queue-depth bound.
- **Severity reasoning.** **Major, not critical:** nothing is corrupted, nothing is duplicated, no record lies, and the condition self-heals the instant the backlog drops below 50 — which round 1 demonstrated by making it happen in **0.196 s** (the 50th slot opening at `06:27:52.514` to the enqueue at `06:27:52.581`). **Major, not minor:** it contradicts a published contract in the document I5 names, under a real fault injection; it is reached by ordinary use of a documented feature at a threshold no document mentions; it silently and completely denies service to runs that share nothing with the blocking work; it is invisible in every run-scoped surface; and there is no reaper, so on a queue that stays deep the run's `Pending` state has **no code-level bound at all**. **Why the two doors are not separately severable:** on its own, the Parts A/B door has no invariant and no contract and would have to be filed as an observation; on its own, the Part D door is a bounded ~200 ms extra latency per 50 queueable runs and would be a minor docs gap. The severity lives in the conjunction, which is the honest reason to file once. **Scope the negative half honestly:** both measured starvation windows were ended by an intervention (a cancel, then a natural drain), so "unbounded" is **code-read** — no `Pending` reaper, no snapshot exclusion for perpetually-blocked candidates — and **not** a measured "forever".
- **Notes:**
  - **Part D is the cheapest reproduction of the whole scenario.** It needs no probe, no cancels and no drain — 51 `Pending` runs plus one `docker compose kill` shows the ceiling in one tick. A fix that raises or paginates the limit should regression-test at exactly this shape.
  - **The same starvation is reachable with no mutex, no lock and no contention — code-read, not executed.** `tryQueueRun` returns `false, nil` for any step whose `uses.job` starts with `git://` (`postgres.go:513-518`), *before* any lock is touched, with the same silent rollback. So >50 `Pending` runs awaiting git resolution starve every newer run outright. **And the git resolver has its own 50-row oldest-first batch** (`ListPendingRuns(ctx, 50, …)`, `scheduler.go:291` → `postgres.go:2064-2069`), so a deep backlog starves *resolution* as well as *enqueue*. Reaching this live needs an unresolvable `git://` AppSource fixture this wave does not have; it **was not executed** and is recorded as a code-read amplifier only.
  - **The scheduler is the one background job for which "query load at `interval / N`" does not apply, and this session is a clean confirmation.** All 127 Part A ticks and all 61 Part D ticks came from a **single** host at a time (`w2-9/derived-numbers.txt`, `w2-9/partD-tick-candidates.txt`), because `RunScheduler` holds its advisory lock **across** ticks rather than per-tick — the carve-out at `docs/superpowers/plans/2026-07-30-edge-case-campaign-w2.md:47` and the exception to W2-1's finding. So for this job both load **and** worst-case latency sit at the nominal 200 ms on one replica.
  - **A saturated snapshot is not free: 252.0 aborted transactions per second, indefinitely.** In the 25.200 s window the leader executed **6,350** `begin`, **6,350** `SELECT … FOR UPDATE`, **6,350** `INSERT INTO mutex_holders`, **6,350** `ERROR: duplicate key value violates unique constraint "mutex_holders_pkey"` and **6,350** `rollback` — **252.0 of each per second** (derived), i.e. ~1,005 statements/s of pure churn plus 252 logged Postgres errors/s, for exactly as long as the backlog exists. **Read the INSERT figure with the halving in mind:** the raw grep count in the capture is **12,700**, not 6,350 (`w2-9/partA-pglog-analysis.txt` line 11, `w2-9/derived-numbers.txt` line 27), because each failing INSERT is logged **twice** — once as the `execute` line and once as the `STATEMENT:` echo Postgres attaches to the `ERROR`. **6,350 = 12,700 / 2 = 127 × 50** is the semantic count and is the one stated above; the other four figures are read directly at 6,350. 58,385 of the 74,951 captured log lines came from the leader host alone. This is the cost of re-deriving the same rejection five times a second for 50 runs; nothing memoises or backs off (contrast `docs/operations.md:53`'s three sweeps, which do).
  - **What could NOT be verified.** (i) That the wait is literally unbounded — both observations were terminated deliberately, so the claim is code-read. (ii) The git-unresolved amplifier, live (no fixture). (iii) Whether the WebUI presents anything different from the API bodies captured in `w2-9/partA-surfacing.txt` — the Svelte source was not read for this scenario and no browser session was run. (iv) Whether a *graceful* controller stop behaves differently from Part D's SIGKILL (only SIGKILL was injected). (v) Whether repeated promotions make progress through a deep backlog — the 41 post-promotion ticks captured all returned the same 50 candidates because the head of the queue was mutex-blocked, so the "50 per tick drains it quickly" reading was never exercised on a *queueable* backlog and remains untested. **(v) is why the Part D door cannot carry a major on its own**, and therefore why this is filed as one entry rather than two.
  - **Instrument hygiene.** `log_statement='all'` was armed and reverted **three** times (`w2-9/gate-g3-arm.txt`, `w2-9/partA-pglog-disarm.txt`, `w2-9/partB-pglog-disarm.txt`, `w2-9/partD-disarm.txt`), each revert verified `none` in a **fresh** session, and `log_line_prefix` was also `RESET` to `%m [%p]` at teardown (`w2-9/teardown.txt`) — one `ALTER SYSTEM` per `psql -c` throughout, per W2-7. It was armed only in short windows on purpose: at 200 ms ticks with 50 candidates the scheduler alone drives ~2,300 Postgres log lines/s.

## W2-9 — observation: the starved run is honestly and completely displayed, which is why nobody would notice; the one alert that fires misattributes the cause to three things that were all fine, and no surface anywhere names the 50-row limit
- **Invariant:** none violated, and the *absence* of an **I7** hook (`docs/superpowers/specs/2026-07-29-edge-case-testing-design.md:54`, "run status, approval status, and audit rows never contradict each other or reality") is the substance of the entry: every surface reported reality accurately. Recorded because the diagnosability gap is what turns the major above from a nuisance into an outage that gets misdiagnosed, and because a fix needs to know exactly which surfaces exist.
- **Severity:** minor (observation)
- **Repro:** `test/edgecase/scenarios/w2-9-head-of-line.md`, step A8, read against a **live** starved run (probe 2, `55429883`, at `06:30:44` — 16 s into a 787.6 s starvation). Evidence: `w2-9/partA-surfacing.txt`, `w2-9/partA-claim-log.txt`, `w2-9/g5-final.txt`, `w2-9/gate.txt`, `w2-9/docs-greps.txt`.
- **Observed:**
  - **Run-scoped surfaces: accurate, and empty of explanation.** `GET /api/v1/runs/{id}` returns the whole body with `"status":"Pending"` and **no reason, message or diagnostic field of any kind**. `GET /api/v1/runs/{id}/steps` → `[{"index":0,…,"status":"Pending",…}]`. `GET /api/v1/runs/{id}/logs` → `[]`, and the DB agrees (0 `logs` rows, 0 `step_reports` rows, for every starved run, all session — `w2-9/g5-final.txt`). Contrast W2-4: the queued-run reaper at least writes its reason into the run's own log before failing it. Nothing writes anything here, because the code path that rejects the run (`tryQueueRun`'s rollback, `postgres.go:487`) is by construction incapable of leaving a trace.
  - **Fleet-scoped surfaces: one gauge, no semantics.** The only metric that moves is `unifiedcd_runs_current{status="Pending"} 56` (`w2-9/partA-surfacing.txt`). There is **no** metric for snapshot saturation, batch fill, or oldest-`Pending` age, and nothing exposes the number 50 — so the gauge is only interpretable by someone who already knows the limit.
  - **The shipped alert does fire — and then points three ways, all wrong.** `deployments/observability/prometheus-alerts.yaml:18-27` ships `UnifiedCDQueueBacklog`: `max(Pending) + max(Queued) > 20 for 10m`, `severity: warning`, description *"More than 20 runs have been waiting (Pending + Queued) for 10 minutes. Agents may be under-provisioned, mislabeled, or down."* At 56 `Pending` this **would** have fired (threshold 20, sustained past 10 min in this session — derived from the census series; **no Prometheus was running, so the firing itself was not observed**). **State the mitigation honestly: an operator on the shipped observability stack gets a warning.** But all three suggested causes were false here — two agents alive and heartbeating, `agentSelector: [kind:linux]` matching both agents' labels (`w2-9/gate.txt`), `agent1` idle and long-polling 273 times — and the real cause, a saturated oldest-first snapshot, is named nowhere in the alert, in `docs/operations.md:158` (the "Approval-gate backlog" bullet, the last one under §**"Monitoring Points"** at `:151` — an earlier draft called that section "What to watch", which is not a heading this document has), or in `docs/troubleshooting.md`.
  - **Nor is there a documented way to look.** `docs/operations.md:279` labels the gauge "queue backlog = Pending + Queued" and `:301-302` gives the PromQL, and that is the whole of it. No document states the batch size, no CLI subcommand shows a run's position in the `created_at` ordering, and `grep -rn -i "head-of-line\|starv\|fair\|FIFO\|queue depth\|backlog" docs/` finds nothing that would lead an operator to the mechanism (`w2-9/docs-greps.txt`).
  - **What was NOT verified, and this entry's title depends on it.** The title asserts "no surface anywhere names the 50-row limit", but **the WebUI was not inspected** — every surface above was read via the HTTP API and the metrics endpoint (`w2-9/partA-surfacing.txt`), the Svelte source was not read for this scenario, and no browser session was run. So the claim is established for the API, the CLI, the metrics and the docs, and is **unverified for the WebUI**. Anyone acting on the title should close that gap first; it is one file read plus one page load.
- **Expected:** as-designed on every limb — the displayed state is correct, the gauge is documented, the alert exists. The observation is that the union of these surfaces cannot distinguish "50-slot snapshot saturated by unqueueable runs" from "not enough agents", which are opposite remediations: the first needs the *backlog* cancelled or the limit raised, the second needs *capacity added* — and adding agents to this rig would have changed nothing, since one was already idle.
- **Notes:** the cheapest operator-side detector available today, given what exists, is `max(unifiedcd_runs_current{status="Pending"}) >= 50` **and** `max(unifiedcd_agents{state="alive"}) > 0` **and** a flat gap between `unifiedcd_runs_created_total` and `unifiedcd_runs_finished_total` — i.e. it has to be assembled from three unrelated metrics by someone who already knows the constant. Recorded so a fix can be judged on whether it removes that requirement.

## Checkpoint: W2 complete
