- **Scenarios run: all nine, none dropped.** W2-1 (leader exclusion for every background job — Phases 0-4 on the plain `test/ha` stack with runtime Postgres statement logging: **4 entries**), W2-2 (agent replacement — Parts A / A2 / A3 / B / B2 / C: **5 entries**), W2-3 (stuck-run reaper boundary — Arms A / B1 / B2 / C0 / C2 / C2b / D0 / D1: **6 entries**), W2-4 (queued-run reaper grace boundary — Part A, seven Part B trials, Part B5 backlog amplification, Parts C and D: **5 entries**), W2-5 (unlinked `call:` child — Part A two controls, Part B injected arm: **4 entries**), W2-6 (schedule duplicate fire — Parts A / B(Arm A, 20 kills) / C / C2 / D: **5 entries**), W2-7 (duplicate agent ID — Parts A1 / A2 / B / C / D under a new `dupagent.override.yaml`: **8 entries**), W2-8 (approval race — Parts A / B / C / D / D2 / E and the review-mandated Part F in a second session: **4 entries**), W2-9 (Pending-snapshot starvation — Parts A / B / B4 / D, Part D added during execution: **2 entries**). **43 entries total = 15 violation entries (14 new violations) + 25 observations + 3 resolved branch-internal asset bugs.** *(Corrected at review: two W2-2 entries — `:627` drain-vs-orchestrator-defaults and `:652` no-reason-on-a-reconcile-failed-run — were reclassified from minor violations to observations, because neither contradicts anything. The 11 majors and the 43-entry total are unaffected; all nine scenarios were recounted independently.)* Two scenario arms were deliberately superseded rather than run and both say so on their own entries: W2-6's brief-specified Arm C (backdate `last_fired_at` by hand) was made unnecessary by Part D reaching the same branch by pure fault injection, and W2-9's Part C (the no-mutex `git://` amplifier) needs a git fixture this campaign does not have.
- **Classification rule:** unchanged from the W0 and W1 checkpoints and applied to every entry above — **violation** = observed behaviour contradicts an invariant (I1-I7) or a documented contract; **observation** = behaviour matched expectations but reveals a risk; **third bucket** = a defect in the campaign's own assets, fixed inside this branch, reported outside both tallies. The W1 clarifications carry unchanged: the "documented contract" limb requires a *published* promise (an exported API field, a schema column, or a statement in `docs/`) and **not** an unexported helper's own doc comment (W2-1, W2-4, W2-5 and W2-7 each had to invoke this, and each says so inline); and entry titles encode the classification — observations say "observation" in the title and repeat it on the Severity line, third-bucket entries carry an explicit `Classification:` line, and an entry with neither marker is a violation. **W2 added one procedural rule the W1 rule did not anticipate: an entry may be a *violation entry* without being an *additive* violation.** W2-7's Part D entry is a violation in form — no observation marker, `Severity: major` — but its defect is W2-3's, reached by a natural trigger instead of a `DELETE`, and it carries a `Classification:` line saying so. A mechanical reader would double-count it. **The wave therefore reports two numbers wherever they differ, and they differ only for W2-7: violation entries 4, new violations 3.**
- **Violations: 14 new — 11 major, 3 minor** (15 violation *entries*; the 15th is W2-7 Part D, non-additive). Per scenario: W2-1 **1** (0 major / 1 minor), W2-2 **1** (0/1), W2-3 **2** (1/1), W2-4 **1** (1/0), W2-5 **1** (1/0), W2-6 **2** (2/0), W2-7 **3 new of 4 entries** (3/0), W2-8 **2** (2/0), W2-9 **1** (1/0) — 6 + 4 + 4 by primary limb (I1 / I7 / documented contract), which is the same 14. **Two entries listed below as minors are struck and reclassified**; they are kept in place rather than deleted so the reclassification is auditable.
  - **major, I1 — W2-3** (`:687`): the stuck-run reaper's `a.id IS NULL` branch has no liveness test at all, so a healthy, heartbeating agent's run is failed as "agent lost" whenever its `agents` row is absent, and the heartbeat the docs say prevents exactly this cannot restore the row (`TouchAgent` is a bare `UPDATE`; only `UpsertAgentOnClaim` re-inserts). Reaped at `claimed_at + 71.28 s` with the agent still streaming accepted stdout **4.727 s after** the terminal write. Exposure is a ~30 s window against a 30 s sweep, i.e. roughly even odds — not a narrow race.
  - **major, I1 — W2-4** (`:779`): the queued-run reaper lists its unclaimable set once and never re-validates, so it failed a run **8.0 ms after** a live, label-matching agent claimed it and **3.0 ms after** that agent's step was recorded `Running`, with the reason "no eligible agent available to claim it". The stale window is the whole sweep loop and scales with the backlog — **4 ms for one run, 475 ms for 161** (~2.93 ms/run, uncapped: no `LIMIT` on the SELECT and no batch cap on the loop). Also contradicts `docs/high-availability.md:89`.
  - **major, documented contract (`docs/jobs.md:671-673`), I6 measured + I7 — W2-5** (`:885`): a `call:` child whose one link report is lost is permanently unreachable; the parent's cancel cascade walked an empty child list and the orphan ran to `Succeeded` **38.840 s** past its terminal parent, writing 37 further side effects. The published durability promise ("this remains accurate if the parent agent stops before it can submit a final step report") is falsified without qualification, and the observed state is worse than an inaccurate badge — there is no step-1 row at all.
  - **major, I1 + I2 — W2-6** (`:969`): a schedule fire is two autocommit writes with no enclosing transaction and nothing reconciles them. Two runs were created for cron occurrence `00:22:00`, **2.030 s apart**, on two different replicas, and **both executed the step** (60 log lines where 30 were due). The duplicate is invisible in the API — `runs.triggered_by` carries no occurrence stamp, and the `UPDATE schedules SET last_fired_at = $1` bind is the only surface in the system that names the occurrence.
  - **major, documented contract (`docs/resources.md:324`), I5 closest fit — W2-6** (`:1005`): a `* * * * *` schedule is born **~59.9 minutes behind**, cannot catch up under stable leadership, and lives **8.4-13.1 s** from the silent-skip cliff; one refused `last_fired_at` write pushed it over and it then **never fired again** — 8 consecutive checks, 8 `UPDATE`s, 0 runs, 0 log lines, and no API path back short of `DELETE`+`POST`. **This is the wave's severity-open entry — see carry-forward item 1.**
  - **major, I1 (I7 second limb) — W2-7** (`:1126`): the startup reconcile has no grace and no ownership check, so a twin process terminalized runs it never executed — a 238-second-old run 1.9 s after the twin started, and a 4-second-old run just as readily. `handleAgentReconcileRuns` takes the agent id from the URL path and calls no guard at all.
  - **major, I1 (I7 second limb) — W2-7** (`:1143`): with both processes live, each heartbeat reap kills the *other* process's run — two legitimate runs destroyed **1.682 s apart**, no injection of any kind, and **not one log line anywhere** (the heartbeat reconcile emits only on `failOrphanedRun` error). Strictly worse than the W2-3 precedent on both axes: no induced precondition and no `WARN`.
  - **major, I7 (I1 secondary) — W2-7** (`:1163`): the ownership guard is a bare string compare against `claimed_by`, so **any local reader of an agent's credential file** can write to and terminalize a run that agent is executing — **no duplicate ID required**. A third client injected a log line and a fabricated step, then forged `Succeeded` **49.1 s** into a ~300 s step, **and the forged terminal write left no audit row at all** (agent identity routes carry no audit middleware). Both of I7's named surfaces broken at once.
  - **major, I7 — W2-8** (`:1278`): the approval endpoint never looks at the run, so an ordinary `unified-cli approve` writes `Approved` and a **named human** onto an already-terminal run and the audit log records a `204` success. Reproduced 5 times on `Failed` runs and once on `Cancelled`; the exposure window is the gate's whole remaining `timeoutMinutes` (documented default **60 minutes**), demonstrated with **9m48s** still on `timeout_at`. Closes W1 carry-forward item 1, which predicted the window and never fired the POST.
  - **major (top of band), I7 + published contract (`docs/jobs.md:1775-1777`, `:1788`/`:1791-1793`), I6 measured — W2-8 Part F** (`:1306`): an approve committing inside the agent's 5 s cancel-detection fence makes the gate return **true**, so the post-gate step body **executes** on a run the controller has already marked `Cancelled` — **4/4 aimed attempts**, `echo` output written **0.56-2.96 s** after the cancel, while `step_reports` holds no row for that step and the live API answers `"status":"Pending"`. Two ordinary documented calls (or two clicks — the UI still draws live Approve/Reject buttons on a `Cancelled` run), `dev` rights, no fault injection. **Counted as additive, not a restatement**: `approval.go:55` and `pipeline.go:126` are agent-side ordering defects that would still race a cancel against the next step on a controller where `DecideApproval`'s missing run-status guard were already fixed.
  - **major, documented contract (`docs/high-availability.md:163`) + I5 — W2-9** (`:1398`): the scheduler only ever examines the **50 oldest** `Pending` runs (`limit = 50` hardcoded at `internal/controller/scheduler.go:58`), so a fully runnable run declaring no concurrency at all is **never considered**, not merely deferred: **252.639 s** and **787.615 s** of starvation against a **0.185 s** baseline, 127 consecutive ticks whose candidate set was the identical 50 rows, an agent long-polling **273** times for work, a first post-promotion tick that processed **50 of 58**, and not one log line anywhere. **ONE defect, two doors, counted ONCE** — Parts A/B (ordinary traffic, no fault) and Part D (SIGKILL of the leader) both bottom out at `scheduler.go:58`; the entry states this explicitly because a mechanical reader would file two majors.
  - **minor, documented contract — W2-1** (`:515`): ten of the eleven "leader-elected" background jobs have no leader at all — the advisory lock is a per-tick mutex, so each sweep executes up to N× per nominal interval on N replicas (**2.15-2.40 measured** on three), on a different replica each time. `docs/high-availability.md:94` ("held ... for the lifetime of the goroutine") is true of exactly one job; `:382-384`'s "every **30s**, **the leader**" is wrong on both the cadence (43 sweeps in 573.4 s) and the singular. Held at minor because the *safety* property is intact — every sweep acquires around its work, so no sweep ever ran concurrently (see the negative-control observation) — but it carries one behavioural consequence: the leader-local `failureBackoff` in `archiver.go:25-28` and `run_retention.go:35-38` is silently defeated by rotating leadership (code-read; both jobs unwired on `test/ha`).
  - **minor, I7 — W2-2** (`:587`): a cascade-cancelled `call:` child, and **every plainly-cancelled run**, is left with a step permanently reporting `Running` under a terminal run — 14m46s after the fact in the measured case, and rendered by the WebUI as a live-incrementing elapsed time. Filed not on a docs gap but on the fact that shipped PR #113 covers one `MarkRunFinished` caller and misses the rest, **including the remainder of the very path it targeted** (`failOrphanedRun`'s own cascade). Seven entry points, none of which touch descendants' steps. Defer to W1-5's funnel fix.
  - ~~**minor, I5 — W2-2** (`:627`)~~ **RECLASSIFIED — observation, not a violation, and not counted above.** Nothing is contradicted: `docs/configuration.md:173` and `docs/high-availability.md:341` are accurate as written and the entry says so, so this is a documentation gap on W2-1's `:572` precedent. Content unchanged and still worth reading: the plan's "rolling restart kills in-flight work with no drain window" premise is **false** — the documented drain works and carried a run 107.31 s past SIGTERM to `Succeeded` — but the default is unbounded and **no orchestrator default gives it time to**: k8s 30 s, Docker's documented 10 s, and bare `docker compose restart` measured at **1.013 s**. There is no agent-side `terminationGracePeriodSeconds` guidance anywhere in `docs/`.
  - ~~**minor, I5 — W2-2** (`:652`)~~ **RECLASSIFIED — observation, not a violation, and not counted above.** Its own invariant line calls it a diagnosability defect and disclaims I5; its Expected cites `queuedrun_reaper.go:66-72`, an unexported function that `:479` disqualifies as a contract; and the two identical-shape entries in this wave (`:712`, `:814`) are both observations. Content unchanged: a run failed by the agent-replacement reconcile carries no operator-visible reason — no error field on the run, no message on the step, no system log line — unlike the queued-run reaper, which appends one to the run's own log before failing it. The two reapers disagree about whether a machine-initiated failure should explain itself; the fix is one line.
  - **minor, I1 — W2-3** (`:741`): `failOrphanedRun`'s crash window between `MarkRunFinished`'s commit and the descendant walk is **1 ms** wide and nothing re-drives a cascade lost inside it. **Measured, NOT reproduced live in 10 attempts** — filed at minor deliberately on that ground, with the note that it would be major if reproduced; container-level injection is ~3 orders of magnitude too coarse (`docker kill` round trip 0.636-0.707 s against a 1 ms target, ~0.15%/attempt).
- **Not counted in either tally — 3 resolved branch-internal asset bugs** (W1's count was 1, so the campaign total is now 4). **W2-3** (`:769`): `inject.sh nginx-unblock` bound `svc="${2:?...}"` unconditionally before dispatching, so the documented invocation exited 1 under `set -eu` and, written as `|| true` in a teardown, silently left the partition in place — it survived a whole wave as a documented wart in the W1-5 runbook; fixed in `a7ae25a`. **W2-6** (`:1107`): `schedule-every-minute.payload.json` was documented as a `POST /api/v1/jobs` body and returns 400 there; schedules have their own endpoint. **W2-7** (`:1267`): the campaign's Postgres-instrumentation snippet put two `ALTER SYSTEM` statements in one `psql -c`, which Postgres refuses — **and the failure is silent**, so an armed-looking instrument records nothing; it invalidated no W2-6 measurement (the drift was between W2-6's checked-in text and what it executed). **Scope corrected at this checkpoint: the broken form was in six committed copies across three runbooks, not only W2-6's two** — W2-1's arm and revert (`:209`, `:535` at `2a1dcf5`) and W2-3's arm and revert (`:430-432`, `:527`) carried it too, and all four were split in `6dd3608`; the entry at `:1273` now names all six and names the two occurrences that are deliberately left quoting the broken form. **The lesson all three share, and it is the same one W1-4's `spec.mutex` bug recorded:** a campaign asset exercised only on a scenario's happy path can carry a defect through an entire wave, because the arm that would expose it — teardown, heal, revert — is the arm whose failure is deliberately suppressed. Standing rule for W3+: **never write `|| true` on an injection, heal or revert command without also asserting the post-condition in a session that did not issue it.**
- **Observations: 25** (23 as first tallied, plus the two W2-2 entries reclassified at review — `:627` and `:652`) — 23 carry `Severity: minor (observation)`; two (W2-2 `:612`, `:669`) carry only `Severity: observation` with an as-designed/negative-control qualifier and no severity band, which is a formatting drift worth normalising rather than a classification question. By scenario: **W2-1 (3)** — 15,665 lock events with zero overlapping holds on the per-tick keys plus a single unshared scheduler holder at every census and 0.40 s failover, which is the negative control W0-1's split-brain finding needed; all three unlocked jobs confirmed running on every replica (two documented as deliberate, `DeleteStaleAgents` documented nowhere, git resolver 5.0 queries/s **per replica** on a completely idle stack); five of eleven keys unobservable on `test/ha` for two non-defect reasons. **W2-2 (4)** — the documented agent drain works and carried a run **107.31 s** past SIGTERM to `Succeeded`, but the default is unbounded and no orchestrator default gives it time to (k8s 30 s, Docker's documented 10 s, bare `docker compose restart` measured at **1.013 s**), with no agent-side `terminationGracePeriodSeconds` guidance anywhere in `docs/` — a gap, not a contradiction; a run failed by the agent-replacement reconcile carries no operator-visible reason at all, unlike the queued-run reaper, which appends one to the run's own log before failing it; the startup reconcile has no claim grace and failed a run 2.409 s after it was claimed while both other orphan definitions carry 60 s (as-designed and documented); I1 and I3 both held under agent replacement (child cancelled 3.2 ms after the parent, mutex released in the run's own transaction, successor acquired in 0.111 s). **W2-3 (3)** — a heartbeat for a deleted `agents` row is a zero-row `UPDATE` that still answers 204, so every surface shows a healthy agent the reaper treats as gone; the reaper's two conjuncts measured (the 60 s grace can never bind on the heartbeat path and is the *only* gate on the deleted-row path); the `interval / N` correction. **W2-4 (4)** — the queued-run reaper is the only reaper that tells the operator why, but names the label selector even when labels were never the problem; the documented grace is a floor, not the deadline (`created_at + grace + U(0, interval)`, so 25.1 s past the grace still won in 6 of 7 trials); a capability-unschedulable run is left `Queued` on purpose but only while an unusable agent stays connected; the grace is measured from `created_at`, so a run that spent it blocked on a mutex gets **zero** grace. **W2-5 (3)** — the terminal report that `callstep.go:56-61` calls a self-heal is answered `200 OK` and silently dropped once the parent is terminal, i.e. exactly when the link is needed; `api.Run.ParentRunID` does not exist and `runs` has no parent column, so `step_reports.child_run_id` is the sole edge in both directions; a human cancel gets exactly **one** un-retried cascade attempt. **W2-6 (2)** — one locked `schedules` row stops all scheduling cluster-wide with no timeout (51.3 s of `Pending` on an unrelated API-triggered run); the `CreateRun`→`last_fired_at` window is 1-3 ms (38 fires, median 2.0 ms), 20 aimed `SIGKILL`s did not land in it, every promotion re-checks immediately, failover 0.28-0.47 s. **W2-7 (3)** — nothing anywhere reveals that two processes share one agent ID, and `last_seen_at` looks *healthier* with a duplicate present; the zombie window on a **connected** agent is **0.939-4.938 s, not W1-5's 40.2-162.5 s**; reaping a mutex-holding run releases the mutex 2.026 s before its holder stops, so I3's letter holds and the release is premature rather than leaked. **W2-8 (2)** — the decision and the approval reaper are two unsynchronised writers CAS-ing one row and the earlier statement wins in 9/9 inside a 7 ms band, with the human's loss reported as `409 "already decided"` when nobody decided anything; the two approval clocks do not measurably differ (skew bounded to ~(−3.6, +4.8) ms) yet the agent's detection is **always** later than `timeout_at`, by 6.6-19.5 ms over 16 runs, by code ordering rather than skew. **W2-9 (1)** — the starved run is honestly and completely displayed on every surface, which is why nobody would notice; the one shipped alert that fires misattributes the cause to three things that were all fine, and no surface anywhere names the 50-row limit.
- **RULE AMENDMENT, then the tally. The I1-vs-I7 convention is resolved here once, explicitly, rather than applied silently per entry.** W1-5 established (`:306`, restated at `:484`) that a run reaching exactly one terminal state which is *simply* wrong is "an I7 concern, **not an I1 one**", and W2-2 (`:588`) follows it. Four W2 entries file what looks like the identical shape at **I1 primary** (`:687`, `:779`, `:1126`, `:1143`). **They are not the identical shape, and the discriminator is the word "simply" (that is the word `:306` and `:484` actually use; an earlier draft of this amendment misquoted it as "merely") — so the W1 formulation is refined here, not overturned, and the four labels stand.** **Scope of the refinement, stated so W3 cannot over-read it: this refines the *campaign's own labelling convention*. It is NOT a demonstration that I1's spec text (`docs/superpowers/specs/2026-07-29-edge-case-testing-design.md:48`) was breached on its literal first clause — in all four entries the run does reach exactly one terminal state — so W3 must not later cite `:687`, `:779`, `:1126` or `:1143` as textual I1 breaches.** In W1-5 and W2-2 the *record alone* is wrong: the work had already completed successfully (W1-5) or had already stopped (W2-2), nothing was destroyed, and the only harm is that a surface lies — an I7 harm. In the four W2 entries the wrong terminal write **is itself the destruction of the run's live execution**: the controller writes a terminal status onto a run a demonstrably healthy agent is still executing, that agent's cancel poller abandons the work within one `CancelPollInterval`, and nothing re-queues it (`docs/high-availability.md:401-404`) — work an API-accepted run was performing is lost, which is a run-**accounting** harm, not a display harm. Each of the four states that destruction explicitly in its own Observed section. **The rule for W3+, as an instruction:** *wrong record over work that had already finished or already stopped* → **I7 primary, I1 held**; *wrong record that terminates work still in flight* → **I1 primary, I7 as the second limb** for the interval in which the DB and reality disagree. Consequence: the counts below are as-labelled and the wave is I1-dominant on that reading; a reader applying the unrefined W1 sentence would make it I7-dominant, which is why the refinement is stated rather than assumed. `:804` previously justified its I1 label by citing `:484`, which says the opposite for its own shape — corrected in place, as is the same miscitation at `:1127`. **Invariant tally for the 14 new violations** (each as its own entry states it — **do not re-merge; several attributions were corrected during review and the corrections are load-bearing**): **I1** primary in **6** (W2-3 `:687` major, W2-3 `:741` minor, W2-4 major, W2-6 `:969` major, W2-7 `:1126` major, W2-7 `:1143` major) — of which W2-6 `:969` carries **I2** as a co-primary consequence limb and the two W2-7 majors carry **I7** as an explicit second limb. **I7** primary in **4** (W2-2 `:587` minor, W2-7 `:1163` major, W2-8 `:1278` major, W2-8 Part F major). **I5** primary in **0** among the violations — the two W2-2 minors that carried it (`:627`, `:652`) were reclassified as observations at review, and both had already disclaimed I5 in their own text — leaving **1** entry where I5 is a carrying limb behind a contract primary (W2-9). So: I1 6 + I7 4 + documented contract 4 = **14**, which reconciles with the count above. **Documented contract** primary in **4** (W2-1 minor on `docs/high-availability.md`; W2-5 on `docs/jobs.md:671-673`; W2-6 `:1005` on `docs/resources.md:324`; W2-9 on `docs/high-availability.md:163`). **I6** is primary nowhere: it is cited in W2-5 and in W2-8 Part F, and in both cases per the spec's own caveat (`...design.md:53`) it produces a *measurement handed to the operator*, not a scored verdict — W2-7's zombie-window observation is the wave's I6 deliverable. **I3 recorded zero violations and was actively re-confirmed twice** — W2-2 (`:669`, mutex released in the run's own transaction, successor in 0.111 s, no `mutex_holders`/`named_lock_slots` residue) and W2-7 (`:1255`, the risk runs the *other* way: release is premature by 2.026 s, not leaked). **I4 recorded zero violations because W2 never exercised it** — no arm of any W2 scenario touched log integrity or archival; that is a coverage gap owned by W3, not a pass.

### Cross-wave carry-forward

- **1. The single highest-value follow-up measurement in this batch is W2-6's, and it is a severity question, not a curiosity.** One unattended **~3-hour promotion-free** observation settles whether a `* * * * *` schedule reaches the silent-skip cliff with **no fault at all**: apply a fresh every-minute schedule to an otherwise idle stack, **inject nothing and kill nothing**, and sample `schedules.last_fired_at`, `NOW()` and `count(*) FROM runs WHERE triggered_by='schedule:...'` every 30 s. If the fire count freezes while `last_fired_at` keeps advancing, **the W2-6 entry at `:1005` is critical, not major** — silent, permanent loss of *every* occurrence of a schedule, with no run, no log line, no metric, and a row that still looks healthy. The arithmetic is already measured: the closed form is `A_{k+1} = A_k + (c − 60)` with `c ∈ [60, 60.2]` for a stable leader, drift **+0.084 s/min**, and birth headroom only `60 − f` seconds (**13.067 s** and **8.4-8.6 s** measured across two incarnations), so 13.067 s of headroom is consumed in **~156 minutes**. **Two validity conditions, both learned the hard way in W2-6 and both non-negotiable.** (i) **The window must be promotion-free.** Leader promotions *heal* the lag: `lastScheduleCheck` is process-local, so a freshly promoted leader checks on its first tick, `c ≈ 0`, and the lag drops by ~60 s. Measured across Arm A's 20 kills: **8 promotions landed after that minute's check had already run and each cut the lag by 59.6-59.9 s, total −478.156 s** — which is why 20 `SIGKILL`s left the schedule *healthier* than they found it, and why one promotion buys back ~12 hours of drift. A window with any promotion in it measures nothing. (ii) **The sampler must be continuous.** The intermediate fingerprint (`d` jumping by one cron period) survives only **60.36 s** before the silent-advance branch erases it; a per-minute or per-kill sampler sees nothing at all. Budget: one idle stack, ~3 h wall, one 30 s sampler loop. Nothing else in the W2 batch has this ratio of cost to consequence.
- **2. W2-8 Part F's discriminator is the second-highest-value follow-up, and it must be run on BOTH execution backends.** The question: does `cancelRun()` actually **kill** a long post-gate step, or does the step run to completion? Part F's `after` step was an `echo`, so it finished before the fence could bite; the entry's "execution is bounded (≤5 s)" premise is explicitly **code-read, not measured** (`stepCtx` descends from `runCtx`). Put a ~120 s step after the gate and check. **If a long post-gate step is not interrupted, the exposure is unbounded rather than ≤5 s and W2-8 Part F is critical.** The both-backends requirement is not padding: the Part F fixture is `native: true`, and `docs/jobs.md:314-322` documents native `ucd-sh` background-job behaviour as an in-process goroutine "bounded only by the step's own context", whereas a container-exec step's kill path is a different mechanism entirely — a native-only answer covers native and says nothing about container-exec. Run both arms or the answer is half an answer. Note the asymmetry either way: an *interrupted* post-gate step is not benign (a half-applied deploy is arguably worse than a clean skip), but interruption at least bounds it.
- **3. `RunGitResolver` is the one W2 item whose harm is undemonstrated purely for want of a fixture, and it should be folded into W3's git work rather than standing alone.** W2-1 established the facts and the honest limit: the job has **no lock at all** and this is the *documented* design (`docs/high-availability.md:91-92`, `none (idempotent)` / "harmless if duplicated"), so confirming the absence is a null result and a violation requires falsifying the "harmless" claim itself. Three code-read reasons it deserves testing rather than trust, all three inferred and none observed: (a) `resolveGitPendingRuns` does a full `resolver.ResolveSpec` — a git fetch plus, for private repos, a secret decrypt — per candidate **per replica**, so the remote git host and the key-manager path see N× the traffic and "idempotent" says nothing about that cost; (b) the `failureBackoff` that exists precisely so a poisoned candidate "doesn't fill every batch" is created **inside** the goroutine and is therefore per-replica, so a candidate excluded on replica A is still selected by B and C — the same defect shape W2-1 filed against the two leader-local backoffs, reached from the other direction, **and with a documented-contract limb available rather than only an unexported comment: `docs/operations.md:53` names the git resolver in published prose and states that "The backoff state is held by the current leader only and resets on failover", which is false in the direction that matters (see `:547` item (3));** (c) the resolve-deadline path calls `AppendLog(... systemLogStepIndex ...)` **before** `MarkRunFinished` with no lock and no CAS, so N replicas reaching the deadline in one tick can each append the same operator-visible line to the run's log — a duplicated side effect on the one surface an operator reads. **(a) and (c) are the ones worth a scenario.** Three further W2 items queue behind the *same* fixture and should be scheduled with it, not separately: **W1-7** (AppSource reconciler, deferred by the spec for exactly this reason), **W2-4's** un-run git variant (`tryQueueRun` skips unresolved `git://` `uses:` before touching status, so such a run accumulates `created_at` age while `Pending` and enters `Queued` with zero grace — same mechanism as the measured mutex case, untested), and **W2-9's** git amplifier (>50 `Pending` runs awaiting resolution starve every newer run outright, **and** `ListPendingRuns(ctx, 50, …)` gives the resolver its own 50-row oldest-first batch, so a deep backlog starves resolution as well as enqueue). Also note the cheap multiplier W2-1 found: the resolver's snapshot is a 50-row `ORDER BY created_at` read of the *same shape* as the one W2-9 is about, taken independently on every replica — an N-replica multiplier on its query cost, though not on its semantics. **Two distinct statements with two independent hardcoded 50s, not one** (`ListPendingRuns`, `scheduler.go:291` → `postgres.go:2066-2068`, unlocked/per-replica; versus `TransitionPendingToQueued`, `scheduler.go:58` → `postgres.go:440`, leader-only) — an earlier version of this item said "the *same*", which would let a fixer raising one limit believe both were raised. See `:575` and `:1443`. **Recommendation: one W3 task builds the git-over-HTTP fixture (dumb protocol: bare repo + `git update-server-info` + static file server) and four scenarios consume it.**
- **4. The `LogPusher` quadratic request amplification remains a W6-S2 input, unchanged by W2.** W1-6 measured `flushLocked` re-issuing a full `AppendLogBulk` for **every** batch still pending on each 2 s tick, so cumulative volume is triangular — **O(t²) in outage duration, not O(t)** (20,447 rejections measured, ~67 req/s derived peak against an ~8.3 req/s floor). W2 exercised nothing that bears on it: no W2 arm ran a chatty step under a sustained outage. It stays owned by **W6-S2**, which must measure request-count *shape* alongside drop-marker frequency and lost-line counts, and the disk-spill decision stays gated on that measurement and must be taken together with the coalesce-the-backlog fix — spilling makes the backlog cheaper to *hold* and does nothing about resending it, so spill plus today's `flushLocked` makes the O(t²) cost worse. Keep the three `LogPusher` items distinct when triaging: **F9** is data loss at the 1 MiB byte cap (never approached), the **W1-2 major** is data loss at the bounded 5 s step-end retry exit, and **this** is pure wasted request volume with no data loss by its own mechanism.
- **5. `step_reports.child_run_id` is the sole parent-child edge in the entire system, and W2-5 settled that definitively — every later wave reasoning about `call:` composition must start here.** There is **no `api.Run.ParentRunID` field** (`api.Run` has no such member; `types.go:65` is `api.CalledBy.ParentRunID`, reachable only through the nested `omitempty CalledBy`), and **no `runs` parent column** (live `information_schema` read; nothing in migrations `001`-`017` adds one). So one row carries all three traversals at once: parent→children (`ListChildRunIDs`, which gates `cancelDescendantRuns` and therefore **all seven** cascade entry points), child→parent (`GetRunParent`, which is the same row read backwards and is what populates `calledBy`), and the WebUI's "Called by …" link (`RunDetail.svelte:1148`). That row is written by a **separate, unretried, error-discarded** HTTP request (`callstep.go:62`, `_ = client.ReportStep(...)`; `Client.ReportStep` is a bare `c.do` with no retry wrapper), and two production triggers need no injection at all: a `POST` landing on an expired token gets one `401` and **no replay** (`Client.do` replays 401 only for GETs), and the same `ctx` that carries the step deadline aborts the link request while the child — created by an already-completed request — exists and runs. **The only surviving forensic trace of an orphan's origin is `runs.triggered_by = 'agent:<agentId>'`, which names the agent, not the run**, so with two agents and concurrent parents an orphan is discoverable but unattributable. The durable fix is a `runs.parent_run_id` column populated inside `createRunFromJob`'s own transaction, with `step_reports.child_run_id` demoted to a UI convenience; retrying harder is **not** sufficient, because `handleAgentStepReport` answers `200 {"alreadyFinalized":true}` before touching `step_reports` once the run is terminal and `Client.do` reads any 2xx as success.

### Forward questions answered

- **(a) Which W3+ scenarios do the reaper/queue findings change? Five of the six W3 scenarios and one W4 scenario, in concrete ways.**
  1. **W3-1 (cache TTL expiry racing a restore) — two prerequisites and one technique.** Cache cleanup is one of the four goroutines that **never start on `test/ha`** (`obj != nil`-gated; the boot log says `"no object store configured — log archival disabled"`), so W3-1 must configure an object store or the job it races does not exist — W2-1 proved that from the boot log and its null-result entry (`:577`) is the standing instruction: **a key with zero holders is three different results — not wired, wired but not yet ticked, wired but broken — and only the third is a finding; check the boot log first.** And do not race the cleanup sweep by repetition: W2-1, W2-3, W2-4 and W2-8 independently measured the per-tick jobs' sweeps as a **burst of 2-3 executions within ~10 ms followed by a full nominal interval of silence** on a stable `(epoch mod interval)` grid drifting ~12-13 ms/tick. Use W2-4's `partB-phase.sh` phase-locking (place the trigger on the measured grid) rather than repeating attempts — it converted a ~3% long shot into a coin flip, and W2-7 Part D hit a two-grid intersection on the **first** aimed attempt.
  2. **W3-2 (S3 outage during log archival) — test the backoff, because W2-1 predicts it does not work.** The log archiver carries `newFailureBackoff` **outside** its tick loop under an explicit "**Leader-local**" comment, and W2-1 established that leadership rotates every tick across the ten per-tick jobs, so on three replicas the replica that recorded a failure is usually not the replica running the next sweep and the other two have **empty** backoff maps — a poisoned run should be re-selected into the oldest-first batch immediately. W2-1 could not demonstrate it (the job is unwired on `test/ha`); W3-2 will have the object store wired anyway and gets the measurement almost free. **Correction — an earlier version of this item said the comment is on an unexported construct and "cannot itself carry a violation", so the finding "would have to be framed on observed re-selection". That is only half right, and it would have cost W3-2 its strongest limb.** The comment indeed cannot carry it (`:479`), but `docs/operations.md:53` — §"Sweep failure backoff" — is a **published** paragraph naming the log archiver by name, promising that backoff stops a broken run occupying "the head of every oldest-first batch", and asserting that "**The backoff state is held by the current leader only and resets on failover**". W2-1 shows it resets **every tick** instead, because the archiver's lock is a per-tick mutex and no leader persists between sweeps. So W3-2 should frame the finding on **that contract plus observed re-selection**, not on the unexported comment, and should quote `:53` verbatim before citing it (per methodological finding 2 at `:1509`).
  3. **W3-5 (archive seal racing the final log flush) — its premise is now a measured asymmetry, not a hypothesis.** W2-2, W2-5 and W2-8 Part F together established that `handleAgentStepReport` returns `200 {"alreadyFinalized":true}` **without writing** once the run is terminal, while the **`logs/bulk` path has no equivalent guard** and accepted a post-terminal line with `204` (that missing guard is the only reason W2-8 Part F's execution is visible at all). W3-5 should take that asymmetry as its starting fact and measure which artifact wins, rather than re-deriving it.
  4. **W3-6 (run retention racing an in-flight artifact upload) — one prerequisite and one shape to look for.** Run retention is disabled by default on `test/ha` (`UNIFIED_RUN_RETENTION_DAYS` unset → 0; boot log `"run retention disabled (keep forever)"`), so W3-6 must set it. It is also the second job carrying the defeated leader-local backoff. More importantly, **the shape W2-4 found is a shape to hunt for, not a one-off:** a sweep that takes a set-valued SELECT and then iterates side-effecting writes over it without re-validating, guarded by a CAS too weak to notice — measured at 4 ms for one run and 475 ms for 161, uncapped. `FinishRun`'s two lock releases (`mutex_holders` DELETE, `named_lock_slots` UPDATE) are **unconditional, not gated on the CAS result**, which is the larger unexercised half of that finding and is an I3/I2 exposure; any W3 sweep with the same read-then-write structure should be checked for it.
  5. **W3-3 (mixed-KEK replicas)** inherits W2-1's git-resolver observation as a load input only: the resolver's per-candidate `ResolveSpec` includes a secret decrypt for private repos and runs on **every** replica, so a wrong-KEK replica is reached by that path N times per candidate without any run being routed to it.
  6. **W4-1 (pod GC racing a live run) — do not budget W1-5's zombie.** W2-7 measured **six** terminal writes from four different killers and every soft fence landed in **0.939-4.938 s** (`RunClaim`'s cancel poller `GetRun`s every `CancelPollInterval = 5 s` and cancels on **any** terminal status). W1-5's 40.2 s and 162.5 s zombies were produced by a *partition* blinding that poller. **Consequence: an "unfenced zombie" claim now requires showing the agent could not reach `GET /api/v1/runs/{id}`**, and an arm designed to *watch* a zombie accumulate side effects has ~5 s of signal, not minutes, so it must be instrumented at second resolution or it will see nothing. The measured post-terminal side-effect exposure was exactly **2** further appends to the shared log. W4-2 and W4-3 are unaffected by W2.
- **(b) Do the three unlocked background jobs need their own scenario in a later wave? One does, two do not — and a fourth job needs a probe rather than a scenario.** **`RunGitResolver`: yes, and it should not stand alone** — fold it into the W3 git-fixture task per carry-forward item 3, where it joins W1-7, W2-4's git-`Pending` variant and W2-9's git amplifier. **`DeleteExpiredOIDCStates`: no.** `docs/high-availability.md:91-92` documents it as `none (idempotent)` / "Runs on all replicas", W2-1 measured it at 1 statement per replica per 10 minutes, and confirming a documented design is a null result. **`DeleteStaleAgents`: no separate scenario — it is already fully characterised, and what remains is a one-line docs fix.** W2-1 measured the three-replica race directly (three unsynchronised `DELETE`s in a 33 ms spread, of which one deleted the row and two matched nothing and therefore logged nothing — which is exactly why the `deleted=N` log line alone cannot distinguish "one replica ran" from "three raced"), and its *consequence* is not a scenario waiting to be written but the W2-3 major already filed: once the row is gone, the reaper's `a.id IS NULL` disjunct is satisfied unconditionally. W2-7 Part D then demonstrated the natural trigger end to end with no DB mutation. The residue is documentation: `DeleteStaleAgents` is absent from the arbitration table and from every other arbitration statement in `docs/`, and the agent's own SIGTERM deregistration is undocumented entirely (`grep -rn -i deregister docs/` matches nothing outside `docs/superpowers/`). **The job that *does* need something is audit retention** — the one leader-elected job of the eleven whose exclusion this campaign has never exercised, because it is wired by default (90 days) but on a 1-hour ticker whose first fire is at boot+1h, against observation windows of 12-48 minutes. It needs a direct `runAuditRetentionOnce` probe under the `edgeprobe` build tag, not a compose scenario, and it is cheap; W0-2's probe pattern is the template.
- **(c) Should `RunGitResolver` be folded into W3's git fixture work? Yes, unambiguously, and it should be the same task that builds the fixture.** See carry-forward item 3 for the full argument. The short form: W2-1 produced everything obtainable without a git-backed job — the absence of the lock, the per-replica statement counts (2871/2871/2871 over 573.4 s, i.e. **5.006 queries/s per replica**, **15.0/s across three**, on a stack with zero jobs and zero runs), and three code-read consequences — and explicitly declined to claim the interesting question was answered. The fixture is the gating cost, four scenarios need it, and building it once is the only sane sequencing.

### Methodological findings — two of them, and both are wave-level rather than scenario-level

- **1. The plan's "Verified code facts (do not re-derive)" block was corrected by eight consecutive tasks, and this is the second wave in a row it happened.** Every scenario but one amended it: W2-1 corrected four bullets (audit retention is wired by default and needs no flag; the absence of a lock is a violation for at most **one** of the three unlocked jobs, not all three, because the arbitration table documents two of them; `pg_locks` cannot census ten of the eleven keys and an empty row is the *expected* result; `docker compose stop` **cannot** age out an agent because the SIGTERM path deregisters in ~0.9 s, which broke W2-3's Arm C as written); W2-2 corrected `cancelDescendantRuns`' call-site list (four direct sites and seven entry points, not the five listed, and the list omitted both the public cancel endpoint and the agent parent-finish report) and retired the "no drain window" premise; **W2-3 overturned a rule W2-1 had itself established two tasks earlier** — `interval / N` is right about query load and wrong about latency, because the acquisitions are clustered within ~10 ms rather than spread, so worst-case latency stays at the nominal interval; W2-4 qualified the capability exemption and the grace semantics; W2-5 confirmed the unlinked-child mechanism and added two injection-free production triggers; W2-6 corrected the "separate pool connections" phrasing (all 48 fires logged the `INSERT` and the `UPDATE` under the **same** backend pid — the defect is two autocommit statements with no enclosing `Begin`, and the wrong phrasing would send a fixer to pool configuration) — **with a caveat this checkpoint originally got wrong: the correction was *recorded* in `FINDINGS.md:999` and in the plan's sub-bullet `plan:75` at execution time, but the plan's own fact line `plan:74` still read "separate pool connections" until the W2 review, so a reader of the facts block met the superseded phrasing first. `plan:74` now carries a `CORRECTED` banner in place, and recorded and propagated finally agree; W2-3's `:739` draws exactly this recorded-versus-propagated distinction and is the model**; W2-7 corrected the zombie magnitude by one to two orders of magnitude and established that the campaign's own instrumentation snippet fails silently; W2-9 corrected its own invariant attribution. Only W2-8 left the block's *claims* standing, and even it flagged an elision (`plan:81` omits `MarkExpiredApprovalsTimedOut`'s third conjunct `timeout_at IS NOT NULL`). **Three of these were load-bearing rather than cosmetic:** the `interval / N` latency rule would have mis-budgeted both W2-3 and W2-4 (three of W2-3's four measured reap latencies exceed the 13.3 s the rule predicted, which a 13.3 s effective cadence cannot produce); `docker compose stop` would have made W2-3's Arm C measure nothing while looking armed; and W2-9's mechanism had to be reframed one phase earlier — from claim-query head-of-line blocking to the 50-row `Pending` snapshot — **before the wave began**, without which the scenario would have measured the wrong query. **The rule for W3+, stated as an instruction rather than an observation: that block is claims-to-check-cheaply-at-the-point-of-use, not givens.** One grep at the point of consumption catches most of these before a scenario is designed around them. And the failure mode is specific enough to name: a block labelled "do not re-derive" invites exactly the trust it should not receive, and the entries most likely to be wrong are the *inferences* drawn from correct constants (W1's revocation window, W2-1's `interval / N`) rather than the constants themselves.
- **2. The campaign's own review cost is real and should be stated plainly, because it recurs.** Four entries had to be corrected for **stretching an invariant to fit a finding**, and in each case the stretch would have inflated or misdirected the severity: **W2-5** relabelled I3 as "no orphaned side effects", which is not a campaign invariant at all (I3 is *no lock leaks*, and no lock is involved anywhere in that scenario); **W2-8 Part F** was corrected off I2, because I2's text is "at most once" and the defect is **zero-vs-once**, not once-vs-twice — a step that should not have run at all is not an at-most-once breach; **W2-9** was corrected off I1, because the draft read "exactly one terminal state" as a *lower* bound and asserted a starved run was in **zero** terminal states, which fails on the entry's own evidence (both probes reached exactly one) and proves far too much, since I1 has no liveness bound and the reading would make I1 violated by every `Pending` row in the system; and **W2-7** was corrected for citing `docs/high-availability.md:414-415` as a broken contract when that sentence **sanctions** the observed behaviour ("the controller fails every `Running` run still claimed by that agent ID") — citing it would have meant citing the sentence that authorises the defect. **The one-line rule distilled for W3+: before citing an invariant or a doc passage, quote it verbatim, read the surrounding section, and check the direction — an invariant must be contradicted by its own text (not by its spirit), and a doc sentence must forbid what you observed rather than describe or permit it.** W2's own entries now model the discipline: every borderline attribution states what it is *not* claiming and why (W2-9's four NOT-claimed bullets, W2-8 Part F's five, W2-6's explicit I2 instrument gap), and W2-6's C2 observation goes further by recording the *declined* stronger reading in full so a reviewer can overrule it. That is the standard to hold.
- **Corrections made at this checkpoint.** *(This bullet formerly read "and one left for the operator"; that item has since been corrected too — see the end of the bullet — and a further batch of review corrections is recorded inline at the entries themselves rather than listed here: the asset-entry scope at `:1273`, the I1/I7 rule amendment at `:1484`, the two reclassifications at `:627`/`:652`, the superseded `interval / N` latency rule at `:529`/`:547`, the `docs/operations.md:53` contract limb at `:547`/`:575`/`:1490`/`:1498`, the withdrawn `high-availability.md:536` citation at `:702`, the `resources.md:324` antecedent at `:1007`, three severity re-arguments at `:972`/`:1008`/`:1165`, `plan:74`'s CORRECTED banner, and a cluster of citation fixes at `:736`, `:737`, `:800`, `:827`, `:877`, `:665`, `:990`, `:1161`, `:1303`.)* Two outright errors found while tallying were fixed in place: the W2-7 asset entry (`:1267`) said this wave's third-bucket count was **2**, which the file contradicts — W2-3's `inject.sh nginx-unblock` entry (`:769`) is a third, and the count is **3** (the plan's mirror of the same sentence is corrected too); and W2-8 Part F cited `plan:116` for the two-violations treatment, which is Task 1's `bulk-submit.sh` step — the correct citation is `plan:306`. A third was flagged at first as turning on intent and has since been **corrected in place**, because the correct reading was already stated in the flag itself and needed no intent call: W2-6's C2 observation (`:1089`) called the Pending-snapshot finding "**W2-4's** head-of-line finding" and cited `FINDINGS.md:779`, `:865`. Those are W2-4 entries about the *queued-run reaper*; the Pending-snapshot/head-of-line finding is **W2-9's** (`:1398`), which did not yet exist when W2-6 was written. `:1089` now points at `:1398`, and keeps the note that `:865` (a run that spends its grace blocked on a mutex while `Pending`) is the *cause* of W2-9's backlog rather than the snapshot finding itself.

### Tooling and asset notes carried forward

- **Runtime Postgres statement logging is now the campaign's primary instrument for anything a log line cannot answer, and its two traps are known.** `ALTER SYSTEM SET log_statement='all'` + `log_line_prefix='%m [%p] h=%h '` (**one `ALTER SYSTEM` per `psql -c`** — two in one `-c` is refused *silently*, per the W2-7 asset entry) yields a complete, timestamped, per-replica record with bind parameters for free (`log_parameter_max_length` is `-1` on `postgres:16-alpine`). It answered five different questions W2 could not otherwise reach: which replica ran a sweep, which cron occurrence a fire was for, how wide a two-statement window is, whether a reaper's SELECT preceded a claim, and how many candidates a snapshot examined. **Trap 1: two log forms.** pgx logs *parameterised* queries as `LOG: execute stmtcache_<hash>:` and arg-less ones as `LOG: statement:`, so a `statement:`-only matcher sees every reaper sweep and **zero** `DecideApproval` calls while looking like it works (W2-8 shipped that bug first; `w2-8/grid2.awk` handles both). **Trap 2: multi-line statements.** Only the first physical line carries the `%m [%p] h=%h` prefix, so continuation lines must be merged before classifying — this is exactly what mislabelled W2-1's `ListPendingRuns(git resolver)` row (the analyzer counted `TransitionPendingToQueued` instead; the distribution `2867/0/0` was the tell, because a per-replica job cannot produce a column of zeroes). **Cost:** ~40 MB / 26 min idle (W2-1), ~114 MB / 48 min (W2-6), ~2,300 lines/s from the scheduler alone at 200 ms ticks with 50 candidates (W2-9) — arm it in short windows and **verify `SHOW log_statement` in a session that did not issue the reload**, on both the arm and the revert.
- **Phase-locking beats repetition, and W2 proved it three times.** Because the per-tick jobs sit on a stable `(epoch mod interval)` grid drifting only ~12-13 ms/tick for as long as the replicas are not restarted, a race can be *placed* rather than repeated. Scoreboard: unaided repetition scored **0/20** (W2-6 Arm A, 2 ms window), **0/10** (W2-3 Arm D1, 1 ms window) and 3 wasted attempts on a shell busy-wait (W2-8, ~120 ms overshoot); phase-locked aiming scored **1/1** (W2-4 Part B5), **1/1 first attempt at ~13 ms accuracy against two independent grids** (W2-7 Part D), **6 of 7** trials landing where intended (W2-4 Part B) and **9/9 unambiguous outcomes** (W2-8 Part C). **The approval reaper's grid is the cheapest phase-lock target in the system**, because its `UPDATE` executes on every winning tick whether or not a row matches, so it is visible on a fully idle stack (period 60.012 s, drift +12 ms/min, cluster spread 8-19 ms). **The limit is the request pipeline, not the clock:** a single-process firer reaches 0.33-0.94 ms, but host→`UPDATE` latency is **49.9-64.3 ms with ~14 ms of jitter**, so aims inside ≈−55 ms of a sweep are a coin toss and −100 ms or wider wins reliably; move the client inside the compose network if you need better.
- **When the window is too narrow to hit, widen the fault instead of sharpening the aim — but say it is a stand-in.** Three scenarios did this and all three disclosed it: W2-5 refused a *URI* rather than racing an instant (`inject.sh steplock` + `nginx-steplink.conf`: an nginx exact-match `location` beats the prefix `location /`, giving a per-agent, per-endpoint 403); W2-6 held a `SELECT … FOR UPDATE` on the schedule row so the blocked write turned a 2 ms window into ~60 s; W2-6 Part D raised `P0001` from a `BEFORE UPDATE` trigger armed for exactly one check. **The discipline that makes these acceptable is uniform across the three:** state that it is a stand-in and not a reproduction of a specific production incident, enumerate the ordinary faults that reach the same state, say plainly that *how often that happens in production is not measured*, evidence the arm's effect **from inside** (a probe-confirmed 403, the injected `WARN`, agent-originated denials in the nginx log), and bound the armed window so everything after it is uninjected. W2-5's per-control injection-state bullet is the model for the failure this discipline prevents: one of its two controls turned out to sit inside a nominally armed window, and the entry says so and re-argues the differential from the control that does not.
- **Instruments and gotchas that cost wall time in W2 and should not cost it again.** `docker compose logs -t <one service>` still prefixes each line with the service name, so `awk '{print $1}'` yields `controller2-1` and not a timestamp — `--no-log-prefix` is required, and `--since` lags several seconds, so a read 3 s after an event can omit it and look like a skipped tick (wait ≥6 s). `docker compose start` takes **1.697 s** to an `agents` row because it re-resolves the `agent-enroll` dependency, against **0.400 s** for raw `docker start` — use the raw form and subtract the lead. The scheduler advisory key `0x65786364` is **1702388580** decimal; an earlier W2-6 gate used `1702389604` and read zero holders, so check against `SELECT objid FROM pg_locks WHERE locktype='advisory'` rather than trusting the conversion. **Agent lifecycle, three facts that interact:** bare `docker compose restart` gives ~1.013 s before SIGKILL (measured on an agent *holding* a run, whose drain is unbounded); an **idle** agent's SIGTERM path always reaches `Deregister` and issues a bare `DELETE FROM agents` (0.301-0.373 s after the host-clock SIGTERM), so a `restart` of an idle agent **also** deletes the shared row; and a standard agent runs 1 execution slot plus a **16-slot detached claim pool** (`--max-detached-concurrent`, default 16), each long-polling 30 s, so all 17 fire together on a fresh process and the cluster size drops by exactly the number of busy execution slots — **a usable busy-slot side channel**. Any task needing a `DELETE` should stop an *idle* agent; any task needing the row *kept* must not restart one.
- **Fixture and asset state at HEAD.** `spec.concurrency.mutex` remains the only working form (W1's lesson, unchanged). `schedule-every-minute.payload.json` goes to **`POST /api/v1/schedules`**, not `/api/v1/jobs`, and `test/edgecase/README.md` now says so with the 400 quoted. `inject.sh nginx-unblock` now takes no service argument (`a7ae25a`); the W1-5 runbook's `<any-service-arg>` workaround is harmless but obsolete. Two new W2 assets are reusable as-is: `compose/nginx-steplink.conf` + `inject.sh steplock <agent>` (URI-scoped 403; per-agent locations must be extended by hand for a third agent) and `compose/dupagent.override.yaml` (a fourth agent service with `--id agent1` on the same credential volume). `UpsertSchedule` omits `last_fired_at` from its `ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE` list, so `DELETE`+`POST` is the **only** way to return a schedule to its birth state — load-bearing for any scenario needing a fresh schedule, and the reason W2-6 Part D could claim no DB mutation.
- **Evidence archive.** `edgecase-evidence/w2/` now holds all nine scenarios' captures (~96 MB), verified against the scratchpad with `diff -r`: eight directories byte-identical, and `w2-2/partB-docker-events.txt` a verified byte-exact **prefix** of a source file that is still growing. That file is the one evidence-hygiene defect this wave left behind: the `docker events` stream started for W2-2 Part B at `02:24:47` was never stopped, so beyond roughly the first thousand lines the file is ~14 hours of unrelated container-lifecycle and healthcheck noise from every later scenario's stack. The cited evidence (lines 7, 11, 13 — `kill sig=15` / `kill sig=9` / `die exit=137`, epochs `1785345889.394` / `1785345890.407` / `1785345890.753`) is intact and matches the entry exactly. **The rule W2-8 Part F already adopted is the one to generalise: capture sampler hygiene rather than asserting it** — `jobs` plus a `ps` grep for stray `psql`/`curl`/`python`/`docker events`, at teardown, written to a file. W2-8 is the only scenario in the wave that did it.

## W3-4 — a bulk log append is a bare per-line loop with no transaction and no idempotency, so any failure the agent sees for a request the controller already committed is answered by re-sending it: a `Succeeded` run whose workload emitted 2002 lines was stored, and served, with 8493 — 4.24x — and the cheaper arm turned one 200 ms proxy-timeout **arm** into three separately timed-out requests, each leaving a committed prefix behind — 173/193/190 lines, permanently duplicated and out of order
- **Invariant:** I4 (log/artifact integrity) — quoted verbatim from `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-07-29-edge-case-testing-design.md:51`: "**Log/artifact integrity** — a Succeeded run's log line count matches what the workload emitted; no duplicates, no reordering; archives stay readable". **Three of the four clauses are breached and the fourth was not exercised.** The run is `Succeeded` (`w3-4/partB-surfacing.txt`: `"status":"Succeeded"`, step `burst` `"exitCode":0`), the count does **not** match what the workload emitted, there **are** duplicates, and there **is** reordering. The "archives stay readable" clause is **not claimed** — this rig has no object store, so nothing was archived (see the `NOT measured` bullet below). **I1, I2, I5, I6 and I7 all held** and each is worth stating because each is a plausible mis-attribution: I1 — every run reached exactly one terminal state, and I1's "duplicate" is scoped to *runs* from duplicate fires/webhooks, not to log rows; **I2 — nothing re-executed**, the step body ran once and the duplication is entirely in the transport of its output (a reviewer who sees "duplicate" and reaches for I2 has mis-read the mechanism; the `edge-logburst` fixture writes no side-effect file precisely so the two cannot be confused); I5 — the system returned to steady state on the first flush after the fault cleared, and `docs/high-availability.md` carries no bound on log delivery (`w3-4/docs-greps.txt`); I6 — no zombie; I7 — run status, step report and audit are all accurate, which is exactly why this is invisible.
- **Severity:** major
- **Repro:** `test/edgecase/scenarios/w3-4-bulk-append-duplication.md`. Stack: `test/ha` + `test/edgecase/compose/logfault.override.yaml` (nginx gains a regex `location` for `POST /api/v1/agents/{id}/runs/{runId}/steps/{n}/logs/bulk` with a runtime-writable include dir); injector `test/edgecase/tools/w3/w3-4-logfault.sh`; workload `test/edgecase/workloads/logburst.payload.json` (job `edge-logburst`, emits exactly **2002** lines: `burst-begin` + `burst-1`…`burst-2000` + `burst-end`). Executed 2026-07-30 `23:35:26Z – 23:50:06Z` on branch `plan/edge-case-w3`.
- **Observed:**
  - **The control first, because the whole entry is a comparison against it.** Run `a644004c` (`23:36:18.600Z → 23:36:57.417Z`, `Succeeded`): **2002** rows, **zero** `(line, ts)` groups with `count(*)>1`, **zero** burst indices out of position, `min(seq)=1 max(seq)=2002 span=2002` (`w3-4/partA-control.txt`). **It is called uninjected on evidence, not on the clock**: all four of its `logs/bulk` requests appear in the nginx access log as `arm=none … status=204` (`w3-4/partA-nginx.txt`). A second, unplanned control arrived later — run `78788e0d` — and it also measured exactly **2002 / 0 / 0** (`w3-4/consolidated.txt`). **Its uninjected status is a wall-clock argument, not a bracket, and the difference is stated here rather than glossed:** an earlier version of this bullet claimed "all four requests `arm=none 204`" for it, and **no nginx access-log line for this run was captured to the scratchpad at all** — `grep -rE "arm=(none|truncate|lostack) target=" $SCRATCH` returns lines for `a644004c` (4), `e87666b7` (10) and `16683d6e` (22) and **nothing** for `78788e0d`, `a0b22da5` or `f7d619ac`. What *is* captured is timing: the run was created `23:45:42.921404+00` (`w3-4/consolidated.txt`) and the arm reload completed at `23:46:06` (`w3-4/partD2-arm.txt`) — **23 s later**, against a fixture whose burst begins only after a `sleep 8` (`workloads/logburst.payload.json`), so the burst had been delivered long before the fault existed. That reasoning is sound but it is the wall clock, which is exactly what this campaign forbids calling a bracket; the claim therefore carries the runbook's required label *(observed live, raw output not captured to scratchpad)*.
  - **Arm 1, "truncate" — one 200 ms proxy timeout, three committed prefixes.** `proxy_read_timeout` on the bulk location alone was cut to 200 ms. Run `e87666b7` (created `23:40:22.184Z`, armed `23:40:27.904Z`, cleared `23:40:36.686Z`, ended `23:41:00.695Z` `Succeeded`). The nginx access log (`w3-4/partB-nginx-attempt1.txt`) classifies every request individually:

    ```
    23:40:24 204 arm=none     ustatus=204 rt=0.005 reqlen=481     (burst-begin, 1 line)
    23:40:30 504 arm=truncate ustatus=504 rt=0.201 reqlen=61273   <- batch A, attempt 1
    23:40:31 504 arm=truncate ustatus=504 rt=0.200 reqlen=61273   <- batch A, attempt 2
    23:40:31 504 arm=truncate ustatus=504 rt=0.202 reqlen=230293  <- batch B, attempt 1
    23:40:32 502 arm=truncate (x2)   no live upstreams — injection artifact, see Notes
    23:40:34 502 arm=truncate (x2)
    23:40:37 204 arm=none     ustatus=204 rt=0.502 reqlen=61273   <- batch A finally lands
    23:40:38 204 arm=none     ustatus=204 rt=1.747 reqlen=230293  <- batch B finally lands
    ```

    and the **controller's own request log records those three requests as `status:500, duration_ms:200`** (`w3-4/partB-surfacing.txt`) — i.e. `handleAgentLogBulk` took the error branch at `internal/controller/api_agent.go:726-729` after nginx had already answered the agent 504 and closed the upstream connection, cancelling `r.Context()` (`:725`).
  - **The prefix lengths, which are the "partial commit" half.** Grouping by `(line, ts)` and by copy count (`w3-4/partB-dup.txt`) resolves each aborted request exactly:

    | batch (`ts`) | distinct lines | copies=3 | copies=2 | copies=1 | rows |
    |---|---|---|---|---|---|
    | `23:40:30.668372` (batch A) | 421 | `burst-1..173` | `174..193` | `194..421` | 787 |
    | `23:40:31.073242` (batch B) | 1579 | — | `burst-422..611` | `612..2000` | 1769 |

    So attempt 1 of batch A committed a prefix of **173** lines before the cancellation, attempt 2 committed **193**, and the single truncated attempt at batch B committed **190** (`611-422+1`). Total rows `1 + 787 + 1769 + 1 = 2558` against an emitted 2002 — a surplus of **556**, which is exactly `173+193+190` (derived arithmetic; every input is in `w3-4/partB-dup.txt`). **383** `(line, ts)` groups had `count(*)>1`; **2383** burst indices were out of position.
  - **Postgres confirms there was never a transaction to roll back.** With `log_statement='all'` armed for that window only (`w3-4/gate-g6-arm.txt`, verified in a fresh session: `all` / `%m [%p] h=%h`), the capture `w3-4/partB-pglog-raw.txt` (71,852 lines) contains **2558** `INSERT INTO logs` statements — *exactly* the number of rows the run ended up with — and **0** `rollback` (`w3-4/partB-pglog-analysis.txt`). Every insert Postgres was asked to perform committed; the abort landed cleanly *between* statements, leaving the prefix behind with no compensation of any kind. The only `ERROR:` in the whole window was a typo in one of the harness's own probe queries.
  - **Arm 2, "lostack" — the whole batch commits and the agent is told it failed.** nginx mirrored each bulk request to a real controller (precontent phase, so strictly before the content phase) while pointing the client-facing leg at a dead upstream. Run `16683d6e` (created `23:43:02.231Z`, armed `23:43:07.942Z`, cleared `23:43:13.752Z`, ended `23:43:40.787Z` `Succeeded`). **15 requests logged `502 arm=lostack target=blackhole`** to the agent, and the controllers logged **204** for every one of them (`w3-4/partC-dup.txt`; the 502s carry `rt≈0.66–0.82 s` because nginx waited for the mirror subrequest to finish before finalising, which is also the proof the mirror delivered). Result: **8493** rows against 2002 emitted — **4.24x** (derived) — **2000** duplicate groups, **8490** of 8491 burst lines out of position, and a copy-count histogram of `1:2, 2:135, 3:492, 4:541, 5:411, 6:421`. `burst-1` exists **six** times, all with `ts=23:43:10.76151+00` and seqs `{4562,4563,4569,4585,9053,11053}`.
  - **The duplicates are byte-identical except for `seq`, and that is not an accident.** `Timestamp` is stamped once per batch, outside the per-line loop (`internal/agent/runner.go:376`), and re-used verbatim on every retry, while `seq` is server-assigned per INSERT. **`logs.seq` is one global sequence** — `CREATE SEQUENCE public.logs_seq_seq` (`internal/store/migrations/001_init.up.sql:103-109`), `DEFAULT nextval(...)` (`:320`), `PRIMARY KEY (run_id, seq)` (`:384`), one covering index at `:557`, and no second sequence anywhere in `internal/store/migrations/` — so a re-sent copy always lands at a **higher** seq than the original. That is why it is invisible to every `seq`-based dedupe in the system (notably SSE's `seq > lastSeq` cursor) and why it appears *after* lines it originally preceded. Group on `(line, ts)`; never on `seq`, and never treat a seq span as a line count.
  - **It is not merely stored — every read surface serves it.** SSE `GET /api/v1/runs/{id}/events` re-reads from the DB by `seq` and ignores the NOTIFY payload (`internal/controller/sse.go:117-120`), so the duplicate is delivered as an ordinary new event. Replay on the duplicated run `a0b22da5`: **2773** `log` events for 2773 DB rows, `burst-1` delivered **4** times with one identical timestamp and four different seqs; the control replay gives **2002** events and `burst-1` exactly once (`w3-4/partD-sse-replay.txt`). A **live** capture, taken straight from `controller1` so it could not be disturbed by the nginx reloads, gave **2626** events for 2626 rows with `burst-1` four times (`w3-4/partD3-sse-live.txt`). `GET /runs/{id}/logs` returns `burst-1` three times for `e87666b7`, and `GET /runs/{id}/logs/stats` reports `{"count":2558,...}` as the authoritative line count against the control's `{"count":2002,...}` (`w3-4/partD4-logapi.txt`).
  - **Attempt count, as the runbook required.** The truncate arm was attempted **4** times against a cap of **10**; **3** produced mid-batch partial commits — `e87666b7` (3 prefixes: 173/193/190), `a0b22da5` (4 prefixes: 181/190/203 on batch A, 197 on batch B) and `f7d619ac` (4 prefixes: 167/181/183 on batch A, 93 on batch B) — i.e. **11 partial commits over 3 successful attempts** (`w3-4/consolidated.txt`). The one miss (`78788e0d`) was an operator timing error, not a code-side non-reproduction — but say on what evidence: the wall-clock argument above *(observed live, raw output not captured to scratchpad)*, **not** a per-request bracket, because none was captured for it.
    - **Which of the six runs carry a captured nginx bracket, stated once so nothing downstream has to guess.** Three do — `a644004c` (control), `e87666b7` (truncate) and `16683d6e` (lostack), in `w3-4/partA-nginx.txt`, `w3-4/partB-nginx-attempt1.txt` and `w3-4/partC-dup.txt`. Three do not — `a0b22da5`, `78788e0d` and `f7d619ac`; their arm attributions in the runbook's results table are wall-clock plus, for the two truncate hits, the self-evidencing shape of the result (a mid-batch committed prefix is only producible by the truncate arm). **No headline number depends on the three unbracketed runs:** 2002, 8493, 4.24x and 173/193/190 all come from `a644004c`, `16683d6e` and `e87666b7` respectively — the three that are fully bracketed. The unbracketed runs contribute only the attempt tally and the corroborating row counts.
    - This was **reproduced live**; the code-read fallback the runbook budgeted for was not needed.
  - **NOT measured — and an earlier version of this entry mis-sold it as the severity discriminator, which understated the finding.** That version called the archive limb "the only thing separating this finding from a critical". It is not, and the reason is worth stating because it is the substance of the finding: **the archive limb creates no new corruption.** It faithfully preserves content that is already corrupt and is already the only copy; its marginal addition is durability past a log trim plus a coverage check that would be defeated by the same inflated count. That extends the corruption's **lifetime**, not its **kind** — and symmetrically, re-running it against Garage cannot move the severity either, because faithfully archiving corrupt data is not a second defect. What the re-run closes is an **unmeasured limb of the entry**, which is worth ten minutes on its own terms. Whether the duplication survives into the object-store archive is **code-read only**: `test/ha` configures no object store, so all three controllers logged `no object store configured — log archival disabled` and `run_log_archives` held **0** rows for the whole session (`w3-4/consolidated.txt`), which means the archiver never started (`cmd/controller/main.go:399`). The code-read argument is that `archiveRunLogs` encodes whatever `TailLogs` returns (`internal/controller/archiver.go:81-92`) and records `line_count`/`max_seq` from that same slice (`:106` → `internal/store/postgres.go:1519-1528`), applying no de-duplication, so log-trim's coverage check would compare the inflated count against itself and pass, and after a trim the corrupted archive would be the only surviving copy. **That is an argument, not a measurement.** It should be re-run against the Garage rig W3 Task 3 adds — to close an unmeasured limb, not to settle the severity.
  - **AMENDED AFTER W3 TASK 3 — the archive limb is now MEASURED, and the code-read argument above holds exactly as written. The severity is unchanged, as the bullet above predicted.** Re-run at `2026-07-31T01:59:43Z–02:00:34Z` against the `test/ha` rig that Task 3 gave an object store (`w3-infra/w3-4-partE-rerun.txt`). Two parts:
    - **Fidelity, on untouched data.** For run `46e21b34` the DB held 4 lines, `run_log_archives` recorded `line_count=4 max_seq=4`, and `runs/46e21b34-…/logs.ndjson` in Garage held exactly 4 ndjson records carrying the same seqs, timestamps and text. The archive is a byte-faithful serialization of what `TailLogs` returns.
    - **No de-duplication, measured.** 4 W3-4-shaped duplicates were planted (`INSERT INTO logs … SELECT … FROM logs WHERE run_id=…` — same line, same `ts`, new seq, giving groups `{1,17} {2,18} {3,19} {4,20}`), the archive row and object were removed to make the run eligible again, and the archiver's next 30 s tick re-archived it: `line_count=8 max_seq=20`, and the object contains **all 8** records including the 4 duplicates, in seq order with the copies trailing. So the archiver applies no de-duplication and no ordering repair, and log-trim's coverage check compares the inflated count against the same inflated set and passes. After a trim the corrupted archive is the only surviving copy.
    - **METHOD, labelled rather than glossed.** The duplicate was planted with SQL, not re-produced through the nginx `lostack` arm. W3-4 already *measured* that the arm puts exactly this shape into `logs`; what was never measured is what the archiver does with it, and `archiveRunLogs` reads through `TailLogs` and cannot tell how a row got there. So the planting is faithful **for this claim** and synthetic **for the mechanism** — the mechanism limb remains the one W3-4 measured live. Do not cite this sub-bullet as an independent reproduction of the duplication.
- **Expected:** **the docs are silent, not contradicted.** `grep -rn -iE "duplicat|idempot|exactly once|at least once|atomic" docs/*.md` plus the log-delivery and ordering greps (`w3-4/docs-greps.txt`) turn up nothing promising that a bulk append is atomic, that a failed bulk request committed nothing, or that log lines are de-duplicated or ordered; `docs/high-availability.md:191-192` documents the SSE stream's LISTEN/NOTIFY implementation without any delivery guarantee. The nearest passage must be read carefully rather than cited as a sanction (the W2-7 lesson): `docs/troubleshooting.md:889-899` explains that "the agent buffers log lines locally and ships them to the controller in batches; if the controller is unreachable … the agent keeps the batches it couldn't send in a bounded in-memory queue" — it **authorises retry** and says nothing whatever about what the controller may already have committed from the attempt the agent scored as a failure. Retry is only safe against an idempotent endpoint, and this one has no client id, no batch id, no nonce, no `ON CONFLICT` and no unique constraint on any content column. So the finding rests on **I4**, not on a broken published promise.
- **Severity reasoning — major, and the argument is precedent consistency, not the archive limb. Read this as a floor, not a ceiling.** An earlier version of this entry made the code-read archive limb the major/critical discriminator; that reasoning is withdrawn (see the `NOT measured` bullet above for why it does not survive scrutiny). What actually holds: **this file's critical band is "data loss / silent corruption / security" (`:6-8`), and the "silent corruption" disjunct is arguably met today, with no archive required.** Permanently duplicated *and reordered* stored content, served by every read surface, with `GET /runs/{id}/logs/stats` reporting the inflated `count` as the authoritative line count and **nothing anywhere recording what the workload emitted** — the companion observation entry's own conclusion is that it is "undetectable from inside the system". Note that this file's nearest comparable severity argument, W2-9's (`:1440`), justifies major-not-critical with "**nothing is corrupted, nothing is duplicated**" — the exact two things that *are* true here. It is filed **major** anyway, for one reason stated plainly: **calibration against the rest of the campaign.** This file holds **72** entries across W0 (8), W1 (19), W2 (43) and W3-4 (2) and has filed **zero** criticals; in particular the **W1-2 major** (`:179`) is *silent, permanent log loss with no drop marker* — the mirror-image defect in the same `LogPusher` machinery — and it was filed major. Filing W3-4 critical while W1-2 sits at major would make the two bands mean different things in the same document. **The escalation call is therefore left explicitly to the operator**, who is the party this file says prioritizes (`:3-4`): if the "silent corruption" disjunct is read literally, this entry meets it, and W1-2 probably does too. What is *not* in doubt and is not softened by the major label: no run state is wrong and no side effect repeats, but the stored log is permanently and silently **wrong** — a surplus rather than a loss — served to every reader, with no marker of any kind, triggered by an ordinary transient failure of one HTTP request.
- **Notes:** the mechanism is four sites. `handleAgentLogBulk` loops and calls `s.store.AppendLog` once per line (`internal/controller/api_agent.go:719-739`, call at `:725`); each call is a standalone autocommitted `p.pool.QueryRow` (`internal/store/postgres.go:918-926`); a mid-loop error does `http.Error(...); return` from inside the loop (`api_agent.go:726-729`) with no rollback and **no indication of how far it got**; and the agent re-sends the entire batch on any error (`internal/agent/runner.go:358-366`, enqueue at `:390-392`, any status ≥ 400 becoming an error at `internal/agent/client.go:107-109`). **Four independent fixes close it and they are not equivalent:** (a) wrap the loop in a transaction — makes the request atomic but not idempotent, so a lost ack still duplicates; (b) give `api.LogAppendRequest` a batch id or per-line client sequence plus a unique constraint — the only fix that also covers the mirror/lost-ack shape; (c) have the 500 body report how many lines committed so the agent resumes rather than restarts — cheap, but only helps the truncate shape; (d) (a) and (b) together. **Two injection artifacts, recorded so a re-run is not surprised and so neither is mistaken for a product finding:** (i) three consecutive upstream timeouts tripped `max_fails=1` on all three controllers in `test/ha/nginx.conf`, briefly ejecting the whole pool and yielding `502 no live upstreams` for unrelated agent traffic (visible as the agent's one `cancel poller: get run failed` WARN) — **but this one is only an artifact of *this* run, not of the rig, and an earlier version of this note wrongly implied otherwise by naming `test/ha/nginx.conf` alone.** `docs/high-availability.md:253-254` names that exact file as "a complete working example" of the reference LB settings and `:258` recommends `server controller:8080 max_fails=1 fail_timeout=5s` **explicitly**, so a doc-following production deployment behaves identically. **The docs gap, recorded and not filed:** the stated rationale at `:249-251` is a **dead (SIGKILL'd)** controller — nothing there says that with `max_fails=1` any *correlated slow-response* event (one shared-dependency stall, one slow query class, one injected timeout) ejects **all** replicas at once and 502s healthy traffic, which is the failure mode observed here. It is **not filed as a violation**: the trigger here was a deliberately injected 200 ms timeout, and `:248-249` does make LB tuning "the **operator's LB responsibility**", so the passage scopes the tuning away rather than promising anything about it (the W2-7 scoping check, applied and passing).
