Whether the missing caveat is worth a docs fix is the **operator's call**; (ii) `worker_shutdown_timeout 1s` in `nginx-logfault.conf` — added deliberately to answer W2-5's "reload may not reach an already-connected agent" — also severs in-flight long-poll claims and any SSE stream crossing a reload, which is why the live SSE capture had to be taken directly from `controller1`. **Cross-references, not re-filings — this is the fourth distinct shape in the same `LogPusher`/`AppendLog` machinery and it overlaps none of the other three.** F9 (`unified-cd-audit-findings`) is *loss at the 1 MiB `p.pending` byte cap*, never approached here (`grep -c "log line(s) dropped"` over both agents' logs for the whole session returns **0**, `w3-4/partB-surfacing.txt`). The **W1-2 major I4** is *loss at the bounded 5 s step-end `Flush` budget*. The **W1-6 observation** is *quadratic request amplification with no data loss*. **W3-4 is no loss at all and a permanent surplus instead**, and it is the only one of the four in which the controller, not the agent, is the party that gets it wrong.

## W3-4 — observation: the duplication is completely silent — run, step report, agent log and controller log are all clean, no drop marker can fire for this class, and the one API that reports a line count reports the inflated one as authoritative
- **Invariant:** none violated; the *absence* of an **I7** hook (`docs/superpowers/specs/2026-07-29-edge-case-testing-design.md:54`, "run status, approval status, and audit rows never contradict each other or reality") is the substance of the entry. Every status surface told the truth: the run really did succeed and the step really did exit 0. Recorded because the diagnosability gap is what turns the violation above from a bug into one nobody will ever attribute, and because a fix has to know which surfaces exist.
- **Severity:** minor (observation)
- **Repro:** `test/edgecase/scenarios/w3-4-bulk-append-duplication.md`, step B8, read against run `e87666b7` (2558 rows for 2002 emitted lines). Evidence: `w3-4/partB-surfacing.txt`, `w3-4/partD4-logapi.txt`, `w3-4/partC-dup.txt`, `w3-4/consolidated.txt`.
- **Observed:**
  - **Run-scoped surfaces: accurate and empty of explanation.** `GET /api/v1/runs/{id}` → `"status":"Succeeded"` with no diagnostic field of any kind; `GET /api/v1/runs/{id}/steps` → `[{"index":0,"name":"burst","status":"Succeeded","exitCode":0,…}]`. Nothing on the run records that three of its log requests were answered 500 by a controller.
  - **The agent says nothing at all.** Across the whole armed window the only agent log line naming the run besides `"running"` was `{"level":"WARN","msg":"cancel poller: get run failed","error":"http 502: response omitted"}`, which is collateral from the injection artifact, not from the log path. `LogPusher.flushLocked` logs **nothing** on failure — it silently queues the batch and re-sends. 556 surplus rows, zero agent log lines.
  - **The drop marker cannot fire for this class, by construction.** `[N log line(s) dropped: controller unreachable]` (`internal/agent/runner.go:396-424`) is gated on `p.droppedLines > 0`, incremented **only** by `appendPendingLocked`'s 1 MiB byte-cap eviction (`:432`). Nothing was dropped here, so the counter stays 0 — `grep -c "log line(s) dropped"` across both agents for the whole session: **0**. The marker is a *loss* marker; there is no *surplus* marker and no notion of one.
  - **The controller's only trace is an INFO access line.** Three `{"level":"INFO","msg":"http request",…,"status":500,"duration_ms":200}` entries, no `WARN`, no `ERROR`, and — the point — nothing anywhere recording that the 500 was returned *after* rows had been committed. An operator reading controller logs sees three transient 500s on a log endpoint, which is what a healthy system looks like during a blip.
  - **The one count-bearing API reports the corrupted number as fact.** `GET /api/v1/runs/{id}/logs/stats` → `{"count":2558,"maxSeq":4560,"minSeq":2003}` for the injected run against `{"count":2002,"maxSeq":2002,"minSeq":1}` for the control. There is no "expected" count to compare against — nothing records how many lines the workload emitted — so **the corruption is undetectable from inside the system**; it was detectable here only because the fixture's line contents are self-indexing.
  - **What was NOT inspected.** The **WebUI was not opened** for this scenario; every surface above was read via the HTTP API, SSE and the database. Anyone acting on the word "completely" in this title should close that gap first — it is one page load.
- **Expected:** as-designed on every limb — a 500 on a log append is a transient condition the agent is *supposed* to retry, and the retry is *supposed* to be invisible. The observation is that the union of these surfaces cannot distinguish "the batch was retried successfully" from "the batch was committed three times", and only the second is permanent.
- **Notes:** the cheapest detector available today is comparing `logs/stats`'s `count` against a duplicate query the product does not ship (`GROUP BY line, ts HAVING count(*)>1`) — i.e. an operator has to already suspect it. If fix (c) from the entry above is taken (report the committed-line count in the 500 body), it should be paired with a controller-side `WARN` naming the run and the prefix length: that single missing line is what made this invisible.

## W3-3 — observation: the mixed-KEK blast radius is exactly the documented one and nothing else — 8 of 24 secret-using runs failed, every fetch attributed per request to the replica that served it, controller3 failing 8/8 and the other two 0/16, with no LB retry, no upstream ejection, and all 51 runs terminal exactly once
- **Invariant:** none violated. **I1 (run accounting)** — `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-07-29-edge-case-testing-design.md:48`, quoted verbatim: "**Run accounting** — every API-accepted run reaches exactly one terminal state; no phantom runs from duplicate fires/webhooks" — **held on all 51 runs**: 28 `Succeeded` + 23 `Failed`, zero non-terminal at any census and zero at teardown (`w3-3/consolidated.txt`, `w3-3/teardown.txt`), and 51 triggers produced exactly 51 rows. **I7 (state display consistency)** — `:54` — **held on the run-status limb**: every `Failed` run really did fail and every `Succeeded` run really did succeed (the separate `Pending`-step entry below is a different limb of I7 and is filed on its own). **I2 not exercised** — the DAG never starts on the failing path (`internal/agent/orchestrator.go:188` returns before `RunPipeline`), so the step body ran **zero** times, and per the W2 correction a zero-vs-once shape does not violate at-most-once. **I3 not exercised** (no mutex in the fixture). **I4 not exercised** — its scope clause is "a Succeeded run's", and the failing runs emit nothing; this rig also has no object store. **I5 does not fit and the reason is worth stating**: a wrong key is a persistent misconfiguration, not a fault to recover *from*, so there is no steady state to return to and no documented bound to measure against.
- **Severity:** minor (observation)
- **Repro:** `test/edgecase/scenarios/w3-3-mixed-kek.md`. Stack: `test/ha` + `test/edgecase/compose/mixedkek.override.yaml` (controller3 alone gets `UNIFIED_CONTROLLER_KEY_FILE: /run/secrets/kek-b`, a second `unified-cli keygen` key mounted at a new target; `test/ha/` unedited). Workload `test/edgecase/workloads/secret-user.payload.json` (job `edge-secret-user`, step env `EDGE_KEK_PROBE: "{{ .Secrets.EDGE_KEK_PROBE }}"`, so `len(SecretsNeeded) > 0`). Executed 2026-07-31 `00:36:31Z – 00:50:22Z` on branch `plan/edge-case-w3`.
- **Observed:**
  - **The baseline first, because the whole entry is a comparison against it.** With all three replicas sharing `test/ha/kek`, 6 runs went 6/6 `Succeeded` with `secret-len=27` (`w3-3/gate-g3-baseline.txt`). **It is called a baseline on per-replica evidence, not on probability**: `w3-3/gate-g3-fetchlog.txt` shows all three replicas served a `POST /api/v1/agents/*/secrets/fetch` with `status 200` — controller1 x3, controller2 x2, **controller3 x1** (at `00:37:13.998`, for run `d4044268`). controller3 could read the secret before the overlay and could not after it; nothing else about it changed.
  - **The overlay took, and controller3 started cleanly.** `restarts=0`, up 53 s at the gate, `/readyz` **200** (`w3-3/gate-g5-rotation.txt`). Inside the container the two key files differ (`cmp` -> 1) and `kek-b` is 64 hex characters after `TrimSpace` (`w3-3/gate-g4-keyfiles.txt`) — a **valid** key file carrying the **wrong** key, which is the state the scenario needs.
  - **8 of 24 = 33.3 %** (`w3-3/partA-runs.txt`). **Every one of the 24 fetches is bracketed per request** from the controllers' own access log, which carries `method`/`path`/`status` (`internal/controller/server.go:180-186`) with `docker compose logs` supplying the replica (`w3-3/partA-fetchlog.txt`): 24 fetch lines for 24 runs — **one per run, no retry**, confirming that `client.FetchSecrets` is called once and is not wrapped in `retryUntilSuccess` (`orchestrator.go:161-162`). Split: **controller1 3 x 200, controller2 13 x 200, controller3 8 x 500**. No claim here rests on a wall clock.
  - **Read the per-replica result, not the fraction.** The 24 fetches split **3 / 13 / 8**, not 8/8/8, because the fetch is one request among many the agents send through the LB (30 s long-poll claims, heartbeats, log appends, step reports) so its upstream is effectively arbitrary. The exact result is therefore **controller3 failed 8 of the 8 it served and the other two failed 0 of 16**; 8/24 landing precisely on 1/3 is partly coincidence, and at N = 24 a true 1/3 has a wide binomial spread. **Reporting this as a measured "33 % failure rate" would overstate what the number is** (derived: 8/24).
  - **nginx did not retry and did not eject — verified, not assumed.** `proxy_next_upstream error timeout http_502 http_503 http_504` (`test/ha/nginx.conf:24`) omits `http_500`, and per nginx's own upstream documentation only `error`/`timeout`/`invalid_header` plus the statuses actually listed count as unsuccessful attempts, so a decrypt 500 neither fails over nor counts toward `max_fails=1` (`:6-8`). Live: **zero** `no live upstreams` and **zero** `temporarily disabled` lines in **1,606** nginx log lines across the whole session (`w3-3/partA-nginx.txt`, `w3-3/consolidated.txt`), and controller3 kept serving — 118 requests of every kind after the recreate. **The strong test is phase 4** (see the key-identity entry below): **nine consecutive 500s spread across all three upstreams** and still no ejection. **This is the exact opposite of W3-4's experience with the same `max_fails=1` config, and the difference is the status code, not the rig** — W3-4's arm produced 504s, which *are* in the list.
  - **Each failure is instant and clean.** `duration_ms` 1-2 on every 500; run creation -> terminal was 0.2-1.1 s throughout (`w3-3/partA-runs.txt`). No hang, no partial execution, no orphan.
- **Expected:** exactly this. `docs/high-availability.md:204-206` says "Every replica must be given the **same** key... a replica started with a different key cannot read secrets written by another"; `:225` and the checklist at `:544` repeat it. **The docs are explicit, correct, and were confirmed on every limb this entry touches.** Recorded as an observation because a documented, warned-about misconfiguration behaving as documented is a conformance result — but the *blast radius* is nowhere quantified in the docs, and "one replica in three silently fails every secret-using run, with no LB retry to mask it" is the operational shape an operator would want when judging how urgent the checklist item is.
- **Notes:** the fixture and the overlay are new campaign assets and both worked first time — `POST /api/v1/jobs` returned **200** and `POST /api/v1/secrets/` returned **204** on attempt 1 (`w3-3/gate-g2-apply.txt`), after offline verification through the real `dsl.Parse` (`KnownFields(true)` + `Validate()`) and through `dsl.ReferencedSecretNames`, the same call `collectSecretNames` makes at `internal/controller/api_agent.go:466` (`w3-3/fixture-dslparse.txt`: `len(SecretsNeeded)=1 names=[EDGE_KEK_PROBE]`). **The trailing slash on `/api/v1/secrets/` is load-bearing** — the sub-router registers `"/"` (`internal/controller/server.go:385-386`). **Not one API 500 was seen on any trigger or gate command** across 51 triggers, so the rig's intermittent-500 allowance was never spent; a re-run should not assume that. **Two pre-execution corrections to the plan's facts block**, both recorded in the runbook and neither changing a conclusion: the KEK mount citation `docker-compose.ha.yaml:22-31` names neither the `&ctrl` anchor (`:15`) nor the mount (`:25`), and `proxy_next_upstream` is at `nginx.conf:24`, not `:23`.

## W3-3 — observation: 23 secret-decrypt failures produced **zero** controller `ERROR` lines while the agent logged 23 `ERROR`s that say nothing — the wrong-key case is the only one of three decrypt classes that stays at `Warn`, the one message that names the fix is the one that cannot fire, and the agent discards the 500 body so the run's own log reads only `http 500: response omitted`
- **Invariant:** none violated. Filed against the **absence** of an **I7** hook — `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-07-29-edge-case-testing-design.md:54`, verbatim: "**State display consistency** — run status, approval status, and audit rows never contradict each other or reality". Nothing here states an untruth: the run really failed, and `http 500` really was the status. The observation is that the union of every surface **cannot name the cause**, which is a diagnosability gap rather than a contradiction. **This entry corrects the campaign plan.** `docs/superpowers/plans/2026-07-30-edge-case-campaign-w3.md:84` asserts the failing path is "unusually well-instrumented" because "**The reason is visible in the run's own logs**"; execution disproves it, and that disproof is the entry's core.
- **Severity:** minor (observation)
- **Repro:** `test/edgecase/scenarios/w3-3-mixed-kek.md` Part B, read against run `13333015-04c0-4419-824d-bd0584503341` (triggered `00:39:23.202Z`, fetch served by controller3 at `00:39:24.232462760Z`, `Failed` at `00:39:24.236983+00`). Evidence: `w3-3/partB-runlogs.txt`, `w3-3/partB-controller.txt`, `w3-3/partB-surfaces.txt`, `w3-3/consolidated.txt`.
- **Observed:**
  - **The run's own log line, all 23 times, is content-free.** `SELECT step_index, stream, ts, line FROM logs WHERE run_id=...` returns **exactly one row**: `-1 | stderr | 2026-07-31 00:39:24.232946+00 | fetch secrets for run 13333015-...: http 500: response omitted`, and `GET /api/v1/runs/{id}/logs` serves the same string to a user (`w3-3/partB-runlogs.txt`). A `SELECT DISTINCT` over the line text of **all 23** failed runs returns **one** distinct value — `fetch secrets for run <id>: http 500: response omitted` (`w3-3/partB-surfaces.txt`). **The mechanism:** `Client.do` replaces the body of every response >= 400 with the literal `"response omitted"` (`internal/agent/client.go:107-108`; `HTTPError.Error()` formats it at `:26-28`), so the message the controller built at `internal/controller/api_secrets.go:142` is destroyed before `orchestrator.go:175-184` can log it. The path is well-*structured* — one line, right stream, right `stepIndex`, written before `FinishRun` — and carries **no diagnostic content at all**.
  - **The HTTP 500 body was not captured on the wire and could not be** — the endpoint is agent-authenticated and run-guarded, and the only client that reaches it throws the body away. **Derived** from two captured inputs, the format string at `api_secrets.go:142` and the `err.Error()` text the controller's own `slog.Warn` logs verbatim, the body is `decrypt EDGE_KEK_PROBE: decrypt dek: cipher: message authentication failed`. **That it is unobservable from the agent side is part of the finding, not a gap in the measurement.**
  - **The controller side, and the plan's open question settled.** controller3 logged, immediately before each `status:500` access line (`w3-3/partB-controller.txt`):

        {"time":"2026-07-31T00:39:24.232412536Z","level":"WARN","msg":"secret decrypt failed","site":"agent-fetch","id":"EDGE_KEK_PROBE","error":"decrypt dek: cipher: message authentication failed"}
        {"time":"2026-07-31T00:39:24.232462760Z","level":"INFO","msg":"http request","method":"POST","path":"/api/v1/agents/agent1/secrets/fetch","status":500,"duration_ms":2,...}

    The plan listed "the exact stdlib error text after `decrypt <name>: decrypt dek:`" as **not established** and predicted `cipher: message authentication failed`. **Confirmed, with one refinement:** `err.Error()` itself is `decrypt dek: cipher: message authentication failed` and carries **no** secret name — the name in the HTTP body comes from the handler's own `"decrypt "+name+": "` prefix, not from `secrets.Decrypt` (which only names the secret in the `ErrBindingMismatch` branch, `internal/secrets/crypto.go:79`).
  - **The level census is the headline.** Across the whole session, **all three controllers logged `ERROR` = 0** while carrying 23 `secret decrypt failed` WARNs between them (8 on controller3 in the mixed window — captured before the repair recreate erased them — plus 8 on controller1, 5 on controller2 and 2 on controller3 in the inverted and repaired phases). The **agents** logged **23 `ERROR`s** (agent1 10, agent2 13) and **zero** lines containing `decrypt`, `kek` or `encryption key` (`w3-3/consolidated.txt`). **The severity and the information sit on opposite sides of the socket:** the party that knows why logs it at `Warn`; the party that logs at `Error` does not know why.
  - **The asymmetry, with both halves stated.** `logSecretDecryptFailure` (`internal/controller/api_secrets.go:161-173`) has three branches:

    | case | level | keys logged | diagnostic sentence |
    |---|---|---|---|
    | binding mismatch (`:162-165`) | `Error` | `site`, `id` | yes — "possible ciphertext tampering or substitution" |
    | provider mismatch (`:167-170`) | `Error` | `site`, `id` | yes — "check UNIFIED_KMS_URI / UNIFIED_CONTROLLER_KEY_FILE" |
    | **wrong local KEK (`:172`)** — **measured** | **`Warn`** | `site`, `id`, **`error`** | no |

    Only the third row is measured; the other two are **code-read** (producing them needs a second key *provider* or a tampered ciphertext, neither of which this scenario builds). **The wrong-key case is the quietest by level and the most informative by content** — an entry reporting only the first half overstates the gap, and one reporting only the second half misses it. **The sharpest point, and it is exact:** the branch whose message tells the operator to "check `UNIFIED_KMS_URI` / `UNIFIED_CONTROLLER_KEY_FILE`" is unreachable for the most likely way to get a wrong `UNIFIED_CONTROLLER_KEY_FILE`, because a wrong *local* key still carries the matching `local:` provider tag and so fails one layer deeper, in `aesGCMDecrypt` rather than the prefix check (`internal/secrets/keymanager.go:70-73`, wrapped as `decrypt dek: %w` at `crypto.go:65`).
  - **Every other surface, enumerated so "silent" is scoped honestly** (`w3-3/partB-surfaces.txt`). `GET /api/v1/runs/{id}` gives `"status":"Failed"` with no diagnostic field. `GET /api/v1/secrets/` gives the name, present and unremarkable, because that handler never attempts a decrypt (`api_secrets.go:48-61`). `/readyz` and `/healthz` on controller3 give **200** both, because `/readyz` checks `shuttingDown` and `store.Ping` only (`internal/controller/server.go:318-333`). The startup line is the same on the wrong-key replica as on the right-key ones except for the file path — `{"level":"INFO","msg":"encryption key loaded","source":"key file /run/secrets/kek-b"}` versus `.../run/secrets/kek` (`w3-3/gate-g4-startup.txt`) — and **had the overlay re-pointed the existing mount instead of adding a new one, the two lines would have been byte-identical.** **The WebUI was NOT opened** for this scenario; every surface above was read via the HTTP API and the database.
- **Expected:** as-designed on every limb. The `"response omitted"` policy is a deliberate secrets-hygiene measure (a 4xx/5xx body from an arbitrary intermediary should not be copied into a log), and `logSecretDecryptFailure`'s doc comment (`api_secrets.go:150-160`) explains that a binding mismatch is "logged distinctly and at a raised level, since it signals ciphertext substitution, tampering, or corruption rather than an ordinary decrypt error". **The docs are silent, not contradicted:** the survey in `w3-3/docs-greps.txt` (`grep -rn -iE "same key|different key|key file|kek|key id|key version|rotat"` and `"unrecoverable|undecryptable|escrow|re-encrypt"` over `docs/*.md`) finds no promise about how a key mismatch is *reported*, only about the requirement itself. So this rests on nothing but the diagnosability argument, which is why it is an observation.
- **Notes:** the cheapest fix is one line — make the fall-through at `:172` an `slog.Error` with a sentence naming `UNIFIED_CONTROLLER_KEY_FILE`, since a GCM failure in the **DEK-unwrap** layer with a **matching** provider tag has exactly two causes (wrong local key, or corrupted `encrypted_dek`) and both deserve `Error`. A second, independent fix: let `Client.do` preserve the body for a **controller-originated** 5xx on a non-secret-bearing endpoint, or have the agent log the status *and* a controller-supplied error code. **Cross-references, not re-filings.** This is not the memory item `unified-cd dev ephemeral-key gotcha` (a single-replica *temporal* mismatch from `UNIFIED_DEV_MODE=1` regenerating a key each restart, which the code warns about loudly at startup, `internal/config/keysource.go:98-99`); this is a multi-replica *spatial* mismatch that produces **no startup warning at all**. It is also not W2-7's credential-file entry, which is about agent credentials rather than the KEK.

## W3-3 — `docs/secrets.md:420` prescribes an unconditional remedy for `decrypt` errors in HA whose stated cause is false and whose fix does not work: applied verbatim it left **9 of 9 runs failing on all three replicas**, because nothing anywhere carries key identity — 67 ciphertext bytes fully accounted for with no room for one, six table columns, no startup check, `/readyz` 200 — so a secret written while the cluster is mixed is destroyed permanently and no surface can enumerate the damage
- **Invariant:** **none — this entry is filed on the documented-contract limb**, which `FINDINGS.md:478-479` states as: an entry is a **violation** when observed behavior contradicts an invariant (I1-I7) **or a documented contract**, where the contract limb "requires a *published* promise — an exported API field, a schema column, or **a statement in `docs/`**". The published promise is `docs/secrets.md:420`, the sole HA row of the Secrets Management Guide's Troubleshooting table: "| `decrypt` errors in HA setup | Replicas were given different key files, or different `UNIFIED_KMS_URI` values. **Give every replica the identical key file (or the same KMS URI).** |" That is a *prescribed fix* for exactly the symptom this scenario produced, stated unconditionally — no caveat in the row, none in its section, and no pointer to the re-`secret set` remedy `docs/operations.md:67` names for the key-loss case. **It was applied verbatim at `00:46:03.977Z` and the symptom persisted at 100 % — 9 of 9 runs `Failed`, with decrypt WARNs on all three replicas** (`w3-3/partD3-repair.txt`). After the repair the row's stated *cause* is false as well: the replicas were **not** given different key files — all three held the identical `test/ha/kek`, controller3's own startup line reading `{"msg":"encryption key loaded","source":"key file /run/secrets/kek"}` at `00:46:06.668Z` with `kek-b` no longer mounted, and the other two untouched throughout. An operator who follows the row does exactly the right thing, sees no change at all, and is handed nothing else to try. **Separately, and still correct: the campaign's invariant set has no secret-store integrity clause.** I4 covers "log/artifact integrity" (`docs/superpowers/specs/2026-07-29-edge-case-testing-design.md:51`) and nothing covers secrets; I7 (`:54`) enumerates "run status, approval status, and audit rows", so the secrets list API reporting a permanently-undecryptable secret as present is adjacent to that but outside its stated scope, and **stretching it would repeat the mis-attribution W2-9 had withdrawn in review**. That gap is **no longer this entry's disposition** — the contract limb carries it — but it is independently correct and is **flagged for the W3 checkpoint as an invariant-coverage gap.**
- **Severity:** **major** — argued against this file's own bands (`:6-8`) rather than defaulted; see the severity reasoning below, where **critical is stated as arguable and the escalation is left explicitly to the operator.**
- **Repro:** `test/edgecase/scenarios/w3-3-mixed-kek.md` Parts C and D. Evidence: `w3-3/partC-ciphertext.txt`, `w3-3/gate-g4-startup.txt`, `w3-3/partD-writes.txt`, `w3-3/partD-runs.txt`, `w3-3/partD3-repair.txt`, `w3-3/consolidated.txt`, and the **untruncated** doc survey `w3-3/docs-greps.txt` (with the superseded capture preserved beside it as `w3-3/docs-greps-ORIGINAL-TRUNCATED.txt` — see the last bullet under Expected).
- **Observed:**
  - **The stored bytes, read from the database rather than argued from the struct.** For `EDGE_KEK_PROBE`, the hex of the first 8 bytes of `encrypted_dek` is **`026c6f63616c3a52`**, i.e. `0x02` then the six ASCII bytes `local:` then straight into the GCM nonce; decoding bytes 2-7 as text prints **`local:`** (`w3-3/partC-ciphertext.txt`). That is a **format version** (`secrets.CryptoVersion`, `internal/secrets/crypto.go:14`, applied by `withVersion` at `:84-88`) and a **provider tag** (`localKeyPrefix`, `internal/secrets/keymanager.go:25`). Neither identifies *which* local key.
  - **The byte budget is the argument, and it is stronger than "no identifier was found".** `length(encrypted_dek)` = **67**, and `1 + 6 + 12 + 32 + 16 = 67` — version, `local:`, GCM nonce, wrapped 32-byte DEK, GCM tag. **Every byte is accounted for; there is nowhere for a key id to be.** The value blob is `length(ciphertext)` = **56** for a 27-byte secret (`1 + 12 + 27 + 16`) and its prefix is `02d158e3ef435d69` — `0x02` then immediately the nonce, with **no provider tag at all**, because only the DEK is wrapped by the `KeyManager`.
  - **The row has six columns and none of them is a key identity.** The live table is `id, name, encrypted_dek, ciphertext, created_at, updated_at`, with `secrets_pkey` on `id` and `secrets_name_key UNIQUE (name)`. **This corrects a fact this scenario carried in from the migration**: `001_init.up.sql:247-256` still describes `scope`/`scope_ref` columns that `016_drop_secret_scope.up.sql:13-18` dropped, and the query written against the migration errored with a missing-column error (the only `ERROR:` in the capture — not a product fault).
  - **`LocalKeyManager` is `struct{ kek []byte }`** (`internal/secrets/keymanager.go:32-34`) — one field, no id, no version, no label. Combined with the bytes above, a replica holding key B **cannot distinguish** "this blob was written with key A" from "this blob is corrupt": the provider tag *matches*, so `ErrProviderMismatch` — the one error that names the key configuration — is unreachable (`keymanager.go:70-73`), and what it gets instead is a bare GCM authentication failure, which `crypto.go:20-24`'s own comment says "cannot say which of them was wrong".
  - **No other surface probes the key either.** Startup does no consistency check of any kind: `KeySource.Resolve` reads the file, checks 64 hex characters, returns (`internal/config/keysource.go:85-89`, `:192-217`), and `main.go:287-301` logs `encryption key loaded` with `Description` = the literal `"key file " + path`. Live, the wrong-key replica's startup output is **structurally identical** to the other two — same three lines, same order, differing only in the path string (`w3-3/gate-g4-startup.txt`). `/readyz` is `shuttingDown` + `store.Ping` (`internal/controller/server.go:318-333`) and returned **200** on the wrong-key replica throughout. `GET /api/v1/secrets` returns names only and never decrypts (`api_secrets.go:48-61`).
  - **The counterfactual is in the codebase's own comment.** `localKeyPrefix`'s comment (`keymanager.go:21-24`) says Vault Transit "already self-describes its ciphertext as `vault:v1:...`" and that matching that convention "lets a provider mismatch be reported precisely instead of surfacing as an opaque AES-GCM authentication failure". **The local provider adopted the self-describing prefix and not the `v1` part of it** — the argument for a key id is made by the code that declined to add one.
  - **THE CONSEQUENCE, WHICH IS WHY THIS IS NOT A TIDINESS COMPLAINT — the write side inverts, then the repair destroys.** Three phases, each bracketed per request:
    1. **A secret written while the cluster is mixed lands on a coin flip nothing reports.** `POST /api/v1/secrets/` encrypts with whichever replica answers (`api_secrets.go:34-43`) and returns `204`. Three writes of the same name went to controller1 (`00:44:11.131`), controller2 (`00:44:13.779`) and controller3 (`00:44:16.402`) — all `204`, all indistinguishable to the caller (`w3-3/partD-writes.txt`). The **last** one is the stored blob (`updated_at 00:44:16.398553+00`), and it was written with the wrong key.
    2. **The polarity inverts, and nothing marks the row.** 12 runs against that blob: **controller3 6/6 succeeded** (`secret-len=30`, the new value) while **controller1 0/5 and controller2 0/1 failed** — the mirror image of Part A, produced by the same rig, the same job and the same secret *name* (`w3-3/partD-runs.txt`). **Both blobs are rows in the same table with the same `0x02local:` prefix**; nothing distinguishes the readable one from the poisoned one.
    3. **Repairing the misconfiguration exactly as the docs prescribe destroys the secret.** controller3 was put back on the shared `test/ha/kek` (`{"msg":"encryption key loaded","source":"key file /run/secrets/kek"}`, `kek-b` no longer mounted) and 9 runs were triggered: **9 of 9 `Failed`**, with all 9 fetches bracketed — controller1 3 x 500, controller2 4 x 500, controller3 2 x 500, and 3 + 4 + 2 decrypt WARNs to match (`w3-3/partD3-repair.txt`). The cluster is now correctly configured, consistent, healthy on every probe — and the secret is unreadable by every replica, forever. **`GET /api/v1/secrets/` still lists it as present**, `/readyz` is 200 on all three, and there is no way to enumerate which rows are affected because no row records which key wrote it.
- **Expected:** **one limb of `docs/` is contradicted and two are not, and keeping them apart is load-bearing.**
  - **CONTRADICTED — `docs/secrets.md:420`.** It prescribes, without condition, "Give every replica the identical key file (or the same KMS URI)" as the fix for "`decrypt` errors in HA setup". Every replica *was* given the identical key file, at `00:46:03.977Z`, and the decrypt errors continued on **all three** — 9/9 runs `Failed`, 3 + 4 + 2 fetch 500s, 3 + 4 + 2 decrypt WARNs. A prescribed remedy that does not remedy is a contradicted contract, and the row's stated cause is additionally false of the repaired cluster. What the row would have to say to be true: restoring the identical key file fixes future traffic and every secret written under the shared key, and does **nothing** for any secret written during the mixed window — those are permanently undecryptable and the only remedy is to re-`secret set` them, which is the same instruction `operations.md:67` already gives for key loss and which nothing routes an HA operator to.
  - **NOT contradicted — `docs/high-availability.md:204-206`.** It promises only that "a replica started with a different key cannot **read** secrets written by another" — a statement about the read direction and about the mixed state. It does not promise, and nothing else does either, that repairing the mismatch restores readability. The write-side inversion (phase 2 above) and the absence of any detection surface simply fall outside it.
  - **NOT contradicted — `docs/high-availability.md:218` and `docs/operations.md:67`.** These name **key loss** as the condition for unrecoverability, and here **the key was never lost**: `test/ha/kek` was present and correct on every replica throughout and is present at the end. **A tempting reading is available and is deliberately not taken:** that they state an "only if", which execution contradicts. Per the W2-7 lesson — read the surrounding section before filing — those passages are backup-and-recovery guidance about the key file, not an exhaustive enumeration of ways a secret can become undecryptable, so the honest verdict on them is silence rather than contradiction. **`:420` is a different kind of statement** — a prescribed fix for an observed symptom, not a scope claim — which is why it carries the violation and they do not.
  - **How this limb was nearly missed, recorded because the campaign's runbooks exist for exactly this.** The doc survey backing the original "the docs are silent" verdict was captured through `head` and stored **40** of the first grep's **55** hits, with the truncation undisclosed. `docs/secrets.md:420` is hit **#55** — the last line of the full output, and the only one that changes the disposition. It was caught in review by re-running the identical command and comparing line counts. `w3-3/docs-greps.txt` has been **re-captured untruncated**, now records its own hit counts inline (55 and 3), and carries a header stating what was wrong with it; the original is preserved verbatim as `w3-3/docs-greps-ORIGINAL-TRUNCATED.txt` because the way it failed is part of the deliverable. **Rule for later waves: a survey used to support a "the docs are silent" verdict must record its own line count, and must never be piped through `head`.**
- **Severity reasoning — argued on this file's own bands (`:6-8`), with the escalation left to the operator.** The outcome is **permanent, silent data destruction** — the "data loss / silent corruption" disjunct of the **critical** band — reached without any fault injection, with **no marker on the affected row, no way to enumerate affected rows, and no health surface that would ever go red**, and with a documented remedy (re-`secret set` every value) that an operator has no way to know they need to apply — indeed a *different*, ineffective remedy is what `docs/` hands them. What holds it below critical in practice, and both are real: it requires an operator to have already violated an explicit, boldfaced, thrice-repeated checklist item, so nothing is destroyed *without* that precondition; and the blast radius is bounded by which secrets were written during the mixed window. **Major is the defensible landing spot; critical is arguable — said here as arguable rather than settled, so the operator, who is the party this file says prioritizes (`:3-4`), makes that call against their own exposure.** (An earlier version of this entry filed it **minor** on the ground that "the campaign's convention is that observations are minor", citing `FINDINGS.md:481`. Both halves were wrong: `:481` is about how entry titles and Severity lines **label** a classification and says nothing whatever about severity, and the entry is not an observation. The miscitation is dropped.) **The single change that would move the severity most is not a code fix but a detector** — see Notes.
- **Notes:** **the fix is cheap and the code already knows the pattern.** (a) Put a key identifier in the wrapped-DEK prefix — `local:v1:<8-byte-fingerprint-of-kek>:` — exactly the `vault:v1:` convention `keymanager.go:21-24` cites approvingly; that alone turns an opaque GCM failure into "this blob was written with a different key" and makes affected rows enumerable with one `SELECT`. (b) Add a startup self-check: decrypt one arbitrary `secrets` row and log an `Error` (or fail readiness) if it fails — the wrong-key replica currently starts, passes `/readyz`, and serves traffic with a **structurally identical** startup log to a healthy one. (c) Have `GET /api/v1/secrets` optionally verify decryptability so the list API can report a poisoned row. **Fix (a) is what makes (b) and (c) precise; without it they can only say "something is wrong", which is exactly the position the wrong-key replica is in today.** **(d) The cheapest fix of all, and the one that closes the limb this entry is filed on: correct `docs/secrets.md:420`.** Its cause clause is false of a repaired cluster and its fix clause is incomplete. It needs to say that restoring the identical key file fixes future traffic and every secret written under the shared key, that any secret written while the cluster was mixed is **permanently undecryptable**, and that the only remedy for those is to re-`secret set` them — the instruction `operations.md:67` already gives for key loss. Until (a) exists there is no way to tell the operator *which* secrets those are, so the doc fix and the code fix are complements, not alternatives. **Cross-reference, not a re-filing:** the W3-3 diagnosability observation above is the *reporting* half of the same misconfiguration (levels, messages, the discarded 500 body); this entry is the *identity and durability* half. They share a cause and neither is the other.

## W3-3 — a run that fails before its first step report displays that step as `Pending` forever on a `Failed` run — 23 of 23 runs, permanent, and not covered by the fix that closed the W1-5 case. **Reached by this scenario, not caused by it**
- **Invariant:** **I7 (state display consistency)** — quoted verbatim from `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-07-29-edge-case-testing-design.md:54`: "**State display consistency** — run status, approval status, and audit rows never contradict each other or reality". `GET /api/v1/runs/{id}/steps` on a terminal `Failed` run returns `[{"index":0,"stageIndex":0,"name":"usesecret","status":"Pending","kind":"run","section":"main"}]`. `Pending` means "has not run **yet**"; on a run that has reached a terminal state the step will never run, so the step view and the run status contradict each other and the step view contradicts reality. **This is the same limb of I7 as the W1-5 minor violation** (W1 checkpoint: "a reaped/orphan-failed run's in-flight `step_reports` row stays `Running`... contradicting the run's own terminal status"), which was accepted as a violation — but it is a **different mechanism** and is **not** closed by that fix.
- **Severity:** minor
- **Repro:** `test/edgecase/scenarios/w3-3-mixed-kek.md` Part B5. Any `edge-secret-user` run whose secrets fetch is served by a wrong-key replica; read `GET /api/v1/runs/{id}/steps`. Evidence: `w3-3/partB-surfaces.txt`, `w3-3/consolidated.txt`.
- **Observed:**
  - **23 of 23** failed runs this session have **zero** `step_reports` rows and a terminal status — `SELECT count(*) FROM runs r WHERE r.status='Failed' AND NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM step_reports s WHERE s.run_id=r.id)` returns **23**, against 23 total `Failed` runs (`w3-3/consolidated.txt`).
  - **The contrast that makes it a display fault rather than a data fault.** For the terminal `Failed` run `0ddf1d79`: `GET /api/v1/runs/{id}` gives `"status":"Failed"`, while `GET /api/v1/runs/{id}/steps` gives `"status":"Pending"` with no `startedAt`, no `endedAt`, no `exitCode`. The `Succeeded` control run `85a34465` returns `"status":"Succeeded","exitCode":0,"startedAt":...,"endedAt":...` from the same endpoint (`w3-3/partB-surfaces.txt`).
  - **The mechanism, and why it is permanent.** `handleGetRunSteps` overlays reported steps onto the planned list "so not-yet-run steps show as Pending" (`internal/controller/api_runs.go:301-308`); `mergedRunSteps` emits the planned entry verbatim when no report exists (`internal/controller/planned_steps.go:63-73`), and `plannedSteps` hard-codes `Status: "Pending"` (`:28`). **Nothing ever revisits it.** The one path that terminalizes step reports, `MarkRunStepsInterrupted` (`internal/store/postgres.go:810-819`), is called from exactly one site — the stuck-run reaper (`internal/controller/stuckrun_reaper.go:81`) — and updates only rows that are `Running`. Here the agent's own `FinishRun(Failed)` is the terminalizing write, it never touches `step_reports`, and there is no row to update in any case.
  - **The class is "runs that terminate before their first step report", not "mixed-KEK runs".** The secrets-fetch failure (`internal/agent/orchestrator.go:161-188`) is one member; a run reaped before its first step started is another. **This scenario reached it; the mixed KEK is not its cause.**
- **Expected:** the code's own comment at `api_runs.go:301` declares the `Pending` overlay deliberate — and that is a real counterargument, which is why it is stated rather than omitted. **The strongest form of it is elsewhere and is stated here too:** `MarkRunStepsInterrupted`'s doc comment (`internal/store/postgres.go:810-813`) says it outright — "Only Running is affected — not-yet-started steps have no `step_reports` row (the UI's `Pending` is filled in from the run plan)" — i.e. the synthesized `Pending` is a known, named property of the design, not an oversight, and the one path that terminalizes step rows deliberately declines to touch it. **Neither counterargument defeats the entry, for two different reasons.** The `postgres.go` comment is **not a published contract** under `FINDINGS.md:479`, which requires "an exported API field, a schema column, or a statement in `docs/`" and excludes a helper's own doc comment: `MarkRunStepsInterrupted` is exported, but only within `internal/store`, so it is visible to no one outside this repository and appears in no document. It is evidence about **intent**, and intent is not a promise to the operator — the same distinction the W1-6 `lastAuthenticatedAt` entry turns on, applied in the opposite direction. And the `api_runs.go:301` comment does not scope this case away: the comment says "so **not-yet-run** steps show as Pending", which is a statement about a run still in progress. Neither it nor any document in `docs/` says what a *terminal* run's unreached steps should display, so **the docs are silent, not contradicted**, and the entry rests on I7 alone. Filed as a violation rather than an observation on **precedent consistency**: W1-5's structurally identical entry (a terminal run whose step report contradicts it) was filed as a minor I7 violation and accepted.
- **Notes:** the natural fix is symmetric with #113's — when a run is finished by any path, project unreached planned steps as `Skipped`/`NotRun` rather than `Pending`, or have `handleGetRunSteps` suppress the `Pending` overlay once the run is terminal (a read-side fix needing no migration and no write path). **Cross-reference, not a re-filing:** the W1-5 minor I7 was *a stale `Running` report row on the orphan-fail path*, fixed on main by PR #113 / `2c018fe` (`MarkRunStepsInterrupted` from `failOrphanedRun`); **this is no report row at all plus a synthesized `Pending`, on the agent's own `FinishRun` path**, which #113 does not touch. Do not close this against that fix.

## W3-infra — observation: the reference HA load-balancer configuration inherits nginx's 1 MiB default body cap, so every artifact upload over 1 MiB dies at the LB with a 413, and no user-facing doc anywhere names the knob
- **Invariant:** **none violated, and — after review — no documented contract contradicted either.** An earlier version of this entry was a **major violation** on the contract limb, resting on `docs/high-availability.md:254`'s "a complete working example"; that call is **withdrawn** and the reasoning for withdrawing it is set out in full under Expected, because it is the more instructive half of this entry. **The I5 citation that version carried is also dropped**: I5 is *bounded recovery*, nothing here is a recovery path, no fault is injected, and the version that cited it already conceded in the same breath that "the run failure itself is a plain functional failure rather than an invariant breach" — which is an argument against the citation, not for it.
- **Severity:** **minor (observation)** — argued on this file's own bands (`:6-8`), where minor is "diagnosability, **docs gap**, cosmetic". This is the docs-gap disjunct exactly: with the contract claim withdrawn, what is left is that the one knob an operator needs is named in **no** user-facing document (surveyed below, 0 hits), while the reference file that would have supplied it silently does not. Everything that would push it up is absent — the failure is loud, immediate and legible (the operator does get the number 413, see (ii) under Observed), it is fully deterministic with no fault injection, nothing is lost or silently corrupted, and the repair is a single nginx directive. Nothing here is "incorrect visible behavior" in the major sense once the contract claim is gone: nginx is doing precisely what nginx documents its default to be.
- **Repro:** Not a scenario — reached while building the W3 infrastructure (Task 3 Step 5 baseline). Plain `test/ha` rig, no overlay, plus the Garage service Task 3 added. Apply `test/edgecase/workloads/artifact-large.payload.json` and trigger `edge-artifact-large` (64 MiB, default `size_mb`). Evidence: **`w3-infra/step5-413-recapture.txt`** (the authoritative capture; see the note at the end of Observed), `w3-infra/step5-bigbody-and-latency-recapture.txt`, `w3-infra/step5-baseline.txt`, `w3-infra/docs-survey-413.txt`.
- **Observed:** the upload never reaches a controller — it dies at the load balancer. Quoted verbatim from `w3-infra/step5-413-recapture.txt` (run `30e5a434-fc5e-4c47-917e-88fcfe34e6c1`, `2026-07-31T02:33:28Z`, `docker compose logs nginx` and `logs agent1 agent2`, trimmed only where marked `…`):
  ```
  nginx-1  | 2026/07/31 02:33:28 [error] 30#30: *16 client intended to send too large chunked body:
           | 1015835+32768 bytes, client: 172.20.0.9, server: , request: "PUT /api/v1/runs/30e5a434-
           | fc5e-4c47-917e-88fcfe34e6c1/artifacts/big-blob HTTP/1.1", host: "nginx:8080"
  nginx-1  | 172.20.0.9 - - [31/Jul/2026:02:33:28 +0000] "PUT /api/v1/runs/30e5a434-…/artifacts/
           | big-blob HTTP/1.1" 413 183 "-" "Go-http-client/1.1"
  agent2-1 | {"time":"2026-07-31T02:33:28.207067842Z","level":"ERROR","msg":"upload-artifact failed",
           |  "step":"upload_blob","error":"upload artifact http 413"}
  agent2-1 | {"time":"2026-07-31T02:33:28.211200734Z","level":"ERROR","msg":"upload artifact failed",
           |  "step":"upload_blob","error":"upload-artifact \"big-blob\": upload artifact http 413"}
  ```
  and the `step_reports` rows for the same run, from the same capture:
  ```
   step_index |  step_name   |  status   | exit_code | secs
  ------------+--------------+-----------+-----------+-------
            0 | make_blob    | Succeeded |         0 | 0.484
            1 | upload_blob  | Failed    |         0 | 0.015
            2 | after_upload | Skipped   |           |
  ```
  with the `runs` row `edge-artifact-large | Failed | agent2`. The cause is that `test/ha/nginx.conf` sets no `client_max_body_size` and so inherits nginx's 1 MiB default — the file has 29 lines and the string appears nowhere in it. With `test/edgecase/compose/bigbody.override.yaml` (`client_max_body_size 0` on the artifact location and nothing else changed) the identical fixture succeeds: run `39629cb6-bfb8-4662-8847-e251ac4abbbf` uploaded 64 MiB with `upload_blob Succeeded` in **0.749 s** and the LB logging `"PUT …/artifacts/big-blob HTTP/1.1" 204`, the object landing in Garage as `artifacts/39629cb6-…/big-blob.tar.gz` at 64 MiB (`w3-infra/step5-bigbody-and-latency-recapture.txt`, phase 2). Scaling holds: 256 MiB gave `upload_blob Succeeded | 3.060` (`w3-infra/step5-baseline.txt:89-94`).

  **Two details that sharpen this rather than soften it.** (i) nginx handled the *chunked* body fine — the error message counts 1015835 bytes already received before refusing, so the transfer encoding was never the problem. (ii) The failure is one of the few agent-side errors that is actually legible, because `UploadArtifact` hand-builds its request and returns `fmt.Errorf("upload artifact http %d", ...)` (`internal/agent/client.go:368-369`; an earlier version of this entry cited `:367-368`, one line short) instead of going through `Client.do`, which would have replaced the body with `"response omitted"` (`client.go:107-108`). An operator does get the number 413. What no surface gives them is which knob produces it.

  **Provenance of this block, recorded because the campaign's evidence rule is explicit and an earlier version of this entry broke it.** The first version quoted the four log lines and the `step_reports` row above against the *original* baseline run `c1a06b5f-fa3b-497c-8a49-c1fc3a5ac557` (`2026-07-31T01:55:16Z`) — but **none of that text was in any capture.** `w3-infra/step5-baseline.txt` holds only an author-written summary line (`:71`, ending "see above", where nothing appeared above); the `step_reports` query for `c1a06b5f` **errored** at `:62-65` (it was written against the pre-`016` migration column names and was fixed only for the later 256 MiB run at `:89-94`); and the `0.863 s` figure quoted for the 64 MiB success at `:84` was likewise a bare summary line with no query output. The finding itself was corroborated in that capture — `c1a06b5f` is `Failed` at `:61` and carries a 291-byte log archive at `:117` against 433-436 bytes for the successful runs — so this was a **presentation failure, not a fabrication**; but verbatim-looking log text and a duration must be backed by a capture, so the rig was rebuilt and **the whole block above was re-captured from scratch**, reproducing the original byte-for-byte on the message text (same `1015835+32768 bytes`, same `413 183`, same two agent `ERROR` lines). The two durations differ, as durations do across runs: `upload_blob Failed` at **0.015 s** here versus the uncaptured 0.012 s claimed before, and the 64 MiB success at **0.749 s** here versus the uncaptured 0.863 s. **The re-captured numbers are the ones this entry now states**; the originals are recorded in this paragraph and nowhere else.
- **Expected — this is where the earlier version went wrong, so the withdrawal is argued rather than announced.** The passage is `docs/high-availability.md:253-254`: "Configure the LB to fail fast and re-try a healthy upstream. For nginx, the reference settings (**see `test/ha/nginx.conf` for a complete working example**) are:" — followed by three failover knobs at `:256-263` (`proxy_connect_timeout`, `max_fails`/`fail_timeout`, `proxy_next_upstream`). The earlier version read "a complete working example" as an unqualified claim about the file, made it the contract limb under `FINDINGS.md:478-479`, and filed a **major violation**. **Three things defeat that, and the third is decisive.**
  - **Grammar.** The parenthetical modifies *"the reference settings"*, not the file at large: "the reference settings (see `test/ha/nginx.conf` for a complete working example) **are:** [three failover directives]". The natural reading is "a complete working example **of these settings**", and that reading is not strained — it is the one the sentence's own subject supplies.
  - **Section scope.** The whole passage sits under `### Upstream failover tuning` (`:245`) and is introduced by "Delivering that sub-second failover to clients is the **operator's LB responsibility**" (`:248-249`). The earlier version answered this by saying the scoping "is about *failover* tuning" — but its own affirmative claim rested on a phrase inside **that same failover-tuning sentence**. A passage cannot be read narrowly to defeat the counter-argument and broadly to carry the claim.
  - **This branch had already ruled on this exact sentence, and ruled the other way.** `FINDINGS.md:1563` — the Notes of the W3-4 entry, on this branch, filed before this one — glosses it as "'a complete working example' **of the reference LB settings**" and then declines to file on it: "It is **not filed as a violation**: … `:248-249` does make LB tuning 'the **operator's LB responsibility**', so the passage scopes the tuning away rather than promising anything about it (the W2-7 scoping check, applied and passing)." That entry gave up a `max_fails=1` docs gap on precisely this ground. Filing a violation here on the broad reading, without so much as citing `:1563`, would have made the branch say two incompatible things about one sentence. **It is withdrawn.**
  - **`docs/operations.md:45` does not rescue it either, and the reason matters.** Its sentence — "a length-sensitive proxy sitting in front of the controller must be configured to allow chunked request bodies" — is **true**; it is merely incomplete, and an omission is not a contradiction. So it cannot carry a contract violation. **The irony it produces survives intact as an observation, and is the sharpest thing in this entry:** the project *anticipated* a proxy problem on exactly this path, wrote a warning about it, and named the one thing nginx does correctly by default (chunked bodies) while missing the one that breaks it (body size).
  - **What is left, and it is real:** behaviour matched nginx's documented default, the docs are **silent** rather than contradicted, and the silence is total — see the survey below. That is an observation about a docs gap, which is what this entry now is.

  **The full survey, per the W3-3 method rule (no `head`, counts printed). It is unchanged by the reclassification and it is what the entry now rests on.** `grep -rn -iE "client_max_body|body size|body_size|\b413\b|request entity too large" docs/` over the whole tree returns **1** hit, and it is a false positive (a line number `:413` cited inside a superpowers plan). Over the user-facing `docs/*.md` it returns **0**. Every nginx mention in `docs/*.md` is **5** lines, all listed in the capture, and none concerns request size. There is **1** doc line in the entire tree that thinks about a proxy and artifact uploads together — `docs/operations.md:45`, quoted and weighed in the bullets above: "The agent→controller artifact PUT uses chunked transfer-encoding (no `Content-Length`), so a length-sensitive proxy sitting in front of the controller must be configured to allow chunked request bodies." **The reviewer re-derived this survey untruncated and got the same result**; the zero is what the entry rests on now.
- **Notes:** `test/ha/nginx.conf` is deliberately **not** changed on this branch — it is the documented reference configuration, so leaving it as shipped keeps this reproducible on the plain rig; `test/edgecase/compose/nginx-bigbody.conf` + `bigbody.override.yaml` are the campaign's way past it and are required by every W3 scenario that uploads an artifact. **The docs-gap recommendation is unchanged by the reclassification** — an entry can be an observation and still be the reason a doc gets a line. Cheapest fixes, not equivalent: (a) add `client_max_body_size 0;` to `test/ha/nginx.conf` and to the `docs/high-availability.md:256-263` tuning list — one line each, and it makes the shipped example complete for the artifact path as well as the failover path; (b) extend `docs/operations.md:45`'s existing proxy sentence to name body size alongside chunked encoding, which is where an operator debugging an artifact failure would actually look, and which is the fix this entry would put first; (c) have the controller publish a recommended LB stanza. **(b) is the one that closes the actual gap**, because it does not depend on any reading of `:253-254`: the sentence there is already about proxies and artifact uploads, it is already incomplete, and completing it costs four words. **`proxy_request_buffering off`** belongs in any fix too, for a different reason: with buffering on (the default) nginx spools the whole artifact to disk before opening the upstream request, which is a silent latency and disk tax on every upload, and it is what makes the overlay necessary rather than optional for W3-6's timing.

## W3-infra — observation: an agent with the `docker` CLI but no reachable daemon advertises the `container` capability, so the scheduler reports a container job as schedulable on it and the run fails at claim time instead
- **Invariant:** none violated. `EvaluateSchedulability` is advisory and the run does fail cleanly with an actionable message. Recorded because it is a permanent mis-advertisement produced by the project's own shipped agent image, and because it settles the W3 plan's open question about sidecars.
- **Severity:** minor (observation)
- **Repro:** Not a scenario — reached while settling the W3 plan's "whether the HA agents can run **sidecars** at all" question (Task 3 Step 2(b)). Plain `test/ha` rig. Evidence: `w3-infra/q-b-sidecars.txt`.
- **Observed:** `GET /api/v1/agents` on the HA rig returns, for both agents, `"capabilities":["native","container"]`. Inside `unified-cd-ha-agent1-1`: `/var/run/docker.sock` does not exist, `docker info` answers `Cannot connect to the Docker daemon at unix:///var/run/docker.sock`, and the container's only mount is the `agent-credentials` volume. The agent can therefore run no container at all, yet advertises that it can. The mechanism is one line: `ociCLI.Available()` is `_, err := lookPath(r.bin); return err == nil` (`internal/runtime/ocicli.go:42-45`) — a binary-on-PATH check with no daemon probe — reached from `Detect` (`internal/runtime/runtime.go:133-150`) and turned into the advertised set by `agentCapabilities` (`internal/agent/agent.go:139-145`). `docker/agent.Dockerfile:17` installs `docker-cli` into the shipped agent image, so **every** agent built from it and run without a socket mount is in this state permanently. The consequence is in `capsCover`/`EvaluateSchedulability` (`internal/controller/schedulability.go:38`, `:86`): the "no registered agent provides capability" reason at `:90` cannot fire, so a container job is reported `Satisfiable` and then fails per-run at `internal/agent/agent.go:593-597` with `isolated job requires a container runtime (docker/podman/nerdctl); mark the job native: true or route it via agentSelector` (the `failClaim` call is at `:597`; an earlier version of this entry cited `:593-596` and stopped one line short of it).
- **Expected:** no doc promises daemon-level detection, which is why this is filed as an observation rather than a violation. **The counter-argument is genuine and is why the severity is minor:** a daemon can die at any moment, so no startup probe is sound, the per-run failure message is specific and actionable, and the shipped image is plainly *meant* to be run with a socket mount. What is still worth the operator's attention is that the pre-flight answer and the run-time answer disagree in a fully static, permanently reproducible way, and that the disagreement is invisible until a job fails.
- **Notes:** a daemon ping in `Available()` behind a short timeout would close it at the cost of one exec per detection, which happens once per agent process. **For the campaign, this is the answer to W3's sidecar question and it is doubly negative:** the campaign envelope uses `native: true`, which sets `pod == nil` (`internal/agent/agent.go:593-623`), and `hostBackend.SetMasker` creates the sidecar log pump **only** when `b.pod != nil` (`internal/agent/backend_host.go:362-368`) — so no sidecar exists on a native claim regardless of the runtime. Dropping `native: true` does not help either, because the claim then needs a runtime and there is no daemon. **Sidecars are unavailable on this rig by two independent mechanisms.** Note also, **narrowly**, that the Task 3 brief — and the plan's verbatim copy of it at `docs/superpowers/plans/2026-07-30-edge-case-campaign-w3.md:197` — says to "read `test/ha/agent.Dockerfile`", and **no such file exists**: `test/ha/` holds `docker-compose.ha.yaml`, `docker-compose.hardfail.yaml`, `ha_test.go`, `hardfailure_test.go`, `kek` and `nginx.conf`, and the agents build from `docker/agent.Dockerfile`. The claim is **scoped to that path**: the plan's own open-questions list at `:143` says only "`agent.Dockerfile`", which `docker/agent.Dockerfile` satisfies, so that line is not wrong and an earlier version of this entry over-claimed by lumping the two together.

## W3-infra — observation: artifact objects are stored under a `.tar.gz` key but contain zstd, and four user-facing doc passages describe the format correctly while the key does not
- **Invariant:** none. Cosmetic / diagnosability.
- **Severity:** minor (observation)
- **Repro:** Not a scenario — noticed while verifying the Task 3 baseline's artifact limb. Evidence: `w3-infra/step5-baseline.txt`, section B7.
- **Observed:** `mc cat garage/unified-cd-logs/artifacts/f0b51370-.../big-blob.tar.gz | head -c 8 | od -An -tx1` gives `28 b5 2f fd 04 68 00 00`. `28 b5 2f fd` is the zstd frame magic; gzip's is `1f 8b`. The key is built as `fmt.Sprintf("artifacts/%s/%s.tar.gz", runID, name)` at `internal/controller/api_artifacts.go:95`, and the same literal suffix is both the filter and the name-reconstruction rule for `handleArtifactList` (`:135-139`) — so the extension is load-bearing for listing, not merely decorative, and cannot be changed without a migration.
- **Expected:** the docs are right and the key is wrong. `docs/cli.md:623` and `:647`, `docs/jobs.md:1346` and `docs/operations.md:45` all describe artifacts as tar+zstd, as does `examples/jobs/artifacts.yaml:27`. Nothing is functionally broken: `unified-cli artifact download` decompresses by content, not by name.
- **Notes:** the cost is entirely to an operator working directly against the bucket — `gunzip` on a pulled object fails with a format error that names neither zstd nor the real cause. Cheapest fix is a doc line next to the artifact key layout saying the `.tar.gz` suffix is historical and the payload is zstd; renaming the key would need a dual-suffix read path in `handleArtifactList` to avoid orphaning every existing object.
